    Book Overview  
	  Title: Ancient Christian Worship
	  Author: Andrew B. McGowan
	  Published by: Baker Academic
	  Year: 2014
    Book Purpose  
	  To explore early Christian worship practices in social, historical, and theological perspective
	  To synthesize the work of many other scholars in the field
    Chapter Structure  
	  7 chapters: Introduction, Meal, Word, Music, Initiation, Prayer, Time
	  Epilogue: The Making of Christian Worship
    Key Topics  
	  Meal: Banquet and Eucharist
	  Word: Reading and Preaching
	  Music: Song and Dance
	  Initiation: Baptism, Anointing, and Foot Washing
	  Prayer: Hours, Ways, and Texts
	  Time: Feasts and Fasts
    Acknowledgments  
	  The author thanks many scholars and colleagues for their advice and assistance
	  The book is a synthesis of the work of many other scholars in the field
    Bibliography and Index  
	  The book includes a bibliography and three indexes: Subjects, Modern Authors, and Scripture and Other Ancient Writings
    Scripture Quotations  
	  The book uses the New Revised Standard Version (NRSV) of the Bible, with some quotations from the King James Version (KJV)
    Translation of Ancient Texts  
	  The author's translations of other ancient texts are used throughout the book
    Hyperlinks  
	  The book's websites are hyperlinked, including Baker Publishing Group, Baker Academic, and Brazos Press
    Dedication  
	  The book is dedicated to Brian and Cate

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Introduction to Christian Worship  
	  Worship is fundamental to the church, but has been contentious throughout history
	  Early Christian literature often critiques and changes worship practices rather than describing them

•   The Challenge of Worship  
	  Modern readers bring a unique difficulty to understanding ancient Christian practice
	  Language has shifted over time, often without awareness of the change

•   The Meaning of Worship  
	  The English word "worship" has changed drastically in meaning over time
	  Worship can mean:
		- Communal prayer and ritual
		- Personal belief or orientation
		- A particular genre of music

•   Historical Context of Worship  
	  Early Christian literature often focuses on encouraging, critiquing, and changing worship practices
	  There is no single, comprehensive source for understanding ancient Christian worship

•   The Complexity of Worship  
	  Worship practices can be problematic and divisive, as well as engaging and inspiring
	  Worship involves both embodied practice and inner faith

•   The Importance of Embodied Practice  
	  Worship is not just about inner faith, but also about embodied practice
	  Christians have always confessed and contested their deepest beliefs and aspirations through worship

•   The Diversity of Early Christian Worship  
	  The history of early Christian worship is diverse and challenging
	  Worship practices varied across different regions and communities

•   The Role of Language in Understanding Worship  
	  Language has shifted over time, often without awareness of the change
	  This can make it difficult for modern readers to understand ancient Christian practice

•   The Need for a Nuanced Understanding of Worship  
	  Worship is a complex and multifaceted concept
	  A nuanced understanding of worship requires considering both its historical and cultural context

•   The Significance of Worship in Christian History  
	  Worship has been a central aspect of Christian practice throughout history
	  Understanding worship is essential for understanding the history of Christianity itself.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Understanding Worship in Ancient and Early Modern Texts  
	  Worship is not just a ritual or a set of beliefs, but a way of life and a relationship with God or others.
	  It involves a set of dispositions and actions that reflect and enact a particular relationship.

    The Meaning of Worship in the King James Version (KJV)  
	  Worship in the KJV refers to a set of physical performances, such as bowing or prostration, that reflect and enact a relationship with God.
	  It also includes participation in sacrificial rituals, but is not limited to them.

    Worship as a Set of Dispositions and Actions  
	  Worship involves a set of bodily acts, such as prostration and gift, that are not just signs pointing to something else, but are worship in themselves.
	  These acts can effect what they signify, and thus reshape the body and the person performing them.

    The Relationship Between Worship and Obedience  
	  Worship is not just a ritual or a set of beliefs, but a way of life that involves obedience or service to God or others.
	  This obedience or service is not limited to specific rituals or gatherings, but is a broader reality that worship creates and represents.

    The Wider Reality of Worship  
	  Worship refers to a wider reality that includes obedience or service, not just specific ritual performances.
	  This wider reality is created and expressed through various forms of worship, including prayer, communal ritual, and personal and physical acts of bodily worship.

    The Concept of Worship in Ancient and Early Modern Texts  
	  The concept of worship in ancient and early modern texts is closer to the concept of worship in the Bible than to modern understandings of worship.
	  Worship in these texts refers to the orientation of all forms of human activity toward a particular allegiance, not just a specific realm of activity like liturgy.

    The Importance of Bodily Acts in Worship  
	  Bodily acts, such as prostration and gift, are central to worship in ancient and early modern texts.
	  These acts are not just signs pointing to something else, but are worship in themselves, and can reshape the body and the person performing them.

    The Relationship Between Worship and Politics/Ethics  
	  Worship is not just about liturgy or music, but has to do with politics and ethics as well.
	  The language of worship in ancient and early modern texts had as much to do with what we would call politics and ethics as with what we call worship.

    The Continuity Between Ancient and Early Modern Texts  
	  Despite a millennium separating ancient and early modern texts, their thought world is closer to the ancient church and the Bible than to modern understandings of worship.
	  This continuity highlights the importance of understanding worship in its historical and cultural context.

    The Significance of Worship in Ancient and Early Modern Texts  
	  Worship in ancient and early modern texts is not just a ritual or a set of beliefs, but a way of life and a relationship with God or others.
	  It involves a set of dispositions and actions that reflect and enact a particular relationship, and has significant implications for our understanding of worship and its relationship to obedience, service, and politics/ethics.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Changes in the concept of worship  
	  The concept of worship has evolved over time
	  Ancient worship was about embodied life and ethics, while modern worship is about inner life and aesthetics

•   Metonymies and worship  
	  Different actions in the ancient world were labeled as worship, but with different meanings
	  These metonymies were related to reverence and service to God, but not in the same way as modern worship

•   Worship in translation  
	  The Bible has been translated into English using outdated equivalences
	  This can lead to misunderstandings about the concept of worship in ancient settings

•   Language and ritual  
	  Language and ritual exist in history and change over time
	  These changes are matters for reflection, not refusal

•   Slippage in language  
	  It's easy to slip into using modern concepts of worship when discussing ancient practices
	  This can lead to a loss of understanding about the original context

•   Modern translations and worship  
	  Modern translations of the Bible can introduce new ideas about worship
	  This can confuse the reader and create a disconnect between ancient and modern practices

•   The concept of worship in ancient Israel  
	  In ancient Israel, worship referred to the whole of service or devotion
	  It was not a distinct concept like it is in modern times

•   The absence of worship in ancient practice  
	  What we now call worship did not quite exist in the ancient world
	  Ancient practices were more focused on communal eating, drinking, music, and other activities

•   Augustine's perspective  
	  Augustine of Hippo recognized the puzzle about the language of worship
	  He suggested that the semantics of Latin were not adequate to the task of describing ancient worship practices

•   The challenge of understanding ancient worship  
	  The concept of worship has changed over time, making it difficult to understand ancient practices
	  It requires reflection and a willingness to acknowledge the differences between ancient and modern concepts of worship

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The concept of worship  
	  Worship (cultus) is due to the divinity
	  No single Latin term can express this concept
	  Greek term "latreia" is used to describe worship in Scripture

•   Language and translation  
	  Language changes over time and may not be adequate to convey the same ideas
	  There may be a commonality of action and purpose across cultures and time

•   Worship in the New Testament  
	  Words translated as "worship" in English versions are not primarily concerned with communal rituals
	  Terms are concerned with reverence, obedience, and bodily performances

•   Types of worship in the NT  
	  Worship can be:
		- Reverence and obedience
		- Bodily performances (e.g. proskynesis, prostration)
		- Dispositions of piety and reverence
		- Specific utterances, actions, or events (e.g. ritual, charity, justice)

•   Worship and the body  
	  Worship is about the body and about service
	  Examples of bodily worship include prostration and other physical performances

•   Worship and communal activities  
	  Worship in the NT is not strongly tied to prayer or Christian gatherings
	  Practices traced to Jesus or apostolic authority may be regarded as worship

•   The concept of worship in the NT  
	  Worship is a reverent orientation of the whole person and community toward God
	  Worship may take place in the domestic and personal realm, as well as in the communal and public

•   The role of communal worship  
	  Communal worship is not the primary focus of worship in the NT
	  Other gatherings and practices (e.g. temple rituals) were more customarily regarded as communal forms of worship

•   The significance of worship  
	  Worship is a fundamental aspect of the Christian faith
	  Worship is about the body, service, and reverence

•   The complexity of worship  
	  Worship is a multifaceted concept that cannot be reduced to a single definition
	  Worship involves language, action, and bodily performance.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Worship in the NT  
	  Worship language in the NT suggests a Christian way of life, but not specific liturgical practices
	  NT texts focus on ethos and character, rather than ritual performance

•   Distinctive Christian Practices  
	  Eating (1 Cor 10:16-17, 11:17-34, Jude 12)
	  Baptism (Acts 2:41, Rom 6:4, 1 Cor 1:13-17, 15:29)
	  Fasting (Matt 6:16-18, Acts 13:23, 14:23)
	  Prayer (Acts 1:14, 6:4, 14:23, Col 4:2)
	  Teaching/proclaiming (1 Cor 12, Col 3:16)

•   Ritual Elements  
	  Prescribed forms of words
	  Bodily performances
	  Use of particular objects and substances

•   Baptism and Eucharist  
	  Directly connected to Jesus' example and teaching in NT texts
	  Each has its own rationale and meaning for Christian identity

•   Christian Identity  
	  Baptism incorporates members into the body (1 Cor 12:13)
	  Eucharist is made by sharing in the one broken bread (1 Cor 10:17)

•   Defining Worship  
	  In this book, worship refers to Christian communal and ritual life
	  This definition reflects NT texts and early Christian practice

•   Historical Context  
	  Early Christians did not have a concept of worship as a distinct form of human activity
	  The term "liturgy" originally referred to public service or philanthropy

•   Justin Martyr's First Apology  
	  Written in the mid-2nd century
	  Describes Christian practice and compares it favorably to Roman sacrifices

•   Emergence of Worship Concept  
	  The idea of worship as communal ritual and prayer began to form in early Christianity
	  Justin Martyr's First Apology shows a developing understanding of Christian practice

•   Modern Understanding  
	  The concept of worship as a distinct form of human activity is recent
	  Early Christian practice was not necessarily understood as worship in the modern sense

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Christian Worship  
	  Emphasized thanksgiving and eucharistia (thanksgiving) in relation to food
	  Linked to the Eucharist, a distinctive sacramental action of Christians

•   Justin's References  
	  Mentioned thanksgiving in relation to food in his work "1 Apol. 13.12"
	  Highlighted the connection between ritual and worldview in early Christianity

•   Sources and Method  
	  Recognized the importance of texts and objects in understanding early Christian worship
	  Acknowledged the limitations of surviving texts and the need for archaeological evidence

•   Texts and Objects  
	  Identified texts as the primary source of information on early Christian practice
	  Noted the importance of material evidence, such as manuscripts and artifacts, in understanding early Christianity

•   Early Christian Objects  
	  Mentioned the discovery of papyri at Oxyrhynchus in Egypt, which had ritual or communal use
	  Noted the significance of these texts as early Christian objects

•   Funerary Evidence  
	  Recognized the importance of catacomb paintings and sarcophagi in understanding early Christian art
	  Acknowledged the limitations of these sources in understanding worship practice

•   Spaces and Rituals  
	  Identified the third century as a turning point in the availability of evidence on early Christian worship
	  Mentioned the discovery of the oldest surviving church at Dura-Europos in Syria

•   Baptismal Rituals  
	  Noted the significance of the Dura-Europos church in understanding baptismal rituals
	  Recognized the limitations of the evidence from this period

•   New Testament Texts  
	  Identified the New Testament texts as the most valuable source of information on early Christian worship
	  Highlighted the importance of these texts as artifacts of Christian worship

•   Archaeological Evidence  
	  Recognized the importance of archaeological evidence in understanding early Christian worship
	  Acknowledged the limitations of the evidence from the period within the scope of the study.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Understanding Early Christian Art and Worship  
	  The study of early Christian art and worship is influenced by the liturgical practices of ancient communities.
	  The New Testament (NT) documents are not straightforward windows into early Christian practice.

•   Genre and Interpretation  
	  The Gospels may be more valuable than assumed as evidence for communal practice after Jesus' emergence.
	  Prescriptive texts in the NT, such as letters, may not indicate actual practice, but rather what the author wanted to see.

•   Modern Liturgical Practices  
	  Modern efforts to create or reform liturgical practices based on NT worship reveal a different problem.
	  Principles such as order, participation, edification, and mutual regard can be discerned, but contexts are not always clear.

•   Baptism and Eucharist  
	  Baptism and Eucharist are grounded in specific injunctions of Jesus, but forms are not specified.
	  These documents assume various practices and critique them, requiring imaginative construction.

•   Reading the NT as a Liturgical Source  
	  Reading the NT as a liturgical source requires imaginative construction of assumed practices.
	  This construction must be accountable to evidence and critically evaluated.

•   Jewish Evidence  
	  The study of early Christian worship has undergone a radical transformation with the recognition of Jesus as a Jew.
	  Jewish evidence, such as the Dead Sea Scrolls, has revealed a diversity and richness of Jewish thought.

•   Diversity of Jewish Thought  
	  Jewish thought was diverse and distinctive, with various texts and traditions.
	  Consideration of the Jewish matrix is essential to understanding early Christian ritual practice.

•   Simplification of Jewish Practice  
	  Many published works simplify or objectify Jewish practice as a neat backdrop for early Christian liturgy.
	  Ancient Judaism was diverse and complex, not a simple backdrop.

•   Importance of Jewish Evidence  
	  Jewish evidence is crucial to understanding early Christian ritual practice.
	  The study of Jewish thought and practice has undergone significant developments in recent years.

•   Challenges in Understanding Early Christian Worship  
	  Understanding early Christian worship requires careful consideration of genre, context, and evidence.
	  Imagination and critical evaluation are necessary to reconstruct early Christian practices.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Jewish Sources  
	  The Dead Sea Scrolls, Rabbinic literature, and other texts provide evidence for Jewish belief and practice
	  These sources are complex and multifaceted, offering no simple or uncontested picture of Jewish tradition

•   Dead Sea Scrolls  
	  Initially thought to shed light on the milieu of Jesus or John the Baptizer
	  Actually provide indirect comparison with John or Jesus, but corroborate and enrich evidence on Jewish prayer and temple rituals

•   Rabbinic Literature  
	  Misused as a source for understanding early Christianity
	  Reflects the ongoing tradition of Judaism, interacting with Christianity after the time of Jesus
	  Includes traditions as old as Jesus or older, but also reflects later realities

•   Rabbinic Literature (continued)  
	  Provides evidence for practices like the Passover seder, baptism of proselytes, and scriptural reading
	  Must be viewed as parallel or interwoven strands of later ritual development, rather than direct influences on Christianity

•   Church Orders  
	  Provide Christian literary evidence for liturgical practices and concerns
	  Include documents like the Didache, Apostolic Tradition, and Apostolic Constitutions

•   Didache  
	  Oldest known church order, transmitted with suggestions of apostolic or divine origin
	  Includes catechetical summary, instructions for baptism and Eucharist, and rules for leadership practice

•   Successor Church Orders  
	  Add more detail and substance to the Didache, including principles and texts for daily prayer and other gatherings
	  Continue to place their ambition for good liturgy in the hands of older and higher authorities

•   Attributions and Authority  
	  Church orders attribute their teachings to apostolic or divine authority
	  Over time, attributions give way to supposed direct quotes from the apostles or teachings ascribed to Jesus himself

•   Liturgical Practices  
	  Church orders provide evidence for liturgical practices and concerns, including baptism, Eucharist, and daily prayer
	  Reflect the ongoing development of Christian worship and ritual

•   Interpretation of Sources  
	  Jewish and Christian sources must be interpreted in their historical and cultural contexts
	  Avoid simplistic or anachronistic views of these sources, recognizing their complexity and multifaceted nature

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Understanding Church Orders  
	  Church orders are intended to be transparent and present themselves as records of apostolic or divine sanction
	  They were not created as distinct compositions, but rather as inheritors and defenders of ancient practices

•   The Apostolic Tradition  
	  A text often referred to as the Apostolic Tradition, associated with Hippolytus, is a problematic and interesting work
	  It can only be reconstructed with moderate certainty from fragments and borrowings
	  Its specific origin and Roman provenance are now considered critically

•   The Significance of the Apostolic Tradition  
	  It is an early and influential work of the church order genre
	  It is a precious witness to the liturgical and pastoral ideals of fourth-century compilers and earlier writers

•   Other Relevant Works  
	  Few other literary works from the early church are as directly focused on worship as the church orders
	  Writings of apologists, such as Justin, include attempts to defend Christian practice
	  Exegetical works, such as those of Origen, provide direct evidence for the ministry of the Word and incidental evidence for other practices

•   Patristic Literature  
	  Letters from significant figures, such as Cyprian and Augustine, sometimes address questions of worship practice directly
	  Patristic literature offers rich resources for understanding early Christian worship practices

•   The Nature of Church Orders  
	  Church orders are living literature, with every version and manuscript having significance
	  They do not fit neatly into modern ideas of original authors or autographs

•   The Importance of Context  
	  Understanding the context in which church orders were written is crucial for interpreting their significance
	  The authors and communities represented in these documents were not conscious inventors of liturgical practice

•   The Role of Scholars  
	  Scholars have established a likely form and content of the Apostolic Tradition
	  However, the specific origin and Roman provenance of the text are now considered critically

•   The Value of Patristic Literature  
	  Patristic literature provides a unique window into early Christian worship practices
	  It offers a rich resource for understanding the development of Christian worship

•   The Complexity of Early Christian Worship  
	  Early Christian worship practices were complex and multifaceted
	  Understanding these practices requires a nuanced and contextualized approach

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Focus of the book  
	  Descriptive rather than prescriptive
	  Reconstructing ancient Christian worship practices
	  Not prescribing what should have been done, but rather what was done

•   Sources of information  
	  Liturgical texts from ancient churches
	  Prayers and hymns from well-known authors like Ephraem and Ambrose
	  Unattributed prayers and hymns that reflect a collective voice of the past

•   Diversity of early Christianity  
	  Processes of community formation involved ritual and theory
	  A strand of orthodoxy emerged through controversies and contests
	  Not all early Christian groups were part of the emerging orthodoxy

•   Challenges in establishing early Christian diversity  
	  Losers rarely write history, making it difficult to establish evidence
	  Important reports and critiques of practices passed on by victorious groups

•   Surviving texts  
	  Apocryphal gospels and acts that reflect diverse positions
	  Gnostic texts from Nag Hammadi, such as the Coptic texts
	  Other literary remains that provide insight into early Christian practices

•   Importance of considering diverse practices  
	  Not just a matter of curiosity, but essential to understanding Christian development
	  Ritual and theology were in formation, with dialogic processes and controversies shaping practices

•   Influence of external communities and traditions  
	  Jewish, Greek, and other communities provided points of contrast, imitation, and competition
	  Internal differences within early Christianity gave shape to emerging practices

•   Emergent practices as choices  
	  Reflecting decisions about what to include or exclude in Christian worship
	  Choices that were influenced by external and internal factors

•   The role of controversy and contest  
	  Controversies and contests shaped the emergence of orthodoxy
	  Internal and external differences contributed to the development of Christian practices

•   The complexity of early Christian worship  
	  A rich and diverse tapestry of practices and traditions
	  A subject that requires careful consideration and nuanced understanding

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Scope of the Study  
	  Begins with the earliest evidence for Christian belief and practice
	  Ends around 400 CE
	  Focuses on the emergence of clarity around the canon of Scripture and creedal definitions of doctrine

•   Characteristics of Christian Inscriptions  
	  Appear and are few in number until the fourth century
	  Skewed towards the funereal
	  Provide remarkable thickness and diversity to the picture of early Christianity

•   Significance of Christian Inscriptions  
	  Witness to the existence of women clergy in periods when this was considered impossible
	  Indicate that traditional beliefs were not always self-evident to those mourning the departed

•   Importance of Liturgical Patterns  
	  Formed during this period and remained fundamental to Christian practice thereafter
	  Include practices that went nowhere in terms of subsequent liturgical history, but were still important to the ancients

•   Relationship between Early and Modern Christianity  
	  The early centuries are fundamental to present Christian identity
	  Creeds and canon continue to have widely acknowledged authority across modern Christian traditions

•   Liturgy and Christian Practice  
	  The forms of worship in many Christian groups today are not consciously based on ancient models
	  Raises questions about how Christian practice should be determined

•   Reformers and Liturgical Practice  
	  Many Reformers understood their liturgical task as restoring ancient models rather than constructing worship anew
	  This is a problematic notion in any case

•   Diversity of Christian Practice  
	  Every Christian liturgical practice is reformed in some sense, not invented from whole cloth
	  Allows for perception of older shapes and texts

•   Importance of Historical Context  
	  Christian practice is shaped by historical context and tradition
	  Understanding this context is essential for understanding Christian practice today

•   Interpretation of Christian Practice  
	  Christian practice should be interpreted in light of its historical context and tradition
	  This requires a nuanced and contextual understanding of Christian practice.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Importance of Ancient Worship Practices  
	  Modern preachers and performers can learn from ancient approaches to words spoken and sung
	  The practice of daily prayer underlies the typical Protestant service of the Word

•   The Story of Christian Worship  
	  Involves both continuity and change
	  This book will explore the history of Christian worship without presenting it as either minimal or radical change

•   The Constantinian Revolution  
	  Did not impose ritual practice on a group previously characterized by spontaneity and freedom of action
	  The history of Christian worship is complex and multifaceted

•   The Sustained Theme of Christian Worship  
	  Seeking, serving, and praising the Maker of all things as revealed in Jesus Christ

•   The Origins of Christian Worship  
	  The first Christian community in Jerusalem was devoted to the apostles' teaching, fellowship, breaking of the bread, and prayers

•   The Breaking of the Bread  
	  A distinctive meal tradition that was not a social event additional to worship
	  The regular form of Christian gathering, which was the forerunner of the Eucharist

•   The Eucharist as Central Act  
	  The breaking of the bread was the central act around which other elements of worship were arranged
	  Reading and preaching, prayer and prophecy were secondary to the eucharistic meal

•   Ancient Eating and the Banquet  
	  The first Christians remembered many meals of Jesus as models for their own eating
	  The eucharistic meal tradition took its place within a wider cultural tradition of shared eating and drinking

•   Common Meals in the Ancient World  
	  Most other groups in the ancient world shared food together in both festive and formal ways
	  The Christians were not unique in having common meals as a central event

•   The Significance of the Eucharistic Meal  
	  Claimed its significance within the wider cultural tradition of shared eating and drinking
	  Remains a central act in Christian worship to this day

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Ancient Banquets  
	  Were formal and purposeful events
	  Included expected formalities and proper conduct
	  Defied modern categories of secular and sacred, familial and public, celebratory and solemn

•   Types of Banquets  
	  Could be large or small, ostentatious or austere, civic or familial
	  Were an integral part of Greco-Roman sacrifices
	  Were celebrated by groups bound by kinship, profession, social, religious, or ethnic ties

•   Philosophical Feasts  
	  Featured in Plato's dialogues
	  Were often set during after-dinner conversations
	  Reflected typical or proper behavior

•   Symposia  
	  Were literary meals and depicted in ancient visual arts
	  Were idealized but reflected typical behavior
	  Have come to serve as models for scholars, conversationalists, and students

•   Formal Dining  
	  Involved gathering in a dining room or tri-clinium
	  Reclining on couches arranged around the room
	  Had expected features, but with much variety in detail

•   Dining Configurations  
	  Could be a rectangular or curved shape (C- or crescent-shaped table)
	  Reflected the relationship of diners to host or social status
	  Had places for guests with different social roles

•   Gender Roles  
	  Were often clearly expressed in classical Greek banquets
	  Women usually sat, rather than reclined, with men
	  Roman customs were more inclusive or egalitarian

•   Jesus' Meals  
	  Reflected interest in issues of status and participation
	  Raised questions about who should sit where and how women could participate

•   Preliminaries for the Banquet  
	  Included washing of hands, offering of an opening prayer or hymn, and libations
	  Set the tone for the meal proper

•   The Meal Proper  
	  Varied in quality and quantity depending on the host or group
	  Was followed by the removal of tables and after-dinner conversations

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Ancient Banquets  
	  Involved wine mixed with water and served in individual cups
	  Shared by diners after prayers or libations to a deity
	  Entertainment and conversation were expected during the meal

•   Communal Suppers  
	  Followed similar patterns to ancient Greek and Roman banquets
	  After-dinner conversation centered on faith-related issues and speech
	  Recognizable to ancient readers as a banquet, but with a more restrained tone

•   Christian Meal  
	  Described by Tertullian as a conventional way to meet and celebrate shared values
	  Involved prayer, singing, and conversation, with a focus on praising God
	  A remarkable and countercultural act, as it did not involve pouring libations for emperors

•   The Last Supper  
	  Depicted Jesus sharing bread and wine with his disciples
	  A meal consisting of bread and wine was not remarkable in ancient times
	  The Last Supper was presented as a celebration of Passover, with bread and wine as the main components

•   Ancient Food  
	  Grains were a staple food across the Greco-Roman world
	  Unleavened bread was common, while leavened bread was more desirable and expensive
	  Other foods, such as fish, vegetables, and meat, were often served at banquets

•   Meat in Ancient Times  
	  Meat was expensive and associated with sacrifice
	  Access to meat was relative to wealth and power
	  Meat was often sold through temples, with devotees eating part of the sacrificial animal

•   Food at Banquets  
	  Bread was at the center of the meal
	  Smaller amounts of highly seasoned foods accompanied the bread
	  The variety, quantity, and quality of these foods increased with the scale and resourcefulness of the host

•   Preserved Foods  
	  Fish was often served in preserved form
	  Other foods, such as salt, vegetables, and legumes, were also preserved

•   Social Significance of Food  
	  Food played a significant role in social gatherings and celebrations
	  The type and quality of food served reflected the host's wealth and status
	  Food was also used to express cultural and religious values

•   Early Christian Identity  
	  The communal supper was a key aspect of early Christian identity
	  It was a way for Christians to express their faith and values in a social setting
	  The meal was a countercultural act, as it did not involve pouring libations for emperors.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Meat and Wine in the Greco-Roman World  
	  Meat was often offered to idols on its way to sale, making it a concern for early Christians
	  Wine was a standard part of a Greek meal, along with bread and a side dish
	  Wine had significant food value, even for poor people like slaves and widows

•   Religious Significance of Meat and Wine  
	  Meat and wine were offered as libations or blessings in Greco-Roman banquets
	  These elements were referred to as a "cuisine of sacrifice" in the Greco-Roman world

•   Early Christian Diet  
	  Early Christians did not live by bread and wine alone, but other foods played a part in their eucharistic eating
	  Bread and wine became the normative pattern of the Christian communal meal

•   Character of Bread and Wine  
	  Bread and wine were the most ordinary foodstuffs, universally accessible and necessary
	  Their importance in the meal ritual was associated with spiritual benefits of the most extraordinary kind

•   Jewish Meals and Traditions  
	  Jewish dining was part of an ancient Mediterranean banqueting tradition
	  Jewish meals, including the seder, had specific sensibilities and rituals

•   The Last Supper and Passover  
	  The Last Supper was likely a formal Passover banquet, as presented in the Synoptic Gospels
	  Christians continued to celebrate a form of Passover in their new feast of Easter

•   The Seder in Jewish Tradition  
	  The seder was a complex meal with significant foods eaten in a specific order
	  The seder involved cups of wine taken and blessed along the way between courses

•   Influence of Greco-Roman Banqueting Tradition  
	  The seder was influenced by the broader Greco-Roman banqueting tradition
	  Jewish diners were required to recline, just like their pagan neighbors

•   Contrast with Greek Model  
	  The seder involved a repetition of a pattern (wine, food, and conversation)
	  This pattern became a series of mini-banquets, or courses, in the seder

•   Importance of the Seder  
	  The seder became an important point of reference for Christians celebrating Easter
	  The seder's complex meal structure and emphasis on wine and conversation were unique in the ancient Mediterranean world.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Significance of the Feast  
	  The Last Supper is a significant event in the Christian tradition
	  Luke's account of the Last Supper includes a unique cup shared by Jesus

•   The Seder Ritual  
	  Some scholars believe the NT narrative is excerpted from the seder ritual
	  However, the seder as depicted in the Mishnah may not have been fully developed at the time of Jesus

•   The Eucharist and the Seder  
	  The Eucharist should not be seen as a Christianized seder in the later and more developed sense
	  The Eucharist emerged in the same world as the seder, but developed alongside it

•   Jewish Meal Traditions  
	  Blessing the cup at the outset is a characteristic Jewish tradition
	  This tradition is attested in the Dead Sea Scrolls and the Mishnah

•   Jewish Meal Practices  
	  Meals in the Dead Sea Scrolls and the Mishnah involve blessings for an opening cup
	  These blessings are a specific prayer form: "Blessed are you Lord God, King of the universe."

•   Other Jewish Meal Traditions  
	  The Qumran community envisaged present and future ideal banquets
	  The Mishnah considers proper blessings and conduct for common meals of associations

•   The Eucharist Emerges  
	  The Eucharist emerges in the same world as these Jewish meal traditions
	  It develops alongside them, rather than merely out of them

•   Christian Communal Eating  
	  Many Suppers: the story of Jesus' Last Supper has been received and recited by Christians
	  The power and poignancy of the scene have created an authoritative and enduring example

•   The Importance of the Last Supper  
	  The Last Supper is not the first supper, either
	  The earliest Christian communities were certainly eating together before the story was written

•   The Development of the Eucharist  
	  The Eucharist emerges in the same world as Jewish meal traditions
	  It develops alongside them, rather than merely out of them

•   Jesus' eating habits  
	  Jesus dined with tax collectors and sinners
	  He received hospitality from unlikely characters
	  He used banquets as a venue for teaching
	  He acted as a miraculous and generous host

	  His eating habits contributed to his popularity and conflicts

	  The Last Supper was not the first significant meal in Jesus' ministry
	  Stories of the resurrection depict Jesus sharing food with disciples

	  The Gospels reflect the concerns of early Christians who wrote and received them
	  The Gospels convey a sense of community meals as a continuation of Jesus' suppers

	  A familiar sequence of taking bread, blessing, breaking, and distributing it for eating is present
	  This pattern is seen in the miraculous feeding stories, the Last Supper, and the resurrection meal at Emmaus

	  Paul takes bread, gives thanks, breaks it, and distributes it for eating

	  They were an important part of early Christian worship and tradition

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Last Supper in ancient Mediterranean societies  
	  Was not a unique ritual practice instituted by Jesus
	  Was an original expression and interpretation of familiar meal customs

•   The Last Supper and the Eucharist  
	  The complex symbolism of eating Jesus' body and drinking his blood has been fundamental to the Eucharist
	  This symbolism has been less prominent in the first decades of the Christian movement

•   The institution narrative of the Christian meal  
	  Found in three Gospels (Matthew, Mark, Luke) and the apostle Paul's 1 Corinthians 11
	  Only two of the four versions have Jesus commanding the continuation of the meal in his memory (Luke 22:19; 1 Corinthians 11:24-25)

•   Comparison of the earliest versions (1 Corinthians and Mark)  
	  Differences in the words used to describe Jesus' actions presiding and praying over bread and cup
	  Similarities in the core message of the Last Supper

•   The two broad traditions (PL and MM)  
	  PL (Paul/Luke) tradition has Jesus giving thanks before breaking and giving the bread
	  MM (Mark/Matthew) tradition has Jesus blessing bread before breaking and giving it

•   Blessing and thanksgiving in ancient Jewish prayer  
	  Blessing (berakah) was a well-developed genre of Jewish prayer, typically addressed to God
	  Thanksgiving (hodayah) prayers were less closely connected with meals

•   The significance of blessing and thanksgiving  
	  Both ways of acknowledging God's grace for the meal
	  Blessing and thanksgiving have subtle but important differences

•   The development of Jewish prayer at meals  
	  The Birkat ha-mazon, an extended prayer based on blessing, became standard at Jewish meals
	  This development occurred not long after the time of Jesus

•   The significance of the Last Supper narrative  
	  The first Christians understood the words of Jesus as a new expression of familiar meal customs
	  The narrative has been subject to interpretation and controversy over the centuries

•   The importance of understanding the original context  
	  The Last Supper narrative should be understood in its original context, rather than with later disputes or supposed original forms or meanings

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Prayer at meals emphasizes thanksgiving  
	  The term "Eucharist" (thanksgiving) is more prevalent than "blessing"
	  This shift in terminology may reflect a change in Christian practice

•   Differences between Last Supper stories  
	  May reflect a point where the terminology has not yet become distinct
	  Memories of Jesus' blessing are merged with the Christians' experience of thanksgiving

•   Identification of bread as Jesus' body  
	  Common to all versions of the Last Supper story

	  In MM, the cup is Jesus' blood of the covenant
	  In PL, the cup is the new covenant in Jesus' blood

	  May refer to the celebratory drinking that accompanied sacrificial feasts
	  Emphasizes the covenantal character of the meal

	  Reflects a communal use of some kind, such as a prayer over the meal

	  The earliest explicit evidence does not include the institution narrative
	  May have been used for a different purpose, such as explanation or interpretation

•   Commensality at Corinth  
	  The first explicit discussion of shared meals in early Christianity

•   Proper conduct of the meal  
	  Paul discusses the meal and its proper conduct in 1 Corinthians 11
	  Emphasizes the importance of building up the community through shared activities

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Inspired Speech in the Church  
	  Paul advises that there be only two or three speakers at a time
	  One person should interpret the message
	  If no one is available to interpret, the speakers should be silent

•   Prophesying in the Church  
	  Paul encourages all to prophesy one by one
	  This allows everyone to learn and be encouraged
	  The goal is for all to benefit from the prophetic messages

•   The Lord's Supper as a Meal  
	  The Lord's Supper is not just about bread and wine, but about sharing a meal together
	  Paul uses the example of Jesus' humility and self-offering to shame the divided community at Corinth

•   Moderation and Mutual Consideration  
	  Paul advises moderation and mutual consideration at table
	  The goal is to discern the body of Christ, both the physical bread and the community that shares it

•   Sacrifice and Food  
	  Food and drink were important religious issues for the Corinthians and their neighbors
	  Paul advises avoiding banquets directly linked to sacrifices, but treating meat itself indifferently

•   The Christian Banquet  
	  The Christian banquet is linked to both gentile and Jewish sacrificial meals
	  Paul argues that eating meat sacrificed to idols is equivalent to partnering with demons

•   Participation in the Christian Meal  
	  Paul interprets the Christian meal as receiving or sharing the body and blood of Christ
	  This is the earliest known theology of the Christian meal

•   Communal vs. Individual Focus  
	  Paul's notion of the body eaten is focused communally, rather than individually
	  The Savior's presence is found in the community that shares the meal

•   The Importance of Discernment  
	  Discerning the body of Christ is the condition for celebrating a meal worthy of the Lord
	  This involves considering the community that shares the meal, not just the physical bread

•   Theological Implications  
	  Paul's theology of the Christian meal has implications for how we understand participation in the meal
	  It emphasizes the importance of community and discernment in celebrating the Lord's Supper

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Paul's understanding of the Eucharist  
	  He views the Eucharist as a sacrificial act, not just a memorial of Jesus' death
	  This understanding is rooted in ancient Mediterranean practices of sacrifice

•   The role of sacrifice in ancient societies  
	  Sacrifices were a way to express dependence on spiritual forces beyond human control
	  They were an integral part of daily life and marked important community events

•   Sacrifice in Jewish and Christian traditions  
	  Jewish sacrifice, like Greek and Roman, served various purposes, including thanksgiving and addressing purity and propriety issues
	  Early Christian writers and readers, Jew or gentile, understood sacrifice as an important aspect of their faith

•   Paul's comparison of the Eucharist to Jewish and Greek sacrifice  
	  He invokes an early anthropological view of sacrifice, comparing the Christian meal to Jewish and Greek cultus
	  The Christian meal is a shared offering, like the Jewish peace offering (zebah selamim)

•   The significance of the Eucharist as a shared meal  
	  It creates a community of faith, with participants as partners in the altar, cup, or table
	  The meal is a concrete, communal, and cultic experience

•   The relationship between the Eucharist and Jesus' death  
	  Paul emphasizes Jesus' death as a central aspect of the Eucharist
	  This emphasis is related to the creation of a new covenant, which binds believers to Jesus

•   The Eucharist as a participation in Jesus' life  
	  The meal can be seen as a participation in Jesus' risen life, not just his death
	  This understanding is not exclusive, and both perspectives are valid

•   The importance of sacrifice in early Christian worship  
	  Sacrifice was a central aspect of early Christian worship, not just a means of dealing with sin or impurity

•   The Didache as a source of early Christian prayer and ritual  
	  The Didache contains the oldest form of prayer and ritual for the Christian banquet
	  It provides insight into the early Christian understanding of the Eucharist

•   The significance of the Eucharist in ancient Mediterranean societies  
	  The Eucharist was a way to express dependence on spiritual forces beyond human control
	  It was an integral part of daily life and marked important community events

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Christian Meals  
	  Were a central practice of the earliest Jerusalem community
	  Involved common meals known as the "breaking of the bread"
	  Were a time for thanksgiving and blessing (Eucharistia)

•   Eucharistia  
	  Originally referred to the prayer offered over bread and/or cup
	  Was quickly extended to mean the food and drink for which thanks were given
	  Became the most widespread term for the Christian meal

•   Agape  
	  Meant "love" or "love-feast"
	  Was used in some communities to refer to the community banquet itself
	  Was not used to refer to the prayers or food and drink

•   Relationship between Eucharistia and Agape  
	  The two terms were partially equivalent
	  Depending on the place and time, one or both might refer to the same Christian meal

•   The Lord's Supper  
	  Was not a name for the early Christian meal
	  Was a phrase used by Paul to contrast self-preoccupied eating with eating with the Lord
	  Was not used as a recurrent label for the event until the 4th century

•   Paul's Use of the Lord's Supper  
	  Was a theological reflection on the meal rather than a label
	  Was not used to refer to the community meal or a part thereof
	  Was only known from Paul's text and terminology

•   The Shape of the Meal  
	  Was a banquet on Greco-Roman lines
	  Involved a solid meal (deipnon) followed by a drinking party (symposion)

•   The First Eucharist  
	  Was a meal that followed the shape of the Last Supper stories
	  Involved a cup taken after the deipnon

•   Influence of Modern Liturgical Experience  
	  Can lead to anachronistic interpretations of the past
	  Reminds us to be cautious when imagining the practices of early Christians

•   The Diversity of Early Christian Meals  
	  Involved different names and practices in different communities
	  Was a time of diversity and experimentation in early Christian worship

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Ancient Mediterranean dining traditions  
	  Varied in shape and form
	  Could be an arena of debate and differentiation among groups

•   Roman dining practices  
	  Called "convivium", meaning "common life"
	  Sometimes more gender inclusive and egalitarian than Greek practices

•   Roman banquet structure  
	  Sometimes retained the Greek model of meal followed by drinking party
	  Other times saw drinking through, as much as or more than after, the meal

•   Jewish eating practices  
	  Influenced by the same traditions as Romans and Greeks
	  May have included other patterns of order, such as blessings of food and drink together

•   Qumran community meals  
	  Involved blessings of food and drink together at the beginning of a meal
	  Men sat before a priest by rank, and their opinions were sought on matters

•   Qumran community meal structure  
	  Priest blessed the first portion of bread or new wine
	  Meal was a time for deliberation and discussion

•   Eucharist in the Didache  
	  Had a shape that contrasted with the Greco-Roman meal
	  Involved blessing of drink and meal elements at the beginning of the meal

•   Eucharist in the Didache structure  
	  Prioritized the cup, but also blessed the broken bread
	  May have de-emphasized the place of wine in the meal

•   Gnostic eucharistic practices  
	  Recorded by Irenaeus in late second-century Gaul
	  Involved a leader named Marcus who made the cup appear purple and red through invocation

•   Gnostic eucharistic meal  
	  May have involved a form of liturgical sleight of hand
	  Does not give a broader sense of the school's eucharistic meal practices

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Christian Worship  
	  Christians in the mid-2nd century celebrated communal meals with Scripture and prayer
	  The bread-cup order was likely used, but not explicitly mentioned

•   Carthaginian Christians  
	  Gathered for a convivium with a meal followed by song, prayer, and discourse (c. 200 CE)
	  This format suggests a strong emphasis on community and shared experience

•   The Didache  
	  Contains the oldest surviving Eucharistic prayers (c. 100 CE)
	  These prayers are brief and focus on giving thanks for the meal and its spiritual significance

•   Eucharistic Prayers in the Didache  
	  Two prayers are provided: one for the cup and one for the broken bread
	  These prayers emphasize the importance of gratitude and the unity of the church

•   Characteristics of the Prayers  
	  Do not mention the death of Jesus or his body and blood
	  Focus on the spiritual significance of the meal and the unity of the church
	  Are similar to Jewish prayers in style and structure

•   Relationship to the Last Supper  
	  The Didache likely knew and accepted the tradition of the Last Supper
	  However, the compilers of the document were more concerned with how to celebrate the meal than why

•   Gospel Meal Narratives  
	  The prayers in the Didache fit within a broader model of giving thanks in a communal setting
	  This model is suggested by various Gospel meal narratives

•   Importance of the Meal  
	  The meal itself was more prominent than the prayers and discourse surrounding it
	  The brevity of the prayers suggests that the focus was on the shared experience of the meal

•   Jewish Influences  
	  The prayers in the Didache are similar to Jewish prayers in style and structure
	  This suggests a strong Jewish influence on early Christian worship practices

•   Theological Emphasis  
	  The prayers in the Didache emphasize the importance of gratitude, unity, and spiritual nourishment
	  This reflects a strong emphasis on the theological significance of the Eucharist in early Christianity

•   Early Christian Eucharistic Prayers  
	  Draw from Jewish and Christian traditions
	  Emphasize Jesus as child of God, descendant of David, and giver of life and knowledge

	  The Didache (mid-2nd century) implies that prayers were extemporized by the president
	  Prayers were often lengthy and included thanksgiving and praise

•   The "We Thank You, Father" Prayer  
	  A Christian eucharistic version of the Jewish blessing or berakah
	  Emphasizes Jesus' role in bringing participants into community as children of God

	  A royal lineage into which Jesus has brought participants
	  Reminiscent of the Johannine imagery of vine and branches

	  Emphasized community and the realization of being God's own people
	  Drinking and eating was a realization and reiteration of this belief

	  Indicates that prayers were often lengthy and extemporized
	  The president would offer prayers and thanksgivings, and the people would respond with "Amen"

	  Preserves an early Christian prayer that gives thanks to Jesus for his mercy and goodness

	  No prayer involving the recitation of the Last Supper story has survived from the first three centuries

•   Liturgical Leadership and Improvisation  
	  Liturgical leadership was defined, but not inflexible
	  The Didache advises that liturgical improvisation is for prophets, not for everyone

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Character of the Eucharistia  
	  A distinct event separate from community banquets
	  Emphasized thanksgiving to God for the meal and gifts of God through Jesus
	  Characterized by an extended prayer over bread and cup

	  Appointed officials (elders, bishops, deacons)

	  Women could have undertaken leadership roles, at least in the first century or so
	  Possibly later, women continued to play a role in leadership and prayer at meals

	  Became a more distinct event over time

	  Emphasized the power of prayer in the eucharistic assembly

•   Evolution of the Eucharistia  
	  From a simple meal to a distinct liturgical event
	  Emphasized thanksgiving and prayer over bread and cup

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Emergence of Church Leadership  
	  Justin's account of a president leading corporate liturgical prayer
	  President composed a variable prayer of thanksgiving and gave instruction
	  President was likely a specific officer, such as a bishop

	  Ignatius needed to defend the bishop's office through his letters
	  Some preferred more flexible modes of authority

•   Bread and Wine in Early Christianity  
	  Bread and wine were everyday staples in ancient Roman culture
	  No particular historical origins or associations required to explain their use
	  Early Christian meals were often simple and ascetic

	  Early eucharistic meals were mostly simple and often ascetic

	  Bread and cup may have acquired symbolic significance that overshadowed side dishes
	  Even around 300 CE, the Apostolic Tradition suggests that bread and cup were central to formal meals

	  Paul's advice to the Corinthians to eat in their own homes before coming to the Lord's table
	  Paul was counseling those used to larger and better meals not to embarrass themselves and others

	  Pliny the Younger investigated Christian meal gatherings after hearing rumors of child sacrifice and cannibalism
	  Pliny reported that the food was everyday and prosaic

•   Development of Eucharistic Practices  
	  Early eucharistic meals were simple and often ascetic
	  Over time, the bread and cup may have acquired symbolic significance that overshadowed side dishes

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Eucharistic Meal Patterns  
	  Wine was often mixed with water, as was the ancient Mediterranean norm
	  Some groups used water only or omitted the cup altogether
	  This was likely an ascetic response to the common use of meat and wine

•   Meat and Wine Avoidance  
	  Many early Christians rejected meat and wine, or vice versa
	  This was not a Christian innovation, but rather a pattern shared with some Jewish groups

•   Fish in Christian Meals  
	  Fish was a desirable feature at some Christian meals, possibly due to its lack of negative connotations
	  This is evident in artistic evidence and scholarly research (e.g. Alistair Stewart-Sykes)

•   Ascetic Eucharists  
	  Early Christian worship often involved ascetic practices, such as avoiding meat and wine
	  This was not unique to Christianity, but rather a broader trend in ancient Mediterranean culture

•   NT Texts and Asceticism  
	  Paul expresses sympathy for those who believe it is good not to eat meat or drink wine (Romans 14:21)
	  First Timothy exhorts its recipient not to drink only water (5:23)

•   Eucharistic Cup Contents  
	  In second-century Christian evidence, water is mentioned as often as wine
	  Groups that avoided wine may have made a point of stating their preference

•   Modern Communion Disputes  
	  Ancient Christian avoiders of wine were concerned about drunkenness, just like moderate wine drinkers
	  There was no ancient movement in favor of using unfermented grape juice

•   Christian Meal Tradition  
	  The Christian meal tradition was a compromise relative to other ancient Mediterranean cultures
	  It focused on bread and mixed wine, rather than meat and wine

•   Theological Interpretation  
	  The eucharistic meal was not just a physical meal, but a theological one
	  Bread and wine could convey similar or stronger meanings than literal meat and wine

•   Holy Food and Holy People  
	  The eucharistic meal was characterized by its simplicity and theological interpretation
	  It was a unique aspect of Christian worship, distinct from other ancient Mediterranean cultures.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Exclusivity of the Eucharist  
	  The Didache considers the Eucharist a sacrifice, exclusive to the faithful
	  The food and drink are set apart from regular use and not shared with outsiders
	  Baptism is required for participation in the meal

•   Ancient Dining Societies  
	  The rules and expectations of ancient dining societies influenced early Christian practices
	  Exclusion of outsiders was common in these societies
	  The Christian community's exclusivity was not unique

•   Exceptions to Exclusivity  
	  Outsiders may have been invited to join the community for specific occasions
	  1 Corinthians 14 describes a situation where outsiders are present, but not participating in the meal

•   Paul's Warning  
	  Paul warns of sickness and death for the unworthy eater (1 Corinthians 11:30)
	  This warning may not have been meant literally, but rather to emphasize the importance of participation

•   Boundary Maintenance  
	  The Pauline Epistles suggest anxiety about Jewish-gentile commensality (sharing meals)
	  Inclusion and boundary maintenance were pressing questions in the early Christian community

•   Gender and Social Status  
	  Women and men, as well as the poor and rich, participated in the Eucharist
	  Holiness was not determined by segregation or social status

•   The Last Supper Traditions  
	  The language of the institution narratives (e.g. eating Jesus' flesh and blood) was rapidly and complexly received
	  The Johannine discourse in John 6 illustrates this development

•   The Johannine Discourse  
	  Jesus speaks of the necessity of eating his flesh and drinking his blood in John 6
	  This discourse is widely understood as a rapid and complex reception of the language of the institution narratives

•   Miraculous Feeding  
	  The Johannine discourse is connected to the miraculous feeding in John 6
	  This narrative illustrates the development of language and themes in the early Christian community

•   Reception of the Institution Narratives  
	  The language of the institution narratives was rapidly and complexly received in the early Christian community
	  This reception is evident in the Johannine discourse and other early Christian texts

•   Early Christian views on the Eucharist  
	  The idea of the Eucharist as flesh and blood was possible from an early point
	  This imagery was used to make points about faith, not just ritual

	  Warned that those who didn't believe in the blood of Christ abstained from the Eucharist
	  Urged unity through the Eucharist, with one flesh and one cup
	  Saw the Eucharist as the medicine of immortality and antidote to death

	  Was seen as having both negative and positive implications for diners
	  Was considered a desirable but potentially dangerous food
	  Was associated with medicine, drugs, and magic

	  Was seen as offering a direct way to participate in God's saving power
	  Was considered more valuable than animal sacrifice, which offered a means of feasting with a god

	  North African Christians carried the broken bread away to break their fast with it

	  The broken bread was taken away from the community meal for later consumption

	  Was seen as a means of participating in God's saving power

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Gospel of Judas and Eucharist  
	  The Gospel of Judas describes a Eucharist where Jesus' disciples are not doing it out of their own will, but because it's a way to praise their god.
	  The text suggests that some early Christians may have rejected the idea of Jesus' presence in the Eucharist.

•   Early Christian Views on Eucharist  
	  Most Christian writings from the 2nd century on emphasize the power and character of the Eucharistic food.
	  There is no hint that the Eucharist was just a symbolic reminder of an idea or doctrine.

•   Ignatius and the Eucharist  
	  Ignatius suggests that some early Christians rejected the idea of Jesus' presence in the Eucharist.
	  These views were not like those of later reformers.

•   The Gospel of Judas and Christ's Presence  
	  The Gospel of Judas suggests that Jesus is not a spiritual savior who came in flesh and blood.
	  This view is different from the idea that Jesus is present in the Eucharist.

•   Justin Martyr's Description of Eucharist  
	  Justin Martyr describes a Eucharist after a baptism, where bread and wine are shared.
	  The president gives thanks and praise to the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.

•   The Role of Deacons in Eucharist  
	  Deacons give bread and wine to those present, and carry away a portion for those absent.

•   Justin's Prayer and the Eucharist  
	  Justin's prayer is spontaneous and lengthier than the prayer in the Didache community.
	  The prayer is part of a baptismal ceremony.

•   Sunday Observance and Eucharist  
	  Justin describes Sunday observance, where the Eucharist is part of a regular assembly.
	  The Eucharist is mentioned alongside the reading of apostolic memoirs and prophetic writings.

•   The Eucharist as a Sacrifice  
	  Justin shows awareness that the Eucharist is a sacrifice that purifies from sin.
	  This view is not explicitly mentioned in the Sunday observance description.

•   The Development of Eucharist  
	  The Eucharist evolved from a banquet to a sacrament.
	  The text highlights the importance of understanding the early Christian views on the Eucharist.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Christian Meals  
	  Were conducted without the accoutrements of a banquet
	  Departed from the Greco-Roman model by having discourse before eating
	  Still considered a full meal

•   Justin's Pattern  
	  Described eating staple foods
	  Participants reclined or sat
	  Portions were taken to absent members

•   Regional Diversity  
	  Different regions had different practices
	  Tertullian in Carthage viewed the Christian banquet in classical terms
	  Later practice in Rome resembled Justin's pattern

•   Changes in Christian Meals  
	  By the 3rd century, Christians were meeting in larger spaces
	  Reclining to eat became harder or impossible
	  Changes in the Christian meal were allowed or demanded

•   Shift in Carthage  
	  Between 200-250 AD, the eucharistic food was received primarily at morning gatherings
	  Evening banquets were still the primary assembly for Tertullian

•   Agape and Convivium Dominicum  
	  The evening gathering was formally an agape (love-feast)
	  Also referred to as a convivium dominicum (Lords dinner party) or cena Dei (Gods banquet)

•   Eucharist Distribution  
	  The Eucharist was distributed at other assemblies, including special fast days
	  Evidence suggests it was often carried away to be eaten later

•   Morning Assemblies  
	  Desire to receive the blessed elements may have encouraged morning assemblies
	  May have been held at the house of the bishop or wealthy patrons

•   Decline of Evening Banquets  
	  By Cyprian's time, the significance of evening banquets had declined
	  May have been due to the larger size of the Christian community

•   Rumors and Tensions  
	  Evening banquets gave rise to rumors about cannibalism and incest
	  Tensions and rivalries existed within the Christian community

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Christian Gatherings  
	  Gatherings were often small and informal
	  The Eucharist was the primary meeting point
	  Food was not a central part of these gatherings

•   Eucharist Distribution  
	  Small amounts of food were distributed
	  Food was carried in a bag or box around the believer's neck
	  This was a reverent and sacramental event

•   Banquets in Early Christianity  
	  Banquets continued to be held in some Christian communities
	  These gatherings were often linked to patronage and family networks
	  They were not necessarily part of the wider Christian community

•   Changes in Banquet Culture  
	  By the 4th century, banquets were no longer a central part of Christian gatherings
	  They were often held in specific networks of patronage and family
	  Clerical and monastic influences complicated these gatherings

•   Funerary Meals  
	  Funerary meals were held in cemetery churches and martyr shrines
	  These meals were often named as "refrigerium" (refreshment)
	  They were sometimes held in semicircular picnic tables

•   Domestic Devotional Practices  
	  Devotional practices were often centered in the home
	  Members of the church might invite leaders and those in need to dine at home
	  This was a reverent and separate occasion from the Eucharist

•   Apostolic Tradition  
	  The Apostolic Tradition contains instructions for a domestic meal
	  The meal is held in the evening with the bishop present
	  The bishop gives thanks and the congregation responds

•   Meal Instructions  
	  The bishop greets the congregation with "The Lord be with you"
	  The congregation responds with "And with your spirit"
	  The bishop then says "Let us give thanks to the Lord"

•   Thanksgiving Prayer  
	  The bishop gives a thanksgiving prayer over the light of the evening lamp
	  This is not a prayer over food itself
	  It is a prayer of thanksgiving and praise

•   Post-Meal Practices  
	  After the meal, the congregation rises and prays
	  This is a time for reflection and devotion
	  It is a separate occasion from the Eucharist and the meal

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Morning Eucharist  
	  The morning Eucharist involved singing psalms and reciting prayers
	  A deacon would hold a mixed cup of oblation and say a psalm from a specific collection
	  The bishop would then say a psalm appropriate to the cup, including Hallelujah

•   The Role of the Bishop  
	  The bishop would give thanks for the cup and distribute fragments to the faithful
	  The bishop would also give a piece of bread to the faithful before they took their own bread

•   The Relationship between Meal and Sacrament  
	  The morning Eucharist had some eucharistic resonances, but was also distinct from the sacrament
	  The compilers of the Apostolic Tradition were concerned to establish the difference between meal and sacrament

•   The Development of Eucharistic Celebrations  
	  By 200 AD, some places had eucharistic celebrations with characteristics that would last for centuries
	  By 300 AD, these celebrations had many of the characteristics they would have for centuries to come

•   The Shape of Morning Sacramental Assemblies  
	  Readings from Scripture, teaching, and prayer preceded the reception of the sacred food
	  This was in contrast to the evening banquet, which involved a meal followed by or interspersed with various forms of speech, music, or conversation

•   The Use of Sacred Space  
	  The first Christian meals took place in houses, including free-standing homes and apartment-like insulae
	  These spaces were both functionally necessary and symbolically appropriate to the event

•   The Connection between Initiation and Meal  
	  The use of central pools in larger freestanding houses allowed for the ritualization of the connection between initiation and meal
	  More public and less domestic venues could also have been used for baptism at an early point

•   The Development of Different Liturgical Forms  
	  The use of different spaces parallels the development of different liturgical forms
	  The emerging Christian movement was less a family or household and more like other associations or clubs

•   The Movement from Domestic to Public Spaces  
	  The Christian movement would have aspired to move from a member's home to their own dedicated venue when necessary or possible
	  This movement reflects the development of different liturgical forms and the use of different spaces

•   The Importance of Sacred Space  
	  Sacred space was both functionally necessary and symbolically appropriate to the event
	  The use of different spaces reflects the development of different liturgical forms and the movement of the Christian movement from domestic to public spaces.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Christian Meeting Spaces  
	  Varied in type and location
	  May have included bathhouses, triclinia, and private homes
	  Practicality was a key factor in choosing meeting spaces

•   The Dura-Europos Church  
	  A house was converted into a church in the Syrian border town of Dura-Europos
	  The largest room was used for prayer gatherings and the Eucharist
	  The space was unadorned and had a small platform for a teacher's dais

•   Architectural Adaptation  
	  The Dura-Europos church was an example of architectural adaptation for Christian communal use
	  The nearby synagogue at Dura had also been converted from a house
	  Both communities likely chose the most expedient option to acquire functional spaces

•   Synagogue Architecture  
	  Synagogue architecture was not well developed at the time
	  Synagogues and churches would later influence each other architecturally and liturgically

•   Continued Importance of Houses  
	  Houses remained important places for Christian gathering
	  They were closely related to patterns of relationship connected with kinship and patronage

•   Other Gathering Spaces  
	  Cemeteries and other open spaces were also venues for important meetings and performances
	  The remembrance of the dead was a particularly important focus

•   The Eucharist as Sacrifice  
	  The Eucharist was depicted as a peaceful act, morally and religiously superior to pagan rituals
	  It was also increasingly seen as a sacrifice, a sharing in flesh and blood

•   Christian Identity and Ritual Expression  
	  The refocusing of communal activity in church buildings did not make them sole centers for ritual expression of faith
	  Houses and other gathering spaces continued to be important for Christian ritual expression

•   Gatherings Led by Bishops  
	  Gatherings led by bishops were not the only ones of significance
	  Other gatherings, such as those in cemeteries and other open spaces, were also important

•   Evolution of Christian Identity  
	  The evolution of Christian identity from household to church is not implied by the Dura-Europos church
	  The nearby synagogue at Dura had also been converted from a house, and both communities likely chose the most expedient option.

•   The Eucharist and its ministers  
	  Were seen as direct successors of the Jerusalem temple's cultus and priesthood
	  Were considered superior to demonic gentile sacrifices
	  Were a clear competitor to Roman sacrifices, especially during times of persecution

	  Became fundamental in Christian tradition
	  Extended beyond the Eucharist to ways of thinking about order and power
	  Made Christian ritual a more effective point of resistance or competition to Roman cultus

	  Led to the reuse of spaces and symbols associated with Roman cultus for Christian worship
	  Made Christian liturgy the most sacred ritual of Roman society

	  Was a distinctive communal and ritual performance in ancient Christianity
	  Was associated with familial and sexual relationships, as well as social interactions

	  Was a common practice in ancient Christianity
	  Was mentioned in four of Paul's letters (Rom. 16:16, 1 Cor. 16:20, 2 Cor. 13:12, 1 Thess. 5:26)
	  Was not scandalous, but rather a normal part of social interactions

	  Was associated with familial and sexual relationships in Greco-Roman culture
	  Was used to express relationships publicly as well as privately
	  Was not limited to Christian communities, but was also practiced in other social interactions

•   The importance of bodily dispositions and actions  
	  Was an important means of exploring belief and creating social bonds
	  Included acts such as eating, drinking, singing, and kissing
	  Was a distinctive aspect of ancient Christian worship

	  Became more effective as a point of resistance or competition to Roman cultus
	  Became more suitable as a tool for an imperial religion
	  Ultimately led to the reuse of spaces and symbols associated with Roman cultus for Christian worship

•   The Christian Kiss  
	  A reworking of an existing practice or convention
	  Brought believers into a relationship like that of family members
	  No specific precedent or model explains its prominence

•   Justin Martyr's Account  
	  Indicates the kiss had a specific place and function in Christian assemblies by the mid-second century
	  Describes the kiss as a way to salute one another after prayers

•   Tertullian's Views  
	  Considers the kiss a seal of prayer
	  Expresses concern that those who fast may abstain from the kiss
	  Sees the kiss as a way to bestow peace with the brethren

	  Notes that a custom has been handed down for brothers to kiss each other after prayers
	  Connects the kiss specifically with the mysteries (Eucharist)
	  Calls the kiss an image of Christ's encounter with the pure soul

•   Kissing the Martyrs  
	  Devotional kisses were offered to the persecuted in their prisons
	  Depicted in the Acts of Paul and Thecla and the Martyrdom of Perpetua and Felicitas

•   Ritual and Communal Modes  
	  Kisses feature in communal worship acts and devotional practices
	  Connects familial, ritual, and communal modes of kissing

•   Dangers Attached to Kissing  
	  Athenagoras warns that the kiss should be given with the greatest care
	  Cyprian implies that fervent kisses offered to martyrs may have led to impropriety

•   Kissing in Worship  
	  The kiss was a ritual within the ritual
	  Its own logic led to its use after prayer, especially at the Eucharist

•   Kissing as an Act of Worship  
	  Depicted in the Martyrdom of Perpetua and Felicitas as an act of worship before death

•   Kissing in Devotional Practices  
	  Offered to martyrs in prison as a way to show devotion and support

	  Part of the initiation ritual, including baptism

	  The kiss was a micro-liturgy with a specific logic
	  Examples of the kiss in the Apostolic Tradition:
		- Given to a new bishop after ordination
		- Exchanged after catechetical assembly
		- Exchanged between the bishop and newly baptized
		- Exchanged among those baptized after prayers

	  Catechumens (those undergoing instruction) did not kiss
	  The kiss was not exchanged between men and women
	  This rule reflects a tendency to separate men and women in Christian assemblies

	  Marked the baptized as a distinct group
	  Became less significant as Christianity became normative in the Roman population

	  Marked the baptized as a distinct group
	  Became less significant as Christianity became normative in the Roman population

•   The kiss in different contexts  
	  Was used in different contexts, including baptism, reconciliation, and the Eucharist
	  Its significance and placement varied across different times and places.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The kiss of peace in early Christianity  
	  Was a significant ritual action in early Christian worship
	  Was often seen as a seal to the prayer that preceded it
	  Was a distinctive group action, not just a personal gesture

	  Meant that the kiss was increasingly understood as prelude to the eucharistic prayer

	  Have generally not made an actual kiss the peace in contemporary Western rites

	  Was lost when the distinctive group that exchanged the kiss was diminished

	  Meant that the kiss became a vestigial action shared among clergy

	  Was celebrated in purpose-built structures, particularly the basilica
	  Was a significant change from earlier practices, which took place in homes or small halls

•   The significance of the conversion of Constantine  
	  Can be exaggerated, but was a significant event in the history of Christianity
	  Was a key factor in the growth of Christianity in the cities of the Roman Empire

•   Changes in Eucharistic Practice  
	  Began before Constantine's conversion
	  Characterized by changes in space and building use
	  Developed from a pragmatic quest for space

•   Purpose-Built Christian Halls  
	  Existed in the latter half of the 3rd century
	  Were a logical development of the need for space

•   Christianization of Temples  
	  Sometimes occurred, but not as a favored model for Christian sacred space
	  Logistical reasons: temples were not gathering places for large assemblies

•   Character of Eucharistic Events  
	  Changed from intimate groups to crowds gathering for a sacrament
	  Transformed from a meal to a liturgy

•   Liturgical Elements  
	  Recognizable in modern churches of sacramental bent
	  Others may surprise, as they varied from place to place

	  Members gathered in the church building before the celebration
	  Sunday morning was the norm, but the Eucharist was celebrated daily in many places

•   Food and Drink  
	  Participants often brought food and drink from which the eucharistic elements were chosen
	  Remainder made available for needy members of the community

•   Congregational Arrangements  
	  Congregations generally did not use chairs
	  Most of the liturgy was spent standing
