	  The Eucharist began with the entrance of the clergy and the greeting by the bishop
	  Readings from Scripture followed, including OT and NT writings
	  The bishop or presbyter gave a sermon, and the catechumens were dismissed before the doors were closed

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Eucharistic Liturgy  
	  Took place in the apse or at the altar in the body of the church
	  Involved a deacon leading prayers for the community and its needs
    The Eucharistic Prayer  
	  Was an extended but set prayer that included the institution narrative
	  Focused on expressing thanks and praise to God
	  Involved ritual gestures, such as the sign of the cross over the bread and wine
    The Breaking of Bread  
	  The bread was broken and the Lord's Prayer was said together
	  The kiss was offered before the eucharistic food and drink were distributed
    Communion  
	  Generally, all members of the church present, except penitents, received Communion
	  There was local variety of custom, and those outside urban areas might receive infrequently
    Liturgy and Music  
	  The Nicene Creed and the hymn Agnus Dei were not yet regular features
	  The Sanctus was not yet present in every liturgy
	  Special liturgical dress was not yet evident
	  Music was an adjunct to the ritual meal rather than a central activity
    Frequency of the Eucharist  
	  The Eucharist was celebrated often daily in many places
	  It was also celebrated on occasions where it seemed appropriate for pastoral need
    Funeral Rites  
	  Funeral rites drew on the fundamental elements of sacrament, prayer, and psalmody
	  They did not yet have a specific form as a rite
    The Eucharist as a Field of Practice  
	  The Eucharist is characterized by diversity and not just a single idea
	  It presents rich and varied themes of memory, presence, celebration, and sacrifice
    The Theme of Thanksgiving  
	  Thanksgiving, or eucharistia, is a strong theme in the Eucharist
	  It is not the only theme, and the Eucharist presents a complex and multifaceted practice
    Conclusion  
	  The Eucharist is a complex and multifaceted practice that cannot be reduced to a single idea
	  It has a rich and varied history, and its themes and practices continue to evolve.

•   The Early Christian Eucharist

	  This change likely happened later than previously presumed
	  Theologically, ideas of real presence and sacrificial offering became clearer and stronger over time

	  The change from banquet to sacrament is also significant
	  Medieval Christian liturgy in the West lost focus on literal participation in the sacred meal

	  Were highly ritualized occasions, centered on sacred eating
	  Were accompanied by prayer and formal discourse
	  Were not a fall from commensality to ritual, but rather a development of existing practices

•   The Eucharist in the Fourth Century  
	  A diverse group of believers gathered around a table to share simple food
	  Prayed and gave thanks to God for the life and work of Jesus
	  Remembered Jesus' acts of eating and affirmed his presence in and at their meal

	  Was the center of Christian life across centuries

	  Medieval Christian liturgy in the West lost focus on literal participation in the sacred meal
	  Reformed churches inherited an assumption that eucharistic reception was not the distinctive and regular act of Christian communal life

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Significance of Words in Christianity  
	  Words have been more than just vehicles for transmission of ideas and culture in Christianity
	  Christianity attributes creation to God's speech, like Judaism and Islam
	  Christianity also suggests that the divine Word is intrinsic to God's being and is the subject of the incarnation

•   The Role of Scripture in Christianity  
	  Scripture is a unique source of revelation in Christianity
	  The verbal instrument of creation is also the subject of the incarnation
	  Words concerning the divine Word are intrinsic to Christian communal ritual and practice

•   Reading and Preaching in Early Christianity  
	  Reading and preaching are central elements of early Christian worship
	  Virtually all ancient Christian texts are concerned with scriptural interpretation or reflect homiletic practice
	  This chapter focuses on the practices of reading and inspired utterance in early Christian communities

•   The Synagogue and Its Purpose  
	  The synagogue has often been regarded as the starting point for Christian reading and preaching
	  The Gospels and Acts of the Apostles refer to Jesus and Paul participating in synagogue assemblies
	  Scholars assume that Christian worship practice was borrowed from or influenced by the synagogue, but recent scholarship urges caution

•   The Nature of the Synagogue  
	  The synagogue literally means a gathering or assembly
	  In the first few centuries CE, it did not always refer to a building, but could mean a group accustomed to gather in one place
	  Not every Jewish community in Judea or elsewhere had a synagogue, in either the sense of assembly or building

•   Synagogues in Early Rabbinic Literature  
	  Synagogues rarely feature in early rabbinic literature
	  The emerging strength of the rabbinic movement was focused elsewhere at earlier times
	  The liturgical evidence of the Mishnah and the Talmuds reflects a later development

•   The Importance of Words in Christian Worship  
	  Words were spoken in many settings in early Christian communities
	  Words were an important element of Christian meal gatherings, shared and consumed as surely as solid food
	  The Word was a central element of Christian communal ritual and practice

•   The Relationship Between Christianity and the Synagogue  
	  Scholars assume that Christian worship practice was borrowed from or influenced by the synagogue
	  Recent scholarship urges caution about these connections and what there was to borrow
	  What we know about first-century synagogues may actually be less than what we know about first-century churches

•   The Focus of This Chapter  
	  This chapter focuses on the actual practices of reading and inspired utterance in early Christian communities
	  It does not seek to construct a history of ancient hermeneutics or explore the content of the earliest Christian kerygma

•   The Significance of Early Christian Literature  
	  Virtually all ancient Christian texts are concerned with scriptural interpretation or reflect homiletic practice
	  Early Christian literature reflects the importance of words in Christian worship and practice

•   Early Synagogues  
	  Not primarily liturgical centers

	  Reading of the Law

•   Theodotus Synagogue  
	  Built near the Temple Mount in Jerusalem
	  Funded by Theodotus, a priest and synagogue leader
	  Included a hostel, rooms, and water fittings for pilgrims

•   Synagogue Architecture  
	  Similar to ancient temples and guild halls
	  Reflects the activities of social groups and communities

•   Reading and Study  
	  Focus of synagogue life
	  Encouraged the development of early Christian knowledge and practice around Scripture

•   The Torah and Synagogue  
	  Central to synagogue reading practice
	  Regarded as a sacred text and object before synagogues existed

•   Emergence of Christian Practices  
	  Reading and interpretation of Scripture influenced early Christianity
	  Preceded and accompanied the emergence of Christian practices of reading and preaching

•   Synagogue Life  
	  Not just a place of worship, but also a community center and pilgrim hostel
	  Included diverse facilities and spaces for various activities

•   Regional Variations  
	  Different regional use and functions of synagogues
	  Terms like "proseuche" and "synagogue" reflect different regional use

•   Development of Synagogues  
	  Gradually became more prominent and influential after the destruction of the Jerusalem temple in 70 CE
	  Replicated or imitated the temple in their functions and architecture

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Public Reading of the Torah  
	  Took place in the temple and synagogue
	  Involved the king or high priest reading from the Torah
	  Occurred on special occasions, such as the Day of Atonement and Feast of Sukkot
    Centrality of Reading in Synagogues  
	  Reading the Torah was a distinctive Jewish communal activity
	  Was the center of Sabbath observance
	  Described by Josephus and Philo as a key aspect of Jewish worship
    Nature of Reading in Synagogues  
	  Involved long sessions of study and conversation
	  Was not just a matter of reading, but also interpretation and discussion
	  Was a communal activity, not individual reflection
    Oral Reading in the Ancient World  
	  Silent reading was not common in the ancient world
	  Oral recitation was a typical practice
	  Books were expensive and literacy levels were not high
    Pattern of Reading in Synagogues  
	  Involved reading a segment or portion of the Torah each week on the Sabbath
	  No common lectionary existed in the synagogue until later centuries
	  Theories suggesting a formalized lectionary pattern are fanciful
    Reading in the Dead Sea Scrolls Community  
	  Involved reading aloud from the Torah
	  Had practical concerns, such as ensuring the reader's voice was clear
	  Was a communal activity, not individual reflection
    Reading as a Communal Activity  
	  Involved shared conversation and discussion
	  Was not just a matter of reading, but also interpretation and understanding
	  Was a key aspect of Jewish worship and community
    Role of Priests and Elders  
	  Played a key role in reading and interpreting the Torah
	  Were responsible for leading the community in reading and discussion
	  Were respected for their knowledge and understanding of the Torah
    Importance of Reading in Jewish Worship  
	  Was a central aspect of Jewish worship and community
	  Involved the reading and interpretation of the Torah
	  Was a key way of connecting with God and the community
    Evolution of Reading in Synagogues  
	  Involved a gradual development of reading practices
	  Was influenced by cultural and historical factors
	  Continued to evolve over time, with new practices and traditions emerging.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Jesus' Reading in the Synagogue  
	  Jesus read from the scroll of the prophet Isaiah in the synagogue at Nazareth (Luke 4:16-22)
	  He unrolled the scroll, found the place where it was written, and began to say that the scripture had been fulfilled in their hearing

•   Paul's Reading in the Synagogue  
	  Paul read from the law and the prophets in the synagogue at Antioch (Acts 13:14-16)
	  He stood up and began to speak, addressing the Israelites and others who feared God

•   Synagogue Worship  
	  Public readings of the law and prophets were undertaken in synagogues
	  Interpretation took place with a certain spontaneity and openness to participation

•   Characteristics of Synagogue Preaching  
	  Not a formalized, univocal activity led by one uninterrupted orator
	  A conversation in which those regarded as learned and articulate were preeminent

•   Jewish Homiletics  
	  Did not need to be imagined as a one-sided rhetorical performance
	  May have involved a more interactive and participatory approach

•   Rabbinic Exegesis  
	  Probably derives from oral teaching offered in rabbinic schools rather than synagogues
	  Does not shed much light on how Christians began to read and understand the sacred text communally

•   Targums  
	  Interpretive translations of the Hebrew text into Aramaic
	  May contain traces of early homiletic traditions, but do not provide much insight into communal Bible study

•   Jesus and Paul's Synagogal Scripture Events  
	  Seem more like communal Bible study with some authoritative input than liturgy
	  May have had a ceremonial quality, but this should not be exaggerated

•   Later Evidence for Synagogue Worship  
	  Unlike the early evidence, later evidence for synagogue worship is more formalized and liturgical

•   Implications for Understanding Early Christian Worship  
	  Suggest that early Christian worship may have been more informal and interactive than previously thought
	  May have involved a more communal and participatory approach to reading and interpreting scripture

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Synagogues after the destruction of the temple  
	  Became more likely to be seen as sacred spaces
	  Torah scrolls in shrines became a source of sacrality
	  The Pentateuch itself became a source of sacrality

•   Church and Synagogue  
	  Early Christians were familiar with synagogal reading practices
	  However, this does not imply direct influence on early Christian assemblies
	  Churches were not synagogues and had different liturgical forms

•   Christian gatherings  
	  Emerged alongside temple and synagogue activities
	  Were qualitatively different and complementary events
	  May have been understood as types of association or guild

•   Relationship between Church and Synagogue  
	  Not a direct or organic derivation
	  Not a self-conscious modeling
	  A more subtle affinity based on common interest or purpose

•   Early Christian preaching  
	  May have been intended for maintenance rather than mission
	  Discourse phrased for mission may have been intended for the already convinced
	  The good news or its proclamation was fundamental to the church's existence

•   The role of synagogue in early Christian practice  
	  The sacred writings of Judaism were known and interpreted in early Christian communities
	  The earliest Christians were themselves Jews who had participated in synagogues

•   The influence of synagogue on early Christian practice  
	  Must be examined rather than assumed
	  The connections between synagogue and early Christian practice are complex and multifaceted

•   The evolution of preaching in the early church  
	  Words like preaching and sermons have shifted in meaning over time
	  Ancient preaching can refer to evangelistic activity or liturgical preaching

•   The confusion between ancient and modern homiletics  
	  Can lead to anachronistic assumptions about early Christian preaching
	  Early Christian preaching may have been intended for a different audience or purpose

•   The importance of context in understanding early Christian practice  
	  Must be examined in its historical and cultural context
	  Assumptions about early Christian practice can be misleading or inaccurate

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Preaching in Early Christianity  
	  Preaching was not just a matter of street preaching or altar calls
	  It was often directed at those already connected to the community
	  The basis of conviction and initiation was complex and relational
    The History of the Sermon  
	  Historians have often focused on oral performance and mission
	  The present discussion focuses on the ways speech was used in Christian communal settings
    Preaching and the New Testament  
	  The central Christian gathering was the communal eucharistic meal
	  The Lord's banquet was a venue for various kinds of discourse, including reading and preaching
    The Origins of Christian Preaching  
	  Much of the NT writings were certainly spoken before being written
	  The NT sprang from early Christian preaching, rather than the other way around
    The Challenge of Identifying Early Christian Preaching  
	  Nearly every part of the NT has been claimed as liturgical at one time or another
	  It's difficult to be sure exactly which texts emerged in gatherings, and what other discourses were spoken and heard
    Sorting Early Christian Discourse  
	  Scholars have tended to sort by content (e.g., separating kerygma from didache)
	  Others have sorted by apparent rhetorical form
    The Limitations of Panliturgism  
	  Panliturgism is the idea that many NT texts can be mapped onto ritual practices
	  This approach is generally unconvincing and less favored today
    The Importance of Early Christian Literature  
	  Early Christian literature, including the NT, contains indications of the forms and content of discourse in early Christian assemblies
	  However, the specifics of early Christian preaching are difficult to determine
    The Role of Rhetoric in Early Christian Preaching  
	  Early Christian preaching may have involved formal elements of rhetoric
	  However, the extent to which this was the case is unclear
    The Need for a More Nuanced Understanding  
	  A more nuanced understanding of early Christian preaching is needed
	  This requires a careful consideration of the complexities of early Christian discourse and practice.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Ancient Christian communal discourse  
	  Was not just a single person speaking, but a collective conversation
	  Involved various contributions, including hymns, lessons, revelations, tongues, and interpretations

•   Paul's account in 1 Corinthians 14  
	  Describes a ministry of the Word in a Christian assembly
	  Emphasizes the importance of building up the community through speech
	  Suggests a hierarchy of offices, including apostle, prophet, and teacher

•   Types of speech in ancient Christian communities  
	  Included charismatic utterance, prophecy, and other forms of speech
	  May have merged into other types of speech, such as affirmation or praise

•   The role of prophecy in ancient Christian communities  
	  Was clearly important, even if its forms varied
	  May have been a collective set of inspired discourses, rather than a single sermon

•   Paul's ideal for communal discourse  
	  Suggests a whole procession of prophets, rather than a single teacher or oracle
	  Emphasizes the importance of collective speech and dialogue

•   The example of Paul's ministry in Acts 20:7  
	  Describes a eucharistic meal and a long discourse by Paul
	  May be a dialogue rather than a single long speech, compatible with the Corinthian conversation

•   The importance of understanding ancient Christian communal discourse  
	  Can shed light on the actual functioning of speech in ancient Christian communities
	  Is less useful when trying to conform to an ideal type or method

•   The limitations of using ideal types or methods  
	  May not accurately reflect the actual functioning of speech in ancient Christian communities
	  Can lead to a narrow or inaccurate understanding of ancient Christian worship

•   The value of explicit references in early Christian texts  
	  Provide a firmer ground for understanding ancient Christian communal discourse
	  Can help to discern how speech functioned in ancient Christian assemblies

•   The need to consider multiple examples and perspectives  
	  Can provide a more nuanced and accurate understanding of ancient Christian communal discourse
	  Can help to avoid relying on a single ideal type or method.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The apostles' prominence in the conversation  
	  May reflect the historical experience of the Pauline mission
	  Involved didactic conversations with converts and believers privately
	  Involved apologetic or evangelistic conversations more publicly

•   The nature of early Christian conversations  
	  May have been more like a diatribe or scholastic philosophical discussion
	  Involved oral contributions of different length, content, and form
	  May have been more characteristic of the earliest Christian assemblies than any particular pattern of oratory

•   The crucible of collective, dialogical inspiration  
	  Occurred at communal meals
	  Involved the sharing of various discourses, including brief and extended contributions
	  Involved both scholastic and charismatic elements

	  May have survived in more and less obvious forms
	  Were likely more substantial and formal addresses or readings

	  Was known for his eloquence
	  Must have arisen from rhetorical performance in the assemblies of communities he visited

	  Involves a set of examples or data
	  Followed by a conclusion drawn from them
	  Ends with some application or exhortation

	  The term "word of exhortation" also occurs in 1 and 2 Maccabees

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Importance of Exhortation  
	  Exhortation is emphasized by Stewart-Sykes as a key aspect of early Christian communities
	  It is also highlighted by E. A. Judge as a scholastic aspect of these communities

•   The Scholastic Aspect of Early Christian Communities  
	  Judge's work, "The Early Christians as a Scholastic Community", explores this aspect
	  Part II of his work, published in 1961, continues to develop this idea

•   The Form of the Sermon  
	  The form of the sermon in Hellenistic Judaism and early Christianity is a topic of study
	  Lawrence Wills' work, "The Form of the Sermon in Hellenistic Judaism and Early Christianity", is a key contribution to this field

•   Ancient Christian Worship  
	  Ancient Christian worship is characterized by exhortation, rather than a specific literary or rhetorical form
	  This is in contrast to the Greco-Roman tradition, which had a more formalized approach to worship

•   The Letter to the Hebrews  
	  The Letter to the Hebrews is a strong contender for a homiletic text in the NT writings
	  It features elements of Greco-Roman rhetoric, particularly of the epideictic type

•   Hebrews as a Desk Homily  
	  Hebrews may have begun life as a desk homily, which was later turned into a letter
	  This suggests that it was composed for liturgical use, rather than as a standalone text

•   Opportunities for Composition and Performance  
	  The existence of Hebrews demonstrates that there were individuals capable of sustained and sophisticated oratory
	  This implies that there were real opportunities for composition and communal performance in early Christian communities

•   2 Clement as an Ancient Sermon  
	  2 Clement is an ancient text that claims to be the oldest actual sermon or address surviving from ancient Christianity
	  It is an exhortation to a congregation to act in justice and charity, fitting their divine calling

•   Explicit Indications of Communal Performance  
	  2 Clement includes explicit indications of its own communal performance, such as references to reading Scripture in the assembly
	  The author possibly refers to reading Scripture in the assembly, saying that it is being read to the congregation

•   The Possible Location of 2 Clement  
	  The possible location of 2 Clement is a topic of debate among scholars
	  Some scholars argue that it was composed in the early second century, while others propose a later date.

•   The Preacher's Intention  
	  Linked to repetitive moral urgings and revealed truth
	  Cites from Psalms, Prophets, and Christian writings

	  No single model, but situational adaptation of Greco-Roman conventions

	  Some Christians produced sophisticated compositions for proclamation

	  Silent reading was not unknown, but communal performance and sharing were typical

•   Reading Culture  
	  Texts were typically performed and shared communally
	  Hearing was the primary way to encounter a text, and reading and hearing were often shared

•   Context of Early Christian Reading  
	  Often took place during the eucharistic meal
	  Readings at meals were common in ancient culture

•   Reading in Ancient Culture  
	  Reading and hearing were often shared and the basis of interpretation and conversation
	  Few people could read, but not all readers were wealthy or free

	  This evolution will be explored in more detail

•   The Significance of Silent Reading  
	  Silent reading was not unknown in ancient times, but its significance and implications are still being explored by scholars

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Reading in ancient Mediterranean cultures  
	  Reading was often a performance, with the physical text serving as a score for the reader
	  The act of reading was an interpretation of the text, with the reader bringing their own voice and interpretation to the words

•   Christian assemblies and reading  
	  Reading was not a common practice in early Christian gatherings
	  The absence of scriptural reading may be related to the prevalence of inspired speech and prophetic utterances

•   Evidence for reading in early Christianity  
	  The New Testament texts provide evidence for early Christian liturgical reading
	  The Pauline Epistles were intended for communal reading and listening

•   Types of reading in early Christianity  
	  Reading of Christian texts, such as the Pauline Epistles and other New Testament writings
	  Reading of letters and documents, such as the Letter to the Colossians and the Revelation to John

	  The New Testament writings were not initially considered Scripture
	  Paul's writings gained a certain authority by the time 2 Peter was written

•   Reading in the New Testament  
	  The Revelation to John claims an authority of a different and higher sort
	  The text claims to offer an immediate glimpse into heavenly things, rather than relying on apostolic authority or established Scripture

•   Communal reading in early Christianity  
	  The New Testament texts suggest that reading was a communal activity
	  The Letter to the Colossians and the Revelation to John imply that reading was a shared experience among the early Christian communities

	  Reading was not a central practice in early Christian worship
	  The focus was on inspired speech, prophetic utterances, and communal singing

	  The practice of reading Scripture developed over time
	  The New Testament texts suggest that reading was not a common practice in the earliest Christian communities

•   The significance of reading in early Christianity  
	  Reading was a way of interpreting and understanding the Christian texts
	  The practice of reading helped to establish the authority and significance of the New Testament writings.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Christian Worship and Scripture  
	  The genre of early Christian worship was likely a recorded heavenly vision
	  This implies a specific kind of revelatory authority, comparable to oral prophetic discourses
	  The authority comes from the origin and content, not from participation in a canonical literature

•   Early Christian Reading Material  
	  The earliest Christian communal reading material may not have been considered Scripture
	  It's unclear what the earliest Christians read, but it was likely writings by Christians themselves
	  These writings were read as documents of present or recent charisma

•   Jewish Scriptures in Early Christianity  
	  The Jewish Scriptures were widely revered by early Christian believers
	  They were reasonably well known, but not necessarily read in a communal setting
	  The Jewish identity of the earliest followers of Jesus likely influenced their reverence for Scripture

•   Paul's Letters and Scripture  
	  Paul's letters assume some form of biblical literacy
	  He cites Scripture to assert that it was written for instruction and encouragement
	  Paul's use of scriptural citations indicates that he and other early Christian authors valued biblical works

•   Access to Scripture  
	  The form of access to Scripture is unclear
	  Options include memorization, access to local copies of books, and the use of florilegia (collections of quotations)
	  2 Timothy 4:13 suggests that books and parchments were available, but the nature of this access is uncertain

•   The Synagogue as a Resource  
	  The synagogue was an important resource for early Christian teachers connected to Jewish life
	  Less itinerant Christians may have read Scripture in the synagogue rather than in church
	  The synagogue's established practice of reading Torah would have influenced early Christian reading practices

•   Participation in Synagogues  
	  Many Christians were active participants in synagogues for some decades
	  This participation would have given them access to Scripture and influenced their reading practices

•   Ownership of Scripture  
	  Economics and literacy limited ownership of personal reading material
	  However, this does not mean that individuals were isolated from the texts
	  In a communal reading culture, one member's ownership of a text could benefit the community

•   The Development of Scriptural Reading  
	  The development of scriptural reading in early Christianity is unclear
	  It's possible that scriptural reading emerged gradually, influenced by Jewish practices and Christian traditions
	  The process of scriptural reading becoming a central part of Christian worship is still a topic of study and debate

•   Implications for Understanding Scripture  
	  The early Christian understanding of Scripture was likely different from our modern understanding
	  Scripture was valued for its revelatory authority and its ability to inspire and encourage believers
	  The development of scriptural reading in early Christianity has implications for our understanding of the Bible and its role in Christian worship.

•   Early Christian Gatherings  
	  Charismatic discourses and communal banquets were common
	  Scripture was not a central focus, but rather a shared experience

	  A scholastic emphasis grew in Christian meetings

	  This involved scriptural readings and interpretation, similar to the synagogue

	  Teachers like Marcion and some gnostic groups were liturgical conservatives

•   Origins of Christian Liturgical Reading  
	  The commonly held view that Christian liturgical reading of Scripture originated in the synagogue is broadly right

•   Second-Generation Christian Ritual  
	  Scripture reading became a clearer phenomenon in a second generation of Christian ritual
	  This may have contributed to the resistance to the use and authority of the Jewish Scriptures

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Diversity of Remembering and Reciting in Ancient Communities  
	  Those who remembered, recited, composed, or prophesied in ancient communities were a diverse group
	  This diversity was present both within the Christian community and in relation to the wider world

•   Paul's Narrative about Jesus  
	  Paul shared a narrative about Jesus as crucified and risen
	  He also handed on other traditions about Jesus to the Corinthians (1 Cor 11:23)

•   Transition from Shared Discourse to Authoritative Text  
	  Written Gospels appeared shortly after Paul's time
	  This marked a transition from shared discourse in the mode of the ancient banquet to the later-normative reading of an authoritative text in Christian worship

•   Justin Martyr's Account of Reading Gospel Stories  
	  Justin Martyr cited Gospel traditions in a literary form
	  He referred to readings from the memoirs of the apostles, indicating that he viewed the texts as reliable sources for authoritative sayings and events

•   Irenaeus' Writings and the Development of the Gospel Collection  
	  Irenaeus' writings reveal a further development of the Gospel collection into a more authoritative shape
	  There were also groups that wanted to read and write other Gospels

•   Diversity of Authoritative Reading  
	  Citations, fragments, and whole copies of alternative Gospels have survived
	  This diversity of authoritative reading was a continuing reality in many Christian gatherings

•   The Emergence of Christian Reading Culture  
	  The emergence of Christian reading culture is paralleled by the appearance of a new reading technology, the codex
	  The codex allowed for random access to texts and facilitated the combination of related texts into single volumes

•   Advantages of the Codex  
	  The codex allowed for random rather than sequential access to texts
	  It facilitated the combination of related texts into single volumes

•   The Impact of the Codex on the Collection of Related Texts  
	  The codex catalyzed the collection of related texts, such as the four Gospels and the Pauline Letters
	  This collection of related texts was useful for communal reading and hearing

•   The Development of the NT Collection  
	  The Christian use of the codex was particularly enthusiastic, especially for the Christian writings that would come to form the NT collection
	  The codex allowed for the combination of numerous scrolls into single volumes, facilitating the development of the NT collection

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The codex collection  
	  Communities experienced the codex as an artifact in their gathering
	  The codex was portable, fundamental to the transmission of literature
	  Particularly important for an authoritative pattern of reading

•   Irenaeus' fourfold Gospel collection  
	  Irenaeus commended the four Gospels as inspired and exclusive
	  He argued from natural symbols of four winds and four corners
	  His arguments suggest the collection preceded and spurred his reflection

•   Justin's use of a similar data set  
	  Justin used a similar collection of Gospels without explicit reflection
	  Suggests a similar collection was available to some in the 150s

•   Libraries and the rule of faith  
	  Irenaeus associated libraries with the rule of faith (later orthodoxy)
	  However, some communities read too much or too little in church

•   Sarapion's case  
	  Sarapion, a bishop in Antioch, wrote to the church at Rhossos about their use of the Gospel of Peter
	  He judged the matter solely on content, not form

•   Sarapion's change of mind  
	  Initially, Sarapion considered the Gospel of Peter harmless
	  Later, he learned of its heretical content and rejected it

•   The Gospel of Peter  
	  Sarapion borrowed the Gospel from Docetist successors
	  Found most of it to be from the true teaching of Jesus, but with some additions

•   Communal use of apocryphal documents  
	  Sarapion's story suggests apocryphal documents were not necessarily a mark of theological departure
	  The Rhossians may simply not have had other Gospels to read

•   Liturgical reading in Oxyrhynchus  
	  The trash heaps of Oxyrhynchus indicate a lack of choice for liturgical reading
	  The Shepherd of Hermas was a popular text among discarded fragments

•   The significance of the codex collection  
	  The codex collection was a key factor in the transmission of literature
	  Particularly important for an authoritative pattern of reading

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Christian Reading Practices  
	  Reading choices were influenced by theology
	  The question of which books were actually known or owned was important
	  The formation of early Christian communal reading practices was complex

•   The Concept of Canon  
	  The term "canon" is a modern concept
	  In ancient Greek, "kanon" referred to a ruler or yardstick, not a collection of books
	  Early Christian writers used "kanon" to refer to the rule of faith, a doctrinal measure of orthodoxy

•   Emergence of NT Books  
	  Most NT books emerged as popular and authoritative across the first few centuries
	  The process was not arbitrary, but rather influenced by aggregation and codex technology

•   Exceptions and Variations  
	  Some communities continued to use or produce different scriptural books
	  The boundaries of what could be read in church were highly porous

•   The Muratorian Fragment  
	  An eighth-century manuscript that reproduces a fragmentary list of authoritative books
	  The list is similar to the writings of Victorinus of Pettau and may date to the late third century

•   Content of the Muratorian Fragment  
	  Defends a list of NT works akin to the canonical set
	  Does not mention Hebrews, 1 or 2 Peter, or 3 John
	  Includes the Apocalypse of Peter and the Shepherd of Hermas

•   Different Forms of Reading  
	  The Muratorian fragment raises questions about different forms of reading or contexts for reading
	  The real issue was not the formation of a book called the Bible, but the right practice of liturgical reading

•   Athanasius' List  
	  In 367, Athanasius, bishop of Alexandria, wrote a letter specifying the limits of proper scriptural reading
	  Provided the oldest list of the 27 books generally regarded as the NT

•   The Definition of a Bible  
	  Athanasius' list was not a definition of a Bible in the modern sense
	  The concept of a Bible as a single, authoritative collection of books developed over time

•   Complexity of Early Christian Reading Practices  
	  The formation of early Christian communal reading practices was complex and influenced by various factors
	  The boundaries of what could be read in church were highly porous and may never have reflected a supposed canon.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Early Christian Canon  
	  There was no single, unified Christian book or canon in the early church
	  Most Christians would not have owned or read the entire canon

•   The Canon Muratori  
	  A fragment of the Canon Muratori, a 4th-century document, is discussed
	  The Latin is poor, making it hard to interpret the text's meaning

•   Athanasius' Canon  
	  In his Festal Letter 20, Athanasius distinguishes between books measured by the rule of faith (canonized) and those not measured by the rule (read-only)

•   Books in the Canon  
	  The Hebrew Bible and NT are considered canonized
	  Deuterocanonical books (e.g., Esther, Didache, Shepherd of Hermas) are read-only

•   Apocryphal Books  
	  Athanasius rejects apocryphal books, which he considers hidden or requiring spiritual maturity
	  These books were likely read privately in academic or scholastic circles

•   Liturgical Use  
	  All books (except apocryphal) were suitable for some liturgical use
	  Athanasius establishes a liturgical canon, with canonized books being authenticated by the rule of faith

•   Doctrinal Canon  
	  Canonized books are considered sources of definitive doctrine and springs of salvation
	  They are authenticated by the rule of faith and are the basis for doctrine

•   Read-Only Books  
	  These books are not marginalized, but rather favored for reading to new converts
	  They are used in catechetical assemblies, but not for mystagogy (post-baptismal education)

•   Implications of Athanasius' Decree  
	  People would continue to read apocryphal books privately, in discussion circles
	  Other texts, like martyr acts, might have been read, but Athanasius is silent about them

•   Athanasius' Authority  
	  Athanasius' decree is not about imposing a new rule, but rather associating his episcopal authority with established practice in churches

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Christian Reading Culture  
	  Limited literacy and reading skills
	  Reading was not assigned randomly, but required skills and training
	  Social status and professional training were initially associated with reading

•   Roles and Readers  
	  Early texts are silent or unclear about who read
	  Reading was associated with leadership, but not necessarily a formal or ordained role
	  Examples of leaders who read include 1 Timothy and 2 Clement

•   Development of Roles  
	  The idea of a reader as a specific office appears in the 3rd century
	  Cyprian of Carthage refers to readers as a rung on the ladder of ordained clergy
	  Readers were expected to progress to the rank of presbyter

•   Liturgical Reading  
	  The extended and differentiated readings of the 4th century eucharistic liturgy
	  Readers continued to have roles, but the preeminence of the Gospels led to a shift in authority
	  The Apostolic Constitutions assert that a presbyter or deacon should read the Gospels

•   Accommodation of Manuscripts  
	  Christian writings show a distinctive emphasis on word divisions and other aids to the semi-trained reader
	  This reflects the social diversity of early Christian communities and the establishment of new structures of authority

•   Authority and Literacy  
	  Literacy became a source of authority in early Christian communities
	  The reader was not just a specialist performer, but a leader whose authority came from the texts proclaimed

•   Assemblies for the Word  
	  The eucharistic meal was the main communal event, including teaching and discourse
	  Reading or interpretation of Scripture was a part of this event from an early point

•   Praying Together  
	  The eucharistic meal was a time of prayer and communal worship
	  Reading and interpretation of Scripture were part of this prayer and worship

•   Social Diversity and Literacy  
	  The social diversity of early Christian communities is reflected in the accommodation of manuscripts
	  Literacy became a source of authority, rather than just a marker of social status

•   Evolution of Reading Culture  
	  The early Christian reading culture evolved over time, with a shift from a specialist performer to a leader
	  The development of new structures of authority and the emphasis on literacy reflect this evolution

•   Impact on Worship  
	  The emphasis on reading and literacy had a significant impact on early Christian worship
	  The development of new structures of authority and the preeminence of the Gospels led to changes in the liturgy and the role of the reader.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Christian Gatherings  
	  The earliest Christian gatherings are not well-documented
	  Acts 2:42 mentions teaching, fellowship, breaking of bread, and prayers, but it's unclear if these were separate events

•   Possible Types of Gatherings  
	  Some scholars suggest that the breaking of bread and prayers in Acts 2:42 refer to the Eucharist and periodic prayers
	  Others propose that the teaching and fellowship were separate events

•   Corinthian Gatherings  
	  1 Corinthians 10 and 11 discuss the Eucharist and its importance
	  Chapter 14 mentions undefined events where unbelievers may be present, but it's unclear if these were separate events

•   Primitive Christian Worship  
	  There is no clear evidence of a primitive Christian worship service or meetings of an entire local Christian community at the earliest times

•   Early Evidence of Other Assemblies  
	  The earliest evidence for other assemblies is linked to Christian observance of specific times of prayer
	  Pliny the Younger's letter to Trajan (111 CE) mentions a communal prayer meeting before dawn

•   Character of Pliny's Account  
	  Pliny's account was written for his imperial patron and not for liturgical historians
	  It's unclear if the communal prayer meeting was a regular occurrence

•   Tertullian's Account  
	  Tertullian's writings indicate that some Christians in Africa agreed on communal gatherings for set hours of prayer, at least on fast days
	  These gatherings included a morning assembly with the Eucharist, but no extended reading or conversation

•   Origen's Account  
	  Origen encourages his readers to pray at the prescribed hours in a regular place of assembly
	  He suggests that this gathering would be accompanied by angelic company

•   Monastic Practice  
	  Origen's account is similar to the theory of later monastic practice, where individuals offer prayers in a regular place of assembly

•   Conclusion  
	  The earliest Christian gatherings are not well-documented, and it's unclear if they were separate from the Eucharist
	  The evidence suggests that communal prayer meetings became more common over time, particularly in Africa and Asia Minor.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Shared Prayer  
	  Shared times of prayer at set hours were a common practice
	  These were not necessarily assemblies of the whole community, but rather individuals gathering to pray together

•   Catechesis  
	  Origen and Tertullian wrote extensively on Scripture, faith, and ethics, indicating a culture of teaching and catechesis
	  Catechesis referred to both instruction in general and teaching in preparation for baptism

•   Communal Expression of Catechesis  
	  Teaching took place in gatherings for Eucharist and prayer
	  Catechesis often overlapped with instruction for the already-baptized and aspiring catechumens

•   Tertullian's Description of Christian Gatherings  
	  Tertullian described evening agape meetings and morning prayer gatherings with eucharistic distribution
	  He also mentioned a different type of meeting focused on Scripture and pastoral discipline

•   Scripture-Focused Meetings  
	  These meetings were like study sessions, where Scripture was the main focus
	  They were not necessarily part of the main meal assembly, where other forms of discourse were more prominent

•   Catechetical Gatherings  
	  Tertullian mentioned catechetical gatherings as a reason for a Christian woman to leave the house
	  These gatherings involved the dispensing of the word of God

•   Word: Reading and Preaching  
	  Origen's work implies a scholastic setting for training an elite group
	  Many of his Homilies were likely delivered in communal gatherings, such as prayer assemblies on fast days

•   Preaching and Teaching  
	  Origen often preached on Wednesdays and Fridays, indicating a focus on regular preaching and teaching
	  Word-centered gatherings were initially structured around need or occasion, rather than being a regular part of the day or week

•   Services of the Word  
	  The Apostolic Tradition mentions instruction in relation to times for prayer
	  The faithful were encouraged to pray before work and prioritize catechesis in the word

•   Early Christian Gatherings  
	  Early Christian gatherings were likely structured around need or occasion, rather than being a regular part of the day or week
	  These gatherings were focused on prayer, catechesis, and the word of God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Catechetical instruction in the early Church  
	  Involved expounding of scriptural texts
	  Was a regular part of daily life, not just on special occasions

•   Morning assemblies  
	  Were held for catechumens (those preparing for baptism)
	  Involved prayer, instruction, and the laying on of hands

•   Scriptural focus  
	  Catechumens were expected to hear the word for three years
	  Instruction was often prayerful or liturgical in nature

•   Separation of catechumens and faithful  
	  Catechumens prayed separately from the faithful
	  Did not participate in the kiss of peace

•   Kissing and dressing  
	  Men and women were to greet each other separately
	  Women were to cover their heads with a pallium

•   Participation of other members  
	  Other members of the community were encouraged to participate
	  Some complexities arose around proper kissing and dressing

•   Private ownership of scriptural texts  
	  Some Christians, particularly women of means, collected and exchanged scriptural texts
	  Private ownership of texts grew, but they were still associated with the church

•   Scriptural texts in the church  
	  Texts found in churches were particularly targeted during the Great Persecution
	  Discovery of texts in houses led to the presumption that Christians met there

•   Catechetical instruction at daily hours of prayer  
	  Was common by the third century
	  Not everyone participated, and some were frustrated by seasonal fluctuations

•   Importance of scriptural instruction  
	  Was seen as essential for spiritual growth and development
	  Origen complained about those who showed up only on feast days or left early

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Lent and Catechesis  
	  Lent emerged as a focus for catechesis in the 4th century
	  Associated with preparation for Easter baptism and emphasis on fasting and self-examination
	  Daily assemblies in Lent included sermons with moral exhortation

•   Christian Gatherings  
	  Various Christian gatherings centered on Scripture, teaching, and prayer
	  Not the primary weekly expression of Christian identity
	  Often related to communal observance of the daily hours of prayer

•   Catechesis  
	  Not solely for education of baptismal candidates
	  Conducted for practical reasons and because of the importance of prayer and learning
	  Often took place in separate gatherings from the Eucharist

•   Eucharist and Catechesis  
	  The Eucharist became more liturgical and less a meal with opportunity for discourse
	  Separate catechetical gatherings became more important as a result
	  The Eucharist remained the primary focus, with scriptural readings added later

•   Synaxis  
	  A common term for Christian gatherings in the 3rd and 4th centuries
	  Could refer to the Eucharist or other gatherings for prayer and catechesis
	  Translated as "service" in the modern sense, but originated from various Christian assemblies

•   Patterns of Reading  
	  Early Christian communal reading was not based on set patterns like lectionaries
	  Texts were chosen according to occasion or opportunity
	  Clerical leadership exercised freedom in reading choices well into the 4th and 5th centuries

•   Synagogue Influence  
	  The assumption that synagogue practice was the immediate source of Christian reading patterns is a common mistake
	  There was no lectionary cycle in the synagogue of Jesus' time

•   Extended Readings  
	  Clerical leadership often chose to read through a particular text or texts in an extended reading
	  Not merely ephemeral or random choices
	  Emerged as a pattern of reading in the 4th and 5th centuries

•   Authoritative Texts  
	  The appropriateness of certain authoritative texts to particular days or circumstances was always apparent
	  Fixed patterns of reading emerged early, but were not rigidly adhered to

•   Development of Lectionaries  
	  Fixed patterns of reading emerged early, but it is unclear when lectionaries became a standard practice
	  The development of lectionaries is a topic that requires further research and study

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Christian Readings  
	  The Peri Pascha (On the Passover) of Melito of Sardis is one of the earliest known homilies.
	  It references a Scripture reading that would have formed part of the vigil of Pascha (not Easter Sunday).

•   Scripture Readings at Paschal Vigils  
	  The exodus account may have been part of a longer set of readings.
	  Uncertainty surrounds the form and content of contemporary Jewish celebration of Passover.

•   Development of Fixity around Easter  
	  A measure of fixity probably developed around Easter in the third century.
	  This fixity was likely tied to the reading of Scripture and the celebration of martyrs' feasts.

•   Augustine's Lectionary  
	  Augustine's sermons show a rich set of norms around Easter, martyrs' feasts, and Christmas/Epiphany.
	  He had discretion to choose most readings for his church at Hippo.

•   Augustine's Reading Practices  
	  He sometimes preached through a particular book in a sequence of homilies.
	  He might accept a lector's mistake as providential and preach accordingly.

•   Expected Readings at Christmas and Epiphany  
	  At Christmas, his community expected the Lukan infancy story to be read.
	  At Epiphany, the Matthean equivalent was expected.

•   Readings at Martyrs' Feasts  
	  The acts of the particular saints being celebrated could be read liturgically.
	  This suggests that Scripture was not fixed, but rather flexible.

•   Lenten Readings  
	  Augustine gave his Tractates on the Gospel of John in course during Lent.
	  He also gave some of his Expositions on the Psalms in course.

•   Baptismal Catechesis Readings  
	  On the day the Lord's Prayer was to be recited by candidates for baptism, the text of Jesus' advice on prayer was read.
	  The Psalms were prominent in Augustine's preaching and the liturgy.

•   Established Readings and Innovation  
	  The Triduum and Easter season had established readings.
	  Augustine was not averse to innovation, and even wanted to read different versions of the Passion Narrative.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Christian Worship  
	  The order of Gospel readings was not fixed in the early Christian period
	  The order of readings varied from year to year and from place to place

•   Augustine's Lectionary  
	  Augustine used a series of readings and sermons that were chosen at his discretion
	  Some readings were associated with major seasons, but there was no set order
	  Augustine would often adjust his readings to suit the mood of the congregation

•   Easter Gospel Stories  
	  The tradition of reading Easter Gospel stories led Augustine to adjourn his Tractates on the Gospel of John
	  He instead expounded on the Letters of John, which were read prior to the Gospel at the Eucharist

•   Acts and Johannine Epistles  
	  A course of reading through Acts began at Hippo, but there was no set passage for set days
	  The Johannine Epistles were also read, but not as part of a set lectionary

•   The Lectionary and the Book  
	  The concept of a set order of readings for the year began to develop in the 4th century
	  The first surviving lectionary is fragmentary and dates back to the 4th century

•   Jerusalem Lectionary  
	  The pilgrim Egeria observed that readings at Jerusalem were often chosen to complement the day or place
	  The Armenian and Georgian versions of the Jerusalem lectionary from the 5th century confirm this

•   Liturgical Reading at Jerusalem  
	  Liturgical reading at Jerusalem focused on major festivals related to the life of Jesus and the saints
	  Martyr acts were also read as Scripture on their heroes' feasts

•   Jewish Scriptures and NT Readings  
	  Jewish Scriptures were read less frequently than NT readings
	  NT readings became a clear set of Christian Scriptures

•   Vigils and Stational Liturgies  
	  Vigils and stational liturgies related to Holy Week provided main appearances for OT readings
	  These appearances were often linked to the celebration of the Eucharist

•   Consolidated Feasts  
	  The consolidated Epiphany/Nativity feast involved reading both Matthean and Lukan infancy stories
	  Good Friday involved reading all four Passion Narratives

•   The Lectionary as a Book  
	  Books designed to provide a pattern of reading began to appear in the 4th century
	  The oldest surviving lectionary is fragmentary and difficult to interpret.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Early Christian Worship  
	  The early Christian worship involved a selection of texts from different books for reading.
	  This selection suggests that some discretion was used in choosing the texts to be read.
    Liturgical Reading Patterns  
	  Later patterns of liturgical reading emerged from the early period of elaboration and consolidation.
	  These patterns were not just related to feasts, but also to the wider liturgical year.
    Preachers and Texts  
	  The great preachers of the fourth century, such as John Chrysostom and Augustine of Hippo, represent the maturity of a Christian rhetorical tradition.
	  Earlier texts, such as those of Melito, provide insight into how this tradition developed.
    Melito's Paschal Sermon  
	  Melito's paschal sermon, discovered in 1936, is a powerful exposition of the resurrection.
	  The sermon is notable for its negativity towards Judaism, which can be understood in the context of tensions between Jews and Christians.
    Melito's Style and Influence  
	  Melito's prose and argument have a clear affinity with Jewish texts and authors.
	  His use of antitheses and lists is reminiscent of the poetic parallelism of the Psalms and Wisdom literature of Judaism.
    The Character of Melito's Prose  
	  Melito's prose is characterized by its dramatic and narrative style.
	  His use of rhetorical devices, such as antitheses and lists, serves to introduce his theme and draw conclusions.
    The Exposition of the Resurrection  
	  Melito's sermon is a powerful exposition of the resurrection, which is seen as a triumph over death.
	  The sermon draws on the exodus story, which is seen as a type of the need for and reality of Christ's triumph over death.
    The Typological Approach  
	  Melito's approach to the text is typological, seeing the exodus story as a type of the need for and reality of Christ's triumph over death.
	  This approach is deeply supersessionist, seeing the Jewish scriptures as pointing to the reality of Christ's triumph over death.
    The Significance of Melito's Sermon  
	  Melito's sermon is significant for its insight into the development of the Christian rhetorical tradition.
	  It also provides a window into the tensions between Jews and Christians in the early Christian period.
    The Earliest Lectionary Texts  
	  The earliest lectionary texts do not conform to the later Byzantine pattern of readings.
	  These texts are significant for their insight into the early Christian worship and the development of the lectionary system.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Melito's View of Scripture  
	  Sees history as an indistinct image that gives way to eternal truth
	  Views the text as a window to content, to be interpreted and expounded

•   Melito's Christology  
	  Identifies Christ as the beginning and end, the king, and the Lord
	  Sees Christ as the one who rose from the dead and is seated at the right hand of the Father

•   Origen's Achievements  
	  One of the great thinkers of the early church and Christianity
	  Made significant contributions to text criticism, interpretation, and theology

•   Origen's Life and Work  
	  Suffered during the persecution of Decius (250)
	  Worked as a private and ecclesiastical teacher with an ascetic outlook

•   Origen's Homilies  
	  Often delivered orally at gatherings for prayer or Eucharist
	  Amount to an oral running commentary on the biblical text

•   Influence of Hellenistic Judaism  
	  Origen was influenced by Philo's works and allegorical exegesis
	  Philo's description of synagogue interpretation as a priestly activity also influenced Origen

•   Origen's Preaching Style  
	  Often composed on the spot, determined by the structure of the biblical text
	  Used a combination of exegesis and allegory in his homilies

•   Example of Origen's Homily  
	  Delivered a homily on the story of the belly-myther of Endor
	  Preached at an assembly for one of the hours of prayer in Jerusalem

•   Origen's Treatment of Scripture  
	  Had a strong concern with the literal meaning of the text
	  Was willing to challenge traditional interpretations and offer new insights

•   Controversy Surrounding Origen  
	  His homilies were later controversial, particularly his treatment of the story of the belly-myther of Endor
	  His views on scripture and preaching were seen as innovative and challenging by some.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Origen's Approach to Reading the OT  
	  Shares a typological approach with Melito and other ancient Christian preachers
	  Sees the OT as a text that demands to be read and understood
	  Insists on the literal meaning of the text, rather than allegorical or spiritualizing interpretations

•   Literal Interpretation of the OT  
	  Origen's homily on 1 Samuel 28 shows his commitment to literal interpretation
	  He sees the text as a struggle that invites capable readers and hearers into its meaning
	  The text has general relevance due to its implications for postmortem existence

•   Preaching as a Struggle with the Text  
	  Origen's preaching is both literal and allegorical
	  He sees homiletics as a struggle with the text itself, rather than resolving it into something else
	  This approach has been enduring in Christian preaching tradition

•   The Importance of the Text  
	  Origen sees the text as wondrously written and worthy of understanding
	  He believes that God has revealed the text to those who are capable of understanding it
	  The text itself demands to be read and understood

•   Preaching and the Psalms  
	  The Psalms became a popular book in the nascent Christian Bible
	  Preachers such as Augustine and Chrysostom made significant contributions to the interpretation of the Psalms

•   Augustine's Approach to Preaching  
	  Leans towards the more exegetical tradition of Origen
	  Focuses on the literal meaning of the text, rather than allegorical or spiritualizing interpretations

•   Chrysostom's Approach to Preaching  
	  Represents a high point in the application of ancient high rhetorical style to sermons
	  Uses the Psalms as a model for his sermons, applying ancient rhetorical techniques to the text

•   The Development of Christian Preaching  
	  The growth and development of Christianity and its liturgy through the fourth century changed the challenge of understanding and describing preaching
	  The quantity of homiletic material that has survived has increased significantly
	  The existence of material evidence for Christian worship has provided a new context for understanding preaching

•   The Importance of Context  
	  The context of Christian worship and liturgy is crucial for understanding preaching
	  The development of churches and church architecture has influenced the way preaching is done
	  The use of space, objects, and bodies in worship has implications for the way preaching is received and understood

•   The Legacy of Origen and Other Preachers  
	  Origen's approach to preaching has had a lasting impact on Christian preaching tradition
	  Other preachers such as Augustine and Chrysostom have built on Origen's approach, contributing to the development of Christian preaching.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    John Chrysostom's Preaching  
	  Chrysostom's sermons were delivered in Antioch and Constantinople, two of the greatest cities of the ancient Mediterranean world.
	  His sermons were among the first examples of preaching as public theology, addressing civic and spiritual significance.
    Rhetorical Sophistication  
	  Chrysostom's sermons reflected a high level of rhetorical sophistication, presenting a new height in ancient preaching and oratory.
	  His sermons were characterized by matter-of-fact exegesis, in contrast to the allegorical approach common in Alexandria.
    The Homilies on the Statues  
	  Chrysostom delivered a set of sermons during a social and political crisis in Antioch in 387.
	  The Homilies on the Statues were delivered during Lent, encouraging the fearful populace to repentance and defending Christianity.
    The Significance of Civic Identity  
	  Chrysostom used the fact that Antioch had been stripped of its status as a metropolis to reflect on the real significance of civic identity.
	  He argued that virtue, not status, is the real honor of a city.
    The Honor of a City  
	  Chrysostom defined the honor of a city as the virtue and piety of its inhabitants.
	  He contrasted this with the external markers of status, such as large buildings and beautiful architecture.
    The Unique Honor of Antioch  
	  Chrysostom pointed out that Antioch had a unique honor, being the city where the disciples were first called Christians.
	  This honor was not shared by any other city, not even Rome.
    The Significance of Christianity  
	  Chrysostom saw Christianity as a source of boldness and virtue, which gave Antioch a unique love-charm for Christ.
	  He argued that this was the true honor of the city, and one that could not be taken away by external events.
    Augustine's Legacy  
	  Augustine had many claims to ecclesial and theological fame, but is not discussed in relation to Chrysostom's preaching in this text.
	  The text mentions Augustine as a separate figure, but does not provide further information about his relationship to Chrysostom's work.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Augustine's Works  
	  Wrote the Confessions and City of God, but his largest work is the Expositions on the Psalms
	  Occupies 19,000 columns in the Patrologia Latina and multiple volumes in modern translations
    Augustine's Reputation  
	  Often associated with original sin and other labels, but his sermons reveal a thoughtful and pastoral bishop
	  His dogmatic works are well-known, but his sermons show a more nuanced and intellectual side
    The Expositions on the Psalms  
	  Part of a larger category of homilies, many of which were taken down by secretaries from actual delivery as sermons
	  Treats the Psalms as addressed to Christ, about Christ, or spoken by him
    Augustine's Interpretation Style  
	  Reflects the tradition of typological interpretation, seeing the Psalms as referring to Christ
	  Focuses on the literary aspects of the Psalms, anticipating later commentators on poetic parallelism
    Augustine's Use of Rhetoric  
	  Avoids rhetorical heights, but comments on rhetoric itself and engages with literary aspects of the Psalms
	  Notes the use of repetition in Scripture to commend divine eloquence
    The Basilica at Hippo  
	  Survives to the present day, if in ruins
	  Includes the cathedra, or episcopal throne, in the apse
    The Context of Augustine's Sermons  
	  Some sermons come from daily eucharistic celebrations, others from communal observance of daily prayer or catechetical assemblies
	  May have been held in a smaller chapel
    Augustine's Treatment of Psalm 72  
	  Far more in the tradition of Origen than Chrysostom
	  Uses a rhetorically modest running commentary, determined by the psalm itself
    The Significance of Augustine's Expositions  
	  Show a single-mindedness of interpretative focus, but not monotonous
	  Anticipate later commentators on poetic parallelism and literary aspects of the Psalms
    The Relationship Between Reading and Preaching  
	  Both are verbal discourse, but have a common origin in the ancient Christian banquet
	  Gradually developed into distinct types of events, with attention to words leading to different types of events

	  The very first Christian singing was also Jewish singing.
	  The Psalms come to mind as a possible influence, but Jewish psalmody does not provide a neat model for continuation or adoption by Christians.

	  The word "psalmos" literally means songs performed accompanied by a stringed instrument.

•   Clement's views on music in Christian community life

•   Clement's approach to music  
	  He viewed all of life (liturgy, labor, leisure) as service to the true Word
	  He favored a sacramental approach to life, not a secularizing one

•   Clement's views on musical idioms  
	  He favored the traditional Dorian mode based on a diatonic scale
	  He criticized music with too much decoration (poikilia)
	  He preferred constant, thoughtful, and sober demeanor in music

	  He associated lyres and similar instruments with the domestic sphere

	  The text has retained enough of its notation to give a sense of both words and music

•   Ancient Christian Worship and Music  
	  Christians in ancient times sang hymns and psalms as part of their worship
	  This tradition was inherited from Jewish psalmodic traditions
	  Singing was a form of reading and proclamation of a text, rather than music in the modern sense

•   Fourth-Century Hymnody  
	  Paul and Tertullian envisioned the vocalization of traditional or inspired texts outside of canonical writings
	  This led to a tendency to canonize singing, with a focus on scriptural texts

•   Laodicean Canons  
	  The canons of Laodicea express concerns about songs and singers
	  Canon 49 prohibits the reading of personal psalms in church and allows only those of canonical books
	  Canon 15 insists on authorized readers and the use of actual books to control liturgical content

•   Psalmodic Traditions  
	  Psalms were performed in long sequences, but were also interspersed with Scripture readings (Canon 17)
	  Rules are often better evidence for transgression than for compliance
	  Attitudes toward hymnody varied over time and from place to place

•   Monastic and Daily Prayer  
	  The singing of psalms was a feature of monastic and daily prayer in the fourth century
	  Psalms were incorporated into an ordered sequence of readings, with local customs and variations

•   Eucharist and Psalmodic Traditions  
	  The Eucharist was a place for psalmody, which was increasingly incorporated into an ordered sequence of readings
	  The Apostolic Constitutions prescribe the use of psalms after two readings from the OT and before readings from the NT

•   Athanasius of Alexandria  
	  Athanasius wrote a sophisticated treatise on the use of the Psalms in his Letter to Marcellinus
	  The letter provides advice about use of the Psalms, including some that were not part of the canonical Psalter

•   Scriptural Texts and Psalms  
	  Psalms were regarded as part of the reading of Scripture, rather than a separate activity
	  Their performance was distinctive and musical

	  The use of psalms in Christian worship was a way of proclaiming a text, rather than a form of music in the modern sense

•   Evolution of Christian Worship  
	  Christian worship and music evolved over time, with a focus on scriptural texts and psalms
	  The use of psalms and hymns became a characteristic feature of Christian churches in the Middle Ages and beyond.

•   Purpose of Singing

•   Phos Hilaron  
	  This hymn is a characteristic example of the urban or cathedral style of prayer.
	  It is a hymn of praise to God, the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.

•   The Acts of John  : A novelistic account from the late 2nd century that expands on the apostle John's life.
	  Recalls Jesus' Last Supper, which centers on a dance and a hymn.

•   The Dance in the Acts of John  : A circle of people holding hands, with Jesus in the middle.
	  The dance is accompanied by a trinitarian litany and paradoxical epithets.
	  The participants respond with "Amen" to the hymn.

•   The Purpose of the Dance  : May represent a custom of the community in which the work was produced.
	  May have been an accompaniment to a meditative hymn of praise.
	  May have been an adjunct to the song and vice versa.

	  The intended authorship may have had an interest in esoteric and mystical matters.

•   Dance in Ancient Greece and Rome  : Music and poetry were typically accompanied by choral movement.
	  Plato suggested that the origins of dance lay in the addition of gestures to forms of utterance.

•   Plato's Ideal Community  : Imagined the ideal community as dancing together.
	  This ideal seems to have remained just that, as far as most dances and dancers were concerned.

•   Christians and Jews  : May have been among the exceptions to the rarity of dance in the late ancient world.
	  Some Christians and Jews may have seen dance as a way to reflect or create a coherence between the body and the inner self.

•   Philo of Alexandria  : Reported on the Therapeutae, an ascetic and philosophically minded Jewish community.
	  The Therapeutae sang original hymns in honor of God, accompanied by gestures and dance.

•   Clement's Account  : Adds his own explanation of similar practices among Christians.
	  Suggests that such dances may have really occurred among Christians.

•   The Significance of Dance  : May have been a way to create a coherence between the body and the inner self.
	  May have been a way to reflect the God encountered in Christ.

	  Imagined rather than historical, but likely based on real practices

	  Christian dances would have taken place indoors, probably as part of the communal meal
	  Before the fourth century, unlikely to have taken place in outdoor or public settings

	  Cemeteries were sometimes included as no-go areas during persecutions
