	  Including at least some of those referred to as gnostics
	  May also be a localized tradition, more evident in places like Alexandria

•   Tensions in Alexandria  
	  Tensions between Christianity based on scholastic culture and authority
	  Dance may have been seen as a part of this scholastic culture
	  Apparatus of dance may have been associated with this elite group

•   Later Evidence  
	  Later evidence makes a connection between dance and the cult of the martyrs
	  May have been an earlier connection too
	  Will be discussed further in the text

•   Popular Form of Piety  
	  Dance may suggest a popular form of piety, but
	  Early Christian dance actually seems to be the domain of a fairly elite group

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Christianity and Dance  
	  There is limited evidence of dance in early Christianity before 300 AD
	  Some hints suggest that the New Prophecy movement, later labeled Montanism, may have adopted traditional Phrygian dance practices

•   Dance in the 4th Century  
	  Dancing becomes a more public and controversial feature of Christian devotion in the 4th century
	  Commemorations of saints take on forms related to local festivals and processions

•   John Chrysostom's Reference to Dance  
	  In 387 AD, John Chrysostom referred to dancing on the feasts of martyrs in Antioch
	  He described the dancing as a beautiful and joyful celebration

•   Processions and Dance  
	  Processions for the saints may have involved literal dancing
	  The metaphorical "general" at the head of the procession could have been the bishop or the relics of the martyrs

•   Victricius' Encouragement of Dance  
	  Victricius, bishop of Rouen in Gaul, urged enthusiastic dance among acts of devotion to the martyrs
	  He linked dance with song and described the dancing as a way to "wear out" and "tire" oneself with joy

•   Public Christian Performance  
	  The church in imperial cities and coloniae provided civic rituals that could be compared to the celebrations accompanying the arrival of victorious emperors
	  Dance was a medium employed within this new genre of the procession

•   Civic and Ecclesial Dimensions  
	  The dances had a civic character as well as an ecclesial one
	  They also had a personal and familial dimension, especially in relation to the care of the dead

•   Performance in Public Venues  
	  The processional dances for the saints were performed in public venues, such as cemeteries and public spaces
	  These venues were effectively the possession of Christianized local communities

•   Monumental Churches and Storytelling  
	  The church marked the space of the towns with monumental churches and with their stories of the victorious dead
	  The dances were a way to celebrate the martyrs and their stories

•   Dance as a Medium of Expression  
	  Dance was a medium employed within the new genre of the procession
	  It was a way to express joy, devotion, and community in public Christian performance.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Christian Processions  
	  Emerged in Mediterranean cities at varying speeds
	  In Rome, traditional patterns persisted until the 6th century
	  Christian processions were initially distinct from Greco-Roman customs

•   Dancing and the Dead  
	  Associated with Greco-Roman custom and folk tradition
	  Added to anxiety among bishops, especially in the West
	  Some bishops distinguished between sober and uncontrolled dancing

•   Attitudes to Celebratory Dancing  
	  Varied among bishops, with some opposing it entirely
	  Others saw it as acceptable, but only when disciplined by the soul
	  Dancing was often linked to meals with and for the dead

•   Organized Choirs  
	  May have danced in church at times
	  Christians continued to dance at festivals of saints
	  This practice lies more in popular culture than in ecclesial and liturgical practice

•   Faith and Devotion  
	  Devotion expressed in dancing and singing was closely related to faith
	  Accounts of liturgical history often underestimate this connection
	  Devotion was not limited to specialists, but was a communal practice

•   Modern Christian Worship  
	  Often characterized as a spectacle, performed by specialists
	  Participation is predominantly aural and visual
	  Song is one aspect of practice where a communal dimension is enacted

•   Ancient Christian Worship  
	  Both participation and spectacle had their place
	  Spectacle only gradually became a regular feature of liturgical gatherings
	  Singing was a collective possibility, whether in chorus or by individuals

•   Instrumental Music  
	  Was not used in early Christian worship
	  This was due to cultural continuity with domestic Jewish piety
	  Aversion to instrumental music was also due to its association with sacrificial cultus

•   Athanasius' Views  
	  Opposed an aestheticizing approach to singing
	  Saw song and dance as acceptable only when disciplined by the soul
	  His views illustrate the importance of harmony between the physical and spiritual self

•   Early Christian Song and Dance  
	  Involved a measure of cultural continuity with domestic Jewish piety
	  Was often seen as a way to express devotion and faith
	  Was not limited to specialists, but was a communal practice.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. Initiation in Early Christianity  
	  Baptism was a universal practice in earliest Christianity
	  It was linked to Jesus' command to baptize (Matt. 28:19)
	  Baptism was older than Jesus' ministry and the church

  2. Origins of Christian Baptism  
	  Baptism was influenced by ancient Jewish practices
	  Water and washing were central to Jewish rituals and stories

  3. Jewish Roots of Baptism  
	  Creation story began with God's spirit moving over the deep (Gen. 1)
	  Moses led the Israelites through water out of Egypt (Exod. 14)
	  Joshua led them through water into the promised land (Josh. 3)

  4. Water Rituals in Judaism  
	  Mosaic law prescribed water rituals for purity and sacrifice (Exod. 30; Lev. 15)
	  Washing became a means to extend temple holiness into daily life

  5. Ritual Baths in Judaism  
	  Ritual baths (miqvaot) were excavated at the Jerusalem temple
	  They enabled a symbolic transition from impurity to worship

  6. Architecture of Ritual Baths  
	  Internal dividers or walls marked a path for the bather to follow
	  The path invited movement into the water and out the other side

  7. Symbolic Significance of Water  
	  Water functioned as a boundary between two states of being
	  It was a path from everyday life to the different world of the temple

  8. Christian Baptism as a Recasting of Ancient Practice  
	  Christian baptism recast ancient religious practice and natural symbols
	  It conveyed new meanings and purposes

  9. Diversity of Baptismal Practices  
	  There was diversity of specific practices and theology in early Christianity
	  The fact of baptism was generally assumed

  10. Importance of Understanding Baptismal Imagery  
	  Robin Jensen's book "Baptismal Imagery in Early Christianity" provides valuable insights
	  It should be consulted for a deeper understanding of baptismal practices and theology

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Ritual Washing in Judaism  
	  Ritual washing was used to change an individual's status before God
	  It made them ready for divine service
	  This practice was present in earlier Judaism

•   Miqvaot and Ritual Washing  
	  Miqvaot (ritual baths) were used for purification and ritual washing
	  They were used for persons and objects requiring purification
	  Converts to Judaism were immersed ritually as part of their initiation

•   Christian Baptism and Jewish Immersion  
	  Christian baptism was not directly influenced by Jewish immersion of proselytes
	  However, the rabbinic Jewish practice of immersing converts may have been encouraged by the Christian example
	  The emphasis on immersion in Jewish conversion rituals may have been influenced by Christian baptism

•   Qumran and the Dead Sea Scrolls  
	  Excavations at Khirbet Qumran revealed a settlement with numerous miqvaot
	  The Qumran settlement and the Dead Sea Scrolls show a strong emphasis on ritual washing
	  The authors of the Dead Sea Scrolls and the inhabitants of Qumran used ritual baths as part of their lifestyle

•   Comparison with John the Baptizer  
	  The Qumran community and the authors of the Dead Sea Scrolls proclaimed a radical departure from mainstream Jewish society
	  They used water and ritual washing as part of their symbolic action
	  This has led to comparisons with John the Baptizer

•   Initiation and Ritual Washing  
	  The Qumran community and the authors of the Dead Sea Scrolls used ritual washing as part of their initiation and repentance practices
	  However, this was not a unique baptism for initiation or repentance
	  It was instead a version of the repeated ritual washing practices found elsewhere in Judaism

•   The Value of Qumran and the Dead Sea Scrolls  
	  The Qumran settlement and the Dead Sea Scrolls add to our understanding of the ritual world of first-century Jews
	  They show the readiness with which water and washing could be employed in symbolic action
	  They do not reveal the direct origins of John's or Jesus' practice

•   John the Baptizer  
	  John's title, the Baptizer, is recorded in the Synoptic Gospels and Josephus
	  He preached the nearness of the reign of God and the anointed one

•   The Reign of God and the Anointed One  
	  John's preaching emphasized the nearness of the reign of God
	  He proclaimed the coming of the anointed one, or Messiah

•   The Significance of Ritual Washing  
	  Ritual washing was a significant practice in first-century Judaism
	  It was used for purification, initiation, and repentance
	  It was an important part of the ritual world of first-century Jews

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   John's Unique Practice  
	  John's practice of washing those who responded was original and perceived as unique, attractive, and dangerous.
	  His practice was not necessarily startling in itself, but its significance and impact were notable.

•   John's Prominence  
	  John was prominent in all the canonical Gospels and was respected by the Jewish historian Josephus.
	  His influence was widespread during his lifetime and continued after his early death.

•   John's Baptism  
	  John's baptism was not about converting people to a new religion or making them his followers.
	  It was about God's demands and the nearness of judgment, and those baptized by John were expected to live a righteous life within the existing structures of Judaism.

•   Character of John's Baptism  
	  John's baptism was a ritual of conversion in the literal sense of personal reorientation.
	  It was not an entry into a different religious community or belief system.

•   Comparison to the Miqveh  
	  John's baptism was like the miqveh in that it involved a change of status.
	  However, John's baptism radicalized this transformation by emphasizing the importance of preparation for divine service in the face of God's imminent judgment.

•   The Role of Isaiah 40:3  
	  The citation of Isaiah 40:3 by all four Gospels suggests that John used this passage in his teaching.
	  This passage evoked the Israelite history of the exodus and was a powerful message in a time of occupation and oppression.

•   John's Baptism as Preparation  
	  John's baptism was part of the way and preparation for the coming of the Lord.
	  It was a transition and not merely a transaction.

•   John's Administration of Baptism  
	  John administered baptism himself, rather than having others perform it.
	  This recalls prophetic symbolic action, such as that of Jeremiah.

•   John's Influence on Jesus' Ministry  
	  The movement around John was part of the matrix within which Jesus' ministry was formed.
	  The Gospel of John presents Andrew as initially a follower of John, and John's teaching was prominent among the first Christians.

•   Legacy of John's Baptism  
	  The character of John's baptism, emphasizing ethical renewal and forgiveness, was retained in the subsequent history of baptism.
	  John's baptism was a significant event in the life of Jesus and the early Christian community.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   John the Baptist's Movement  
	  Took over some of the meanings of the baptism of John
	  Emphasized reorientation to justice and righteousness, forgiveness of sins, and symbolic purification
	  Followers continued to exist and propagate his message after Jesus' ministry

•   The Baptism of Jesus  
	  Jesus was baptized by John, placing himself in John's circle
	  The accounts of Jesus' baptism were important for early Christian belief and practice
	  Introduced clues to more specifically Christian understandings of baptism

•   Key Elements of Jesus' Baptism  
	  Divine adoption or proclamation
	  Giving of the Holy Spirit (in the form of a dove)
	  Forgiveness and repentance (not the only reasons for Christian baptism)

•   Jesus' Prediction of Baptism  
	  John predicted that Jesus would baptize others with the Spirit
	  This link with the Holy Spirit remained a key theme in early Christian baptism

•   Christian Baptism in the New Testament  
	  Presented as a continued ritual sign and proclamation of the reign of God
	  Emphasized the need and opportunity for change in the person and among the people

•   Changes in Baptism after Jesus' Ministry  
	  Emergence of Christian believers as a distinct group led to changes in baptism
	  Crucial changes in the meaning and practice of baptism

•   Early Christian Use of Baptism  
	  Not a complete break from John's baptism
	  Still emphasized the need for change and the closeness of the reign of God

•   Influence of John's Movement on Early Christianity  
	  Continued baptismal activity by John's followers may have influenced early Christian thought and practice
	  May have been an ongoing influence on the earliest Christians

•   The Gospel Accounts of Jesus' Baptism  
	  Mark and Matthew report Jesus' baptism by John
	  Other Gospels imply Jesus' baptism, de-emphasizing John's role

•   The Significance of Jesus' Baptism  
	  Revealed or acknowledged Jesus as Son of God
	  Introduced the theme of baptism as a means of divine adoption and proclamation

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Origins of Christian Baptism  
	  Emerged as a separate initiation ritual from Judaism
	  Initially a sign of participation in God's renewal of Israel and the world
	  Meant repentance, forgiveness, and preparation for divine service

•   Paul's Influence on Baptism  
	  Wrote about baptism from the 40s CE onward
	  Baptism was already a practice in the Christian community before Paul's writings
	  Paul's theology and practice played a significant role in shaping baptism

•   Christological Focus of Baptism  
	  Paul emphasized baptism as a means of entering into Christ
	  Baptism into Christ implied a radical change in identity and allegiance
	  Transcended Jewish identity and divisions of all kinds

•   Inclusivity of Baptism  
	  Applicable to men and women, regardless of social status or circumcision
	  Perfectly suited to understanding Christian allegiance as participation in Jesus

•   Paul's Teaching on Baptism  
	  Emphasized baptism as a means of dying and rising with Christ
	  Linked baptism with Jesus' death and resurrection
	  Baptism was about participating in Christ's attributes and promises

•   Cultural Significance of Baptism  
	  Influenced by Greco-Roman culture and mystery cults
	  Understood as a means of death and rebirth
	  Resonated with gentiles who had participated in mystery cults

•   Baptism as a Means of Initiation  
	  A means of entry into the Christian community
	  Transformed Jewish identity for its members
	  Became the sole initiatory ritual for the emerging Christian movement

•   Paul's Emphasis on Faith  
	  Baptism was a means of justification by faith
	  Faith had come, and the law was no longer necessary
	  Christians were free adult heirs of God's promises in Christ

•   Baptism and Social Status  
	  Transcended social status and divisions of all kinds
	  Made all participants equal in Christ
	  Emphasized the unity of the Christian community

•   The Significance of Baptism  
	  Depended on the understandings brought by participants
	  Influenced by cultural and religious contexts
	  A means of participating in Christ's life and attributes

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Baptism in the Early Church  
	  Baptism was a central ritual in the early Christian church
	  The idea of baptism as a participation in Jesus' death and resurrection was not the only understanding of the ritual

•   Paul's View of Baptism  
	  Paul saw baptism as a rite of adulthood and responsibility
	  He emphasized the idea of baptism as a transition to a new life in Christ

•   John's Gospel and Baptism  
	  John's Gospel presents baptism as a new birth related to the Spirit
	  It emphasizes the idea of a second and different kind of birth, rather than a death and resurrection in Christ

•   The Holy Spirit and Baptism  
	  The Gospels present Jesus as both baptized and baptizing with the Spirit
	  The Great Commission (Matt. 28:19) incorporates the proclamation of Jesus as Savior and the work of the Holy Spirit

•   The Relationship between Baptism and the Holy Spirit  
	  The Gospels imply a connection between Jesus and John's baptisms, but also a hierarchy
	  The Holy Spirit is a prominent actor in Acts, particularly in initiation and conversion

•   Initiation and Conversion in Acts  
	  The Spirit seems to blow where it wills, sacramentally
	  Christian baptism can precede, follow, or coincide with the giving of the Holy Spirit

•   Different Doctrines of Christian Initiation  
	  Traditional churches have sought the basis for laying on of hands and anointing at confirmation
	  Pentecostal groups have associated ecstatic experiences with baptism and conversion

•   The Precise Relationship between Baptism and the Holy Spirit  
	  The stories in Acts do not present a consistent understanding of the relationship between baptism and the Holy Spirit

•   The Significance of Baptism  
	  Baptism is a transition to a new life in Christ
	  It is a new birth related to the Spirit, rather than a death and resurrection in Christ

•   The Evolution of Christian Worship  
	  The early Christian church developed a complex and nuanced understanding of baptism and the Holy Spirit
	  This understanding has evolved over time, with different traditions and interpretations emerging.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Gift of the Holy Spirit  
	  The gift of the Holy Spirit was a distinguishing point between Christian initiation and the washings of John the Baptizer.
	  The gift of the Holy Spirit was a key aspect of Christian initiation, but not the only aspect.

•   Early Christian Practice  
	  Early Christian baptism was often performed in public settings, such as rivers or public baths.
	  Baptism could also be performed in private settings, such as homes or pools.

•   Places of Baptism  
	  Natural bodies of water, baths, and reservoirs were possible places for baptism.
	  Pools in homes or other private settings were also used for baptism.

•   Ritual and Words  
	  There is no clear indication of the actual ritual or words used in the first baptisms.
	  The formula of the Matthean Great Commission may have been recited with a threefold pouring or immersion.

•   Administration of Baptism  
	  Baptism was often performed by an authoritative figure or teacher.
	  Paul laments the emphasis on baptismal lineage created by his own ministry.

•   Who Was Baptized  
	  Entire households were sometimes baptized together.
	  This may have included dependents of a convert, such as children, relatives, and slaves.

•   Baptism of Children and Infants  
	  There is uncertainty about the baptism of children and infants in the first and second centuries.
	  Adults were the normal recipients of baptism for many years.

•   Baptismal Lineage  
	  Paul laments the emphasis on baptismal lineage created by his own ministry.
	  This suggests that baptismal lineage was an important aspect of early Christian practice.

•   Public vs. Private Baptism  
	  Early Christian baptism was often performed in public settings.
	  However, private baptism was also possible, especially in homes or private pools.

•   Symbolism of Baptism  
	  The symbol of baptism assumes complete bathing or immersion.
	  This does not necessarily mean that the first Christians were watching for every part of the body to go under.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Baptism on behalf of the dead  
	  Paul mentions baptism on behalf of the dead in 1 Corinthians 15:29
	  This practice suggests that personal religious experience was not always crucial
	  It implies that baptism was seen as having objective power and as God's own action

•   Vicarious initiation  
	  Baptism on behalf of the dead was a form of vicarious initiation
	  A living believer underwent baptism on behalf of a deceased person
	  This practice underlines the difference between ancient and modern views on the relationship between individual religious experience, community values, and ritual

•   Baptism as a ritual  
	  Baptism was not seen as a mere outward sign of a change in the mind or will
	  It was seen as having objective power and as God's own action
	  The participant and the minister were not the primary agents of baptism

•   The Didache  
	  The Didache is the earliest Christian document to give explicit instructions for baptism and Eucharist
	  It begins with an extended ethical instruction, known as "The Two Ways"
	  This instruction presents a stark contrast between good and evil ways of life and their consequences

•   Preparation for baptism  
	  Preparation for baptism involved a slower and more rigorous process
	  Candidates were expected to fast for one or two days before baptism
	  Fasting marked the candidates as undergoing a change of status

•   Ritual aspect of baptism  
	  The Didache urges candidates, the baptizer, and other believers to fast before baptism
	  Fasting was seen as a way to generate more effective prayer
	  It was also seen as a way to mark the candidates as undergoing a change of status

•   Immersion or saturation  
	  Immersion or saturation of the whole body in baptism remained normative
	  However, the Didache sets out a flowchart to deal with situations where water was scarce

•   Alternative baptism methods  
	  If living water was not available, baptism could be performed in other water
	  If cold water was not available, baptism could be performed in hot water
	  If neither was available, water could be poured on the head three times

•   Trinitarian formula  
	  The trinitarian formula, found in Matthew's Gospel, was to be recited during baptism
	  This formula is: "in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit"

•   Symbolism of baptism  
	  The core symbol of baptism was a threefold dipping (or pouring)
	  This symbol was seen as having objective power and as God's own action

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Baptism in the Didache  
	  Emphasizes movement through water as the central act
	  Recalls the logic of the miqveh (Jewish ritual bath)
	  Implies an immediate passage from baptism to participation in the Eucharist

•   Baptism in the Didache (continued)  
	  Restricts participation in the Eucharist to the baptized
	  Quotes Jesus as saying "Do not give what is holy to the dogs" (Mt 7:6)

•   Implicit meanings of baptism  
	  Created membership in the community and offered participation in its benefits
	  Required adherence to the community's ethical stances

•   Baptism in the Second and Third Centuries  
	  Emphasized sin, repentance, and renewal
	  Involved preparation and consequences beyond the actual washing

•   Justin Martyr's account of baptism  
	  Describes baptism as a straightforward washing with water
	  Involves trinitarian recitation or confession
	  Requires assent to certain teachings and commitment to Christian ethics

•   Preparation for baptism  
	  Involves ethical (and doctrinal) instruction and ascetic preparation
	  Includes prayer, fasting, and asking for forgiveness of previous sins

•   The Shepherd of Hermas  
	  Refers to would-be Christians who change their mind and fall back into immorality
	  Urges the renunciation of all sexual activity as necessary for authentic Christian faith

•   Marcion's view of baptism  
	  Demands that candidates for baptism be virgins, widowed, celibate, or divorced
	  Views Jesus as the son of a higher God, not the OT Creator

•   Baptism as a rite of passage  
	  Creates a new identity and membership in the community
	  Offers participation in the community's benefits and rituals

•   Baptism as a transformative experience  
	  Involves sin, repentance, and renewal
	  Requires a commitment to Christian ethics and values

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Baptism and Sin  
	  Baptism was seen as a new phase of life, free from sin
	  Accommodations were made for those who transgressed later, but only for major sins
	  The Shepherd of Hermas suggests a single opportunity for post-baptismal repentance

•   Justin's View of Baptism  
	  Baptism was a powerful and mysterious reality
	  It provided an answer to deep-seated needs in the human person
	  It was a choice and understanding that replaced ignorance and necessity

•   The Purpose of Baptism  
	  To become the children of choice and understanding, rather than ignorance and necessity
	  To undergo the forgiveness of sins previously committed
	  To be illuminated in understanding

•   Original Sin  
	  Justin's view of human reproduction as problematic anticipates the idea of original sin
	  It was not just a question of arbitrarily assigned culpability, but a diagnosis of a genetic disorder

•   Baptism as Illumination  
	  Baptism was referred to as illumination in Justin's community
	  It implies growth in knowledge and understanding
	  It suggests baptism as changing the believer's spiritual capacity or insight

•   Baptism as Spiritual Armature  
	  Ignatius of Antioch thought of baptism as a gain or gift
	  It was an abiding reality, a sort of spiritual armature
	  It was not just a one-time action to deal with sin, but a spiritual reality

•   Who Baptizes?  
	  Ignatius of Antioch insisted that baptisms should take place under episcopal authority
	  This did not mean bishops always performed baptisms, but that they were the proper authority

•   Tension in the Early Church  
	  A tension emerged between those who saw initiation as joining a close-knit community and others who focused on personal experience or enlightenment
	  This tension is reflected in the different views of baptism and church order

•   The Acts of Paul and Thecla  
	  This ancient religious romance provides a fictional but important example of the tension in the early church
	  It highlights the different views of baptism and church order

•   The Significance of Baptism  
	  Baptism was seen as a powerful and mysterious reality that provided an answer to deep-seated needs in the human person
	  It was a choice and understanding that replaced ignorance and necessity
	  It was a spiritual reality that changed the believer's capacity or insight.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Thecla's Story  
	  Thecla, a young woman, leaves her family and home to follow Paul
	  She baptizes herself and survives a martyr's death, enlightening and baptizing others
	  Tertullian was dismissive of this story, but it persisted in popularity for centuries

•   Women in Baptismal Ministry  
	  Women may have exercised ministries that included participation in baptism and anointing
	  They may have held office as presbyters and bishops, although this is less clear
	  Women deacons played an important role in rituals requiring practical assistance

•   Tertullian's Views on Baptism  
	  Tertullian argued that all Christians have the right to baptize in an emergency
	  Baptism is most properly exercised by the bishop, and otherwise by clergy
	  Martyrdom is considered a baptism of blood, effective in remitting sins

•   Preparation for Baptism  
	  Prolonged preparation with a strong ethical emphasis continued into the third century
	  The catechumenate was a defined structure for instruction and preparation
	  Catechumens were divided into two groups: those preparing for baptism and those moving more quickly to baptism

•   Catechumenate Requirements  
	  Catechumens could be refused admission due to unacceptable professions (e.g. astrologers, prostitutes, gladiators)
	  Artists and public officials who enforced measures against Christians were also excluded
	  Aspiring Christians in these professions would have to change their livelihood to seek baptism

•   Catechumenate Structure  
	  Catechumens could be enrolled with an eventual expectation of baptism or in earlier phases of preparation
	  Others, often those enrolled for some time, moved more quickly or directly to baptism

•   Eucharistic Meals and Catechumens  
	  Catechumens could not participate fully in eucharistic meals central to the Christian community
	  This restriction may have been due to concerns about the catechumen's spiritual readiness

•   Women's Participation in Baptism  
	  Women may have played a significant role in baptizing others, particularly in emergency situations
	  Thecla's story may reflect a historical reality of women as baptizers

•   Anointing and Baptism  
	  Women may have participated in anointing, a ritual that accompanied baptism
	  Anointing was an important aspect of Christian worship and spiritual preparation

•   Martyrdom as Baptism  
	  Martyrdom was considered a baptism of blood, effective in remitting sins
	  This view was held by Tertullian and may have influenced the development of Christian theology

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Catechumenate  
	  Involved negative and positive change for catechumens
	  Involves accounting for former sins and learning Christian faith and life
	  Duration varied, but 3 years was a common practice in some places

•   Preparation for Baptism  
	  Candidates were exhorted to pray, fast, and perform other rituals
	  Tertullian advised frequent prayers, fastings, and all-night vigils
	  Confession of former sins was also a part of the preparation

•   Rituals Before Baptism  
	  Exorcisms were performed by the bishop to remove evil forces
	  Candidates were laid on hands, breathed on, and had their ears and noses sealed
	  This was a concrete, ritualized removal of evil forces

•   The Baptismal Rite  
	  Candidates made a threefold renunciation of the devil and his angels
	  They then made a threefold profession of faith
	  The candidate was immersed three times in parallel with the threefold renunciation

•   The Significance of Baptism  
	  Baptism was seen as a removal of evil forces and a substitution of good ones
	  It was a concrete, ritualized act that marked a new beginning for the candidate

•   Venue and Accoutrements  
	  Some Christians showed a lack of concern for the venue or accoutrements for baptism
	  Tertullian argued that baptism could take place in any body of water
	  However, by his time, baptisms in Carthage may have been taking place in dedicated venues

•   Dedicated Places for Baptism  
	  The provision of dedicated places for baptism was likely due to the need for candidates to strip
	  This was a common expectation, but it also raised concerns about modesty and the roles of male clergy

•   Sanctity and Modesty  
	  The Apostolic Tradition reflects concern for sanctity and modesty in the baptismal rite
	  Baptism was always performed in a pool with flowing water
	  The preference for moving, natural water found in the Didache was also reflected in the Apostolic Tradition

•   The Role of Water  
	  Water was seen as effective regardless of quality and quantity
	  However, by Tertullian's time, baptisms in Carthage may have been taking place in venues specifically set aside for the purpose

•   Angel Intervention  
	  Tertullian is the first witness to prayer offered over the water, seeking angelic intervention
	  After this, it seems to have become common for the water to be blessed

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Importance of Space and Place in Baptism  
	  Baptism was initially associated with moving water, but later became tied to a specific location
	  This shift in emphasis reflects the growing importance of communal worship and the Eucharist

•   Communal Character of Baptism  
	  Baptism was often performed in conjunction with the Eucharist
	  Participants would move from the baptismal site to the Eucharist immediately after

•   Rituals Following Baptism  
	  Anointing and laying on of hands were part of the baptismal ritual
	  The exchange of a kiss and the Eucharist were also included

•   Sealing and Anointing  
	  Baptism was referred to as a "seal" in ancient texts
	  This image referred to the importance of literal seals in antiquity, which functioned as signs of contracts and property

•   Christian Identity as a Seal  
	  Christian identity was likened to a seal of the Holy Spirit in the NT
	  Circumcision was also termed a seal, signifying a covenant relationship

•   Physical Process of Baptism  
	  Baptism was a physical process, the ritual enactment of a contract
	  Participants entered into service of God as king, patron, and parent

•   Development of Rituals  
	  The language of seals encouraged the development of rituals like making the sign of the cross
	  These rituals were not implied by the earliest literary and liturgical use of sealing language

•   Signing Oneself with the Cross  
	  Tertullian reports a widespread devotional practice of signing one's forehead with the cross
	  This practice was not specific to baptism, but a persistent embodied prayer

•   Anointing and Laying on of Hands  
	  Anointing and laying on of hands were part of the baptismal ritual
	  These practices were likely influenced by Jewish and Greco-Roman traditions

•   Evolution of Baptismal Rituals  
	  Baptismal rituals evolved over time to include various practices and symbols
	  These developments reflect the growing importance of communal worship and the Eucharist.

•   Early Christian Practices  
	  Christians performed ordinary actions like lighting lamps and sitting at tables
	  These actions may have been connected to baptismal practices, but this is not explicitly stated

•   Tertullian's Time  
	  Tertullian may have used the signaculum (a cross or mark) on the forehead of baptized candidates
	  This practice may have been in use before Tertullian's time

•   Cyprian's Use of Signaculum  
	  Cyprian uses the signaculum in a clearly baptismal context
	  The signaculum is associated with the Holy Spirit and the Lord's seal

•   Oil in Baptism  
	  The use of oil for anointing the candidate is first reported in the late 2nd century
	  Oil becomes a focal point of sealing in baptismal rituals

•   NT Texts and Anointing  
	  NT texts associate Jesus with anointing and the Holy Spirit
	  These texts can be interpreted baptismally and applied to anointing in particular

•   Literal Anointing for Healing  
	  NT texts suggest literal anointing for healing, but not for baptism
	  Mark 6:13 and James 5:14 are examples of this

•   Irenaeus' Report  
	  Irenaeus reports on some Christians who anointed the newly baptized with balsam
	  Others smeared oil and water on the heads of their candidates instead of going to the bath

•   Oil in Baptismal Ritual  
	  Oil was used in baptismal rituals by Christians around the Mediterranean
	  Various procedures and understandings were attached to this practice

•   Ordinary Life and Anointing  
	  Oil was used on the body in ordinary life for therapeutic and cosmetic purposes
	  Anointing was an integral part of bathing in Mediterranean antiquity

•   Theologizing of Anointing  
	  Around 200, older practices were likely theologized and given symbolic weight
	  Some groups, like those reported by Irenaeus, regarded anointing as having profound meanings

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Tertullian's description of baptism  
	  Anointing takes place after immersion and before laying on of hands by the bishop (On Baptism 68)
	  Anointing is presented as a venerable custom, derived from the anointing of priests and Jesus' anointing with the Spirit

•   Ancient theological rationales  
	  Oil of baptism was likely not recent in Tertullian and Perpetua's Carthage
	  The use of oil in baptism was probably derived from the anointing of priests and Jesus' anointing with the Spirit

•   Syrian practices  
	  The apocryphal Acts of Thomas describes baptisms preceded by anointing
	  The use of oil seems to be at least as significant as the washing itself in these acts

•   Waterless anointing  
	  In one case in the Acts of Thomas, it is not clear that washing takes place at all
	  A waterless anointing for initiation could have been intended in this case

•   Didascalia Apostolorum  
	  Anointing takes place before the bath in this work
	  Oil is used both for the whole body and for the head, depending on circumstance

•   Apostolic Tradition  
	  Gives a prayer for blessing oil (chap. 5)
	  Indicates that oil of exorcism and oil of thanksgiving were both blessed before use in baptism (chap. 21)

•   Meanings attributed to anointings  
	  The first anointing is with the oil of exorcism, relating to the removal of what was old and bad
	  The second anointing is with the oil of thanksgiving, immediately after baptism
	  The third anointing is with the same oil, but the bishop offers a prayer for grace at this point

•   Oil of exorcism and oil of thanksgiving  
	  Candidates are anointed with the oil of exorcism prior to being washed
	  The oil of thanksgiving is used immediately after baptism

•   Bishop's role in anointing  
	  The bishop offers a prayer for grace during the third anointing
	  The bishop also lays on of hands during the third anointing

•   Complexity of anointing practices  
	  Different works and traditions have different approaches to how sacramental oil and water were mixed
	  The meanings attributed to these anointings are complex and varied.

	  Laying on of hands was linked to water baptism in the Acts of the Apostles
	  It was also associated with prayer for healing and commissioning for ministry

	  Hand laying appears in evidence for regular Christian baptismal ritual from around 200 CE
	  It may have been used earlier, but its meaning and significance became more explicit and specific over time

	  Tertullian connected hand laying to the Holy Spirit, not to washing or anointing

	  They may have had different meanings and associations, but were both part of the complex and multifaceted practice of early Christian worship
    The Complexity of Early Christian Ritual  
	  Early Christian ritual was complex and multifaceted, with different practices and traditions coexisting and influencing one another
	  This complexity reflects the dynamic and evolving nature of early Christian worship and practice.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Significance of Hand Laying in the Early Church  
	  Hand laying was a significant ritual in the early Christian church
	  It was associated with baptism, confirmation, and ordination
    The Didascalia's Description of Hand Laying  
	  The Didascalia describes a rite where the bishop lays hands on the baptized
	  The imposition of hands makes real the participation of the baptized in Jesus' baptism
	  The key idea is adoption, with the Holy Spirit prominent in the story of Jesus' baptism
    The Role of the Holy Spirit in Hand Laying  
	  The Holy Spirit is associated with the laying on of hands in the Didascalia
	  The Spirit is seen as a key part of the ritual, particularly in the context of baptism and reconciliation
    The Apostolic Tradition's View of Hand Laying  
	  The Apostolic Tradition sees the action of the Spirit as focused in the water of baptism
	  The hand laying is an enacted prayer for grace, invoking the Holy Spirit
    Ordination and Hand Laying  
	  The acknowledgment or commissioning of men and women for specific offices or orders was ritualized in the early church
	  Hand laying was associated with commissioning Christians for specific tasks, but was not as significant as baptism
    The First Detailed Account of Ordination  
	  The Apostolic Tradition provides the first detailed account of ordination as a specific liturgical action
	  Ordination is seen as a separate action from baptism, but is still closely tied to it
    The Process of Ordination  
	  The candidate for ordination was chosen by the people and named and accepted by them
	  The ordination took place on the Lord's day, along with the celebration of the Eucharist
    The Role of Bishops in Ordination  
	  The ordination of bishops, presbyters, and deacons is described in the Apostolic Tradition
	  The candidate is laid hands on by a single bishop, who prays aloud
    The Significance of Visiting Dignitaries in Ordination  
	  The involvement of visiting dignitaries in the ordination process is attested from the mid-third century
	  This involvement expresses connections across and within local churches
    The Evolution of Hand Laying in the Early Church  
	  Hand laying was initially associated with baptism and reconciliation
	  Over time, it became more closely tied to ordination and the commissioning of Christians for specific tasks

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Ordination Rites  
	  The Apostolic Tradition describes ordination rites for bishops, presbyters, and deacons
	  The rites involve prayers, hand-laying, and other rituals

•   Bishop's Ordination  
	  The bishop's ordination prayer invokes the examples of Abraham, Israelite kings, and apostles
	  The prayer emphasizes the bishop's high priestly role and invokes a high priestly Spirit

•   Presbyter's Ordination  
	  The presbyter's ordination prayer is less priestly in character than the bishop's
	  The primary biblical model is the elders chosen by Moses (Num. 11:16-17)

•   Deacon's Ordination  
	  The deacon's ordination instructions reflect the view that only the bishop lays hands on the deacon
	  The diaconate is not seen as priestly in the same way as the other two orders

•   Other Orders  
	  The Apostolic Tradition also describes the ordination of confessors, widows, readers, virgins, subdeacons, and ministers of healing
	  These orders are not seen as requiring hand-laying or the same level of priestly character as the bishop and presbyter

•   Women in Ministry  
	  The Didascalia Apostolorum refers to women deacons but does not describe their ritual appointment
	  The Apostolic Constitutions includes the oldest known rite with hand-laying for women deacons

•   Baptism and Eucharist  
	  In some ancient texts, baptism was closely related to Eucharistic celebration
	  The newly baptized were often fed a mixture of milk and honey as part of the baptismal celebration

•   Initiatory Processes  
	  As initiatory processes became more complex, participation in the Eucharist became a more clearly stated part of the baptismal ritual
	  The Eucharist took on specific characteristics for baptismal occasions

•   Symbolism in Ordination Rites  
	  The use of oil, cheese, and olives in ordination rites may symbolize the sacramental meal and the festive potluck

•   Complexity of Ordination Rites  
	  The ordination rites in the Apostolic Tradition show signs of expansion or editing
	  The rubrics reflect a complex and late editorial process, reflecting an interdependence of priestly character and presidency at the Eucharist.

•   Biblical symbols of plenty and luxury

	  These symbols were also used in the Bible to describe the Promised Land

	  Milk and honey are used as powerful and startling images in the Odes

•   Milk and honey as symbols of mystical union  
	  The Odes seem less interested in ritual than in mystical union with God
	  The imagery may have encouraged use of the Odes in ritual contexts

	  The imagery of milk and honey may have been used to symbolize spiritual nourishment

	  This imagery may have been used to reflect on eucharistic and baptismal practice

•   Eucharistic practice  
	  The Apostolic Tradition provides insight into how milk and honey were used in the eucharistic meal after baptism
	  The milk and honey were mixed together with bread and wine to fulfill the promise of a land flowing with milk and honey

	  The baptismal Eucharist included three cups: water, milk and honey, and wine
	  The number of cups may have been used to indicate a festive and celebratory atmosphere

•   Implicit meanings and actual practice  
	  The explicit meanings given for the milk and honey may be secondary to the actual practice
	  The use of milk and honey in baptismal rituals may have been influenced by a range of cultural and symbolic meanings.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Eucharist in the Early Church  
	  The Eucharist was a central ritual in the early Christian church
	  It involved the consumption of bread, wine, and possibly milk and honey

•   Ritual Elaborations  
	  The Eucharist was elaborated with additional rituals, such as baptism and anointing
	  These rituals were not necessarily part of the original Eucharist, but were added later

•   The Role of Presbyters and Deacons  
	  Presbyters and deacons played a key role in the Eucharist, holding cups and standing in order
	  They were responsible for distributing the elements to the congregation

•   The Order of the Eucharist  
	  The order of the Eucharist involved the consumption of three elements: water, milk, and wine
	  Each element was consumed three times, with the presbyter or deacon saying a blessing each time

•   The Significance of Milk and Honey  
	  The addition of milk and honey to the Eucharist was an invented ritual
	  These elements were chosen for their symbolic significance, rather than any existing practice

•   Christianity without Baptism?  
	  There is evidence to suggest that some early Christian communities did not practice baptism
	  This is seen in a non-canonical gospel fragment from Oxyrhynchus, which depicts Jesus criticizing the practice of baptism

•   The Argument between Jesus and Levi  
	  The gospel fragment depicts an argument between Jesus and a Pharisee named Levi
	  Levi justifies his practice of washing in a pool, but Jesus criticizes him for focusing on external purity rather than spiritual purity

•   The Critique of Ritual Purity  
	  The gospel fragment critiques the emphasis on ritual purity in Jewish practice
	  It suggests that true purity comes from spiritual enlightenment, rather than external rituals

•   The Nag Hammadi Documents  
	  The Nag Hammadi documents contain writings that critique the practice of baptism
	  These writings suggest that some early Christian communities saw baptism as unnecessary or even spiritually damaging

•   The Spiritual vs. Material Character of Ritual  
	  Some early Christian communities saw the material character of ritual as missing the point of salvation and enlightenment
	  They emphasized the spiritual significance of ritual, rather than its external trappings.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Tertullian's writings on baptism  
	  Tertullian wrote "On Baptism" in response to a Christian teacher who argued that baptism was not necessary.
	  This teacher was a woman who was influenced by gnostic ideas.

•   Gnostic views on baptism  
	  Some gnostic groups, like the Cainites, viewed baptism as inconsequential and rejected it.
	  Other gnostic groups spoke more favorably of baptism, viewing it as a means of ascent to mystical union.

•   The Gospel of Judas  
	  The Gospel of Judas rejects eucharistic celebration and may have had a similar negative attitude towards water baptism.
	  This gospel is mentioned in Irenaeus' "Against Heresies" and is similar to the recently discovered Gospel of Judas.

•   The Cainites  
	  The Cainites were a gnostic group that saw Cain as a divine figure.
	  They may have viewed the material world as something to be endured or escaped rather than as a good creation.

•   Baptism in ancient Christianity  
	  Baptism was an important ritual in ancient Christianity, symbolizing the candidate's new life in Christ.
	  The ritual involved immersion in water, followed by anointing with oil.

•   The Dura-Europos baptistery  
	  The earliest material evidence for a permanent place of baptism comes from the Christian building excavated at Dura-Europos in Syria.
	  The baptistery room was a small, decorated space with a pool and a canopy.

•   The baptistery room at Dura-Europos  
	  The room was connected to two other rooms and opened onto a courtyard.
	  It had an upper chamber that may have been used as a waiting area for candidates.

•   Post-baptismal anointing  
	  After baptism, candidates would move to a larger adjacent room for post-baptismal anointing.
	  This anointing involved the whole body and was performed by deacons of the same sex as the candidate.

•   The significance of the Dura-Europos baptistery  
	  The discovery of the Dura-Europos baptistery provides valuable insights into the practice of baptism in ancient Christianity.
	  It shows that baptism was an important ritual in early Christian communities.

•   The importance of baptism in ancient Christianity  
	  Baptism was a symbol of the candidate's new life in Christ and was seen as a means of spiritual transformation.
	  It was an important part of the early Christian worship and practice.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Baptism in the Early Christian Church  
	  Baptism was a significant ritual in the early Christian church
	  It was often performed in a baptistery, a separate room for the purpose

•   The Baptismal Process  
	  The candidate would stand under a canopy, with water poured over their head and body
	  The pool was not designed for immersion, but rather for complete wetting or washing

•   Decorations in the Baptistery  
	  The room was decorated with wall paintings depicting biblical scenes
	  These included images of the Good Shepherd, women approaching the font, and the wise and foolish virgins

•   The Symbolic Power of Baptism  
	  Baptism was seen as a unique and powerful ritual
	  It was believed to deal with sin, grant membership in the Christian community, and provide spiritual power and insight

•   Repetition of Baptism  
	  The early Christian church debated the issue of repeated baptism
	  Some groups, such as the Pseudo-Clementine Homilies, advocated for multiple washings or baptisms for purification and initiation

•   Followers of Elxai  
	  Elxai, an Eastern Christian teacher, advocated for the repetition of baptism or other repeated washings
	  His influence extended to Rome in the early third century

•   Persecution and Repeated Baptism  
	  Systematic persecution of Christians began in the mid-third century
	  Repeated baptism became an issue when external pressure created doubts about faith

•   Resistance and Suffering  
	  Christians resisted persecution and suffered greatly
	  Some Christians bowed to the threat of violence and offered sacrifices before imperial statues

•   Schism and Apostasy  
	  Schism and apostasy raised questions about the legitimacy of baptism and other sacraments
	  Rival claims to ecclesial and sacramental legitimacy emerged in the leadership

•   Theological Debates  
	  The early Christian church engaged in theological debates about baptism and other rituals
	  These debates reflected the church's struggle to define its practices and beliefs in the face of external pressure and internal divisions

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Baptism in the Early Church  
	  Baptism was a contentious issue in the early Christian church
	  Different levels of severity in treating lapsed Christians led to conflicts among clergy and splits within communities

•   Cyprian and Stephen's Disagreement  
	  African bishop Cyprian and Roman bishop Stephen disagreed on how to handle lapsed Christians
	  Cyprian insisted on rebaptizing heretics, while Stephen believed hand laying was sufficient

•   Baptism in the Fourth Century  
	  The fourth century brought new information and context for baptism
	  Emperors from Constantine onward tolerated and supported Christianity, leading to its growth

•   Regional Diversity of Baptismal Practice  
	  Regional diversity of baptismal practice continued, but patterns fell into geographical strands or traditions
	  Some of these traditions have continued to the present day

•   Adult Conversion and Baptism  
	  Adult conversion and baptism were still normative in the fourth century
	  Catechesis took place on a large scale, and Christians made deliberate choices about when to seek baptism

•   Baptism as a Serious Commitment  
	  Baptism was not an immediate response to Christian conviction, but a deliberate choice
	  It was not merely a symbolic confirmation of changes, but an objective encounter with divine grace

•   Constantine's Baptism  
	  Constantine, the first Christian emperor, delayed his baptism until his deathbed
	  This was due to the awkwardness of his role in state-sanctioned violence and the expectations of the Christian community

•   Augustine's Baptism  
	  Augustine of Hippo was baptized later in life, despite being born to a Christian mother and pagan father
	  He was enrolled as a catechumen as a child and underwent various ceremonies, but delayed baptism due to concerns about post-baptismal dangers

•   Catechumenate and Baptism  
	  The catechumenate was a process of examination and change before baptism
	  It included ceremonies such as being signed with the cross and receiving blessed salt

•   Baptism as a Rite of Passage  
	  Baptism was a significant rite of passage in the early Christian church
	  It marked a deliberate choice and commitment to the Christian community and its values.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Augustine's Conversion  
	  Augustine was initially drawn to the Manichaeans, but later converted to Christianity
	  He was influenced by Bishop Ambrose, but his conversion was not solely due to intellectual conviction
	  His famous conversion story is often misunderstood, as he had already been convinced of Christian faith intellectually

•   Augustine's Theological Contributions  
	  He developed the doctrine of original sin in response to Pelagius
	  This doctrine helped shape the view that baptism is not only for forgiveness of sins, but also addresses a fundamental flaw in human nature

•   Infant Baptism  
	  Augustine's understanding of original sin provided a theological underpinning for infant baptism
	  He used the practice of baptizing infants as evidence for the truth of his view
	  His thought that sin is inherent, rather than acquired, has its antecedents in earlier discussions

•   The Spread of Infant Baptism  
	  Infant baptism became more common in Western Christianity due to Augustine's influence
	  Baptism became a rite of entry into the Christian community, rather than a distinctive sacrament for adult faith and discipleship

•   Augustine's Framework for Dealing with Baptism and Disunity  
	  He developed a more pastoral and accommodating framework for dealing with questions of baptism and disunity
	  This framework was forged in controversy with the Donatists, who believed that the wider Christian movement was compromised and its baptism invalid

•   Augustine's View on Baptismal Validity  
	  He argued that the reality of baptism depends on God, rather than the merits of ministers
	  This view helped to resolve the controversy with the Donatists

•   Augustine's Influence on Western Christianity  
	  He is considered the theorist of infant baptism in Western Christianity
	  His understanding of original sin and baptism helped shape the practice of infant baptism in the Western church

•   Augustine's Legacy  
	  His theological contributions to baptism had significant practical and pastoral consequences
	  His framework for dealing with baptism and disunity helped to resolve controversies and shape the practice of baptism in the Western church

•   Augustine's Context  
	  He lived in a time when most people within the Roman Empire identified as Christians
	  Baptism became a rite of entry into the Christian community, rather than a distinctive sacrament for adult faith and discipleship

•   Augustine's Controversies  
	  He was involved in controversies with Pelagius and the Donatists
	  These controversies helped to shape his understanding of original sin and baptism

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Baptism and the Church  
	  Augustine believed communion with the wider church was necessary for baptism to be effective
	  He argued that the Donatists and other schismatics had valid baptism, but this was not universally accepted

•   Rebaptism  
	  Strictly speaking, rebaptism was impossible
	  However, the Western church avoided rebaptism if water and the Trinitarian name were used

•   Catechesis and Mystagogy  
	  The process of teaching and preparing candidates for baptism was crucial during this period
	  The Apostolic Tradition specified a three-year catechumenate, but this was not always followed

•   Catechumenate  
	  Those preparing for baptism were considered a special category among catechumens
	  They were known as "competentes" in Latin or "photizomenoi" in Greek, meaning "those being enlightened"

•   Cyril's Lectures  
	  Cyril, bishop of Jerusalem, gave a set of addresses to catechumens before baptism
	  These lectures focused on the creed and the meaning of baptism, but did not provide explicit information about baptism itself

•   Postbaptismal Catecheses  
	  Cyril also gave a set of postbaptismal catecheses to new Christians
	  These lectures provided more explicit information about baptism and its meaning

•   Variations in Teaching  
	  Different teachers, such as John Chrysostom and Theodore of Mopsuestia, offered varying levels of information about baptism before and after baptism
	  Some teachers, like Ambrose and Didymus, believed that the meaning of baptism should be kept secret from uninitiated catechumens

•   Mystagogy and Catechesis  
	  Teachers used a principle of mystagogy (Christian education) and catechesis (preparation) to teach about baptism
	  This approach emphasized the objective character of the sacraments and the importance of faith and trust in God

•   The Lord's Prayer  
	  The Lord's Prayer was considered a treasure that should be kept secret from uninitiated catechumens
	  This view reflects the importance of the Lord's Prayer in Christian daily worship

•   Diverse Approaches  
	  Different teachers and churches had varying approaches to teaching about baptism
	  However, all shared a strong sense of the objective character of the sacraments and the importance of faith and trust in God.

	  Used to explain the restoration of true humanity

	  Christian participation in baptism is a share in that mission

	  Metaphors of conversion as reclothing are prominent in the Pauline Letters
	  Associated with celebration and triumph

•   Baptism as a Form of Bathing  
	  Required the removal of clothing and subsequent reclothing
	  A momentous dip would have been no exception
	  A time to change clothes, like bathing normally was

•   The Significance of White Robes  
	  Symbolized the transformation and renewal of the baptized
	  Associated with celebration and triumph
	  A form of reclothing that signified conversion

	  Effects the restoration of true humanity
	  Used to explain the restoration of true humanity

•   The Distinctive Syrian Understanding of Baptism  
	  Reflected in the writings of Ephraem of Nisibis
	  Points to the links with Jesus' own baptism and the messianic or royal identity it bestows
	  A share in the mission of Jesus, the King.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Baptismal Garments in the Bible  
	  Changes of clothing were not always routine, but could express a shift in status (e.g. Gen. 35:2)
	  Tearing clothes and changing to sackcloth was a sign of mourning or supplication (e.g. Gen. 37:29, 34; 2 Sam. 3:31)
    Greco-Roman Society  
	  Public strategies of dressing the body to express need were known in Greco-Roman society
	  The toga virilis, a white robe, symbolized adult male citizenship in the Roman world
    Pauline Theology  
	  Paul theologizes the action of reclothing, linking it to baptism and maturity (Galatians)
	  Paul's theology of baptism is not explicitly linked to new garments in the NT documents
    Early Christian Practice  
	  The explicit evidence of baptismal garments comes as late as the 4th century
	  Before that, clothing imagery is used metaphorically, with baptism as the referent
    Interpretation of Baptismal Garments  
	  The provision of a new and/or white garment was likely an interpretation and elaboration of a prosaic part of the bathing practice underlying the baptismal rite
	  Any quest for a specific origin of the baptismal garment may be pointless
    4th-Century Evidence  
	  The account of Constantine's baptism includes the statement that he donned shining royal garments after the rite
	  Cyril of Jerusalem speaks of the newly baptized as shining in body, but does not discuss the clothing rite in his mystagogical text
    Chrysostom and Chromatius  
	  Chrysostom discusses the new garment of baptism, but means Christ or the imperial garb of a new nature given by God in Christ
	  Chromatius more clearly references the actual robes, moving between the literal and physical garments and the oil of anointing
    Confirmation  
	  African theologians such as Augustine and Optatus of Milevis had a strong view of postbaptismal anointing, calling both baptism and chrismation sacraments
	  Cyprian had used the term sacrament for laying on of hands centuries before

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Baptismal Rite  
	  The baptismal rite consisted of multiple actions, including naming, washing, anointing, and laying on of hands
	  These actions were not separate sacraments, but rather part of a complex rite
    Separation of Sacraments  
	  The identification of distinct ritual elements as separate sacraments foreshadowed a later Western tendency to separate them
	  This separation led to the development of new sacraments, including confirmation
    Confirmation  
	  The idea of a separate rite of confirmation emerged in mid-5th-century texts from southern France
	  Bishops were expected to perform confirmation, but there was uncertainty about the role of presbyters
    Councils and Confirmation  
	  Councils held at Riez (439), Orange (441), and Arles (ca. 450) referred to a confirmatio to be performed by the bishop
	  It is unclear whether this included a delayed form of postbaptismal anointing or was simply the laying on of hands
    Faustus' Homily  
	  A homily attributed to Faustus, bishop of Riez, described confirmation as a distinct rite centered on the laying on of hands
	  The homily emphasized the role of the Holy Spirit in confirmation
    Foot Washing  
	  The story of Jesus' foot washing in John's Gospel is distinct from the institution of the Eucharist in the Synoptic Gospels
	  Jesus' action of washing his disciples' feet is seen as an example for them to follow
    John's Gospel and Early Christian Practice  
	  John's Gospel is difficult to assess as a source of early Christian practice
	  Some commentators see its references to actions as reflecting or prescribing early Christian practice
    Theological Significance  
	  The theology of confirmation, as described by Faustus, emphasized the role of the Holy Spirit in increasing grace
	  This theology exploited the seams left in the Western tradition by the identification of distinct ritual elements as separate sacraments
    Development of Sacraments  
	  The separation of sacraments led to the development of new sacraments, including confirmation
	  This process was influenced by the growth of Christian numbers and the emergence of a more complex structure of local congregations
    Presbyterian Administration  
	  In some regions, presbyters administered the washing and other ritual elements, including oil (chrism) blessed by the bishop
	  There was uncertainty about the role of presbyters in the postbaptismal anointing.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Interpretation of John 13:1-20  
	  The passage can be interpreted as an allusion to baptism, a symbolic reference to mutual service and charity, or a literal command to wash feet.
	  The middle possibility is the most clear and not exclusive, emphasizing the type of service and leadership exercised by Jesus and urged on his followers.

•   Ancient Reception of the Gospel  
	  The Fourth Gospel was widely read in circles beyond normative or orthodox Christianity in the first century or so after its writing.
	  It was read by followers of Valentinus, a philosophically-minded teacher, and was reflected in some of the gnostic documents of the Nag Hammadi library.

•   Esoteric Reading and Rewriting  
	  The rich symbolism of the Gospel lent itself to esoteric reading and rewriting.
	  This is reflected in some of the gnostic documents of the Nag Hammadi library.

•   Textual Variations  
	  There are multiple readings and understandings of John 13:10, including a version where Jesus says "One who has bathed does not need to wash, but is entirely clean."
	  Other witnesses had an extra phrase qualifying the statement, suggesting that the bathed person is clean except for the feet.

•   Practice of Foot Washing  
	  Some ancient witnesses suggest that foot washing was literally to be practiced in the community, despite or in addition to what was accomplished in baptism.
	  This is supported by the additional phrase in some versions of John 13:10.

•   Foot Washing in the Ancient World  
	  Foot washing was a practical and hospitable act performed by people in the ancient Mediterranean world.
	  It was not a ritual peculiarity, but a common practice.

•   Early References to Christians Washing Feet  
	  There are early references to Christians washing feet, but not in the primarily symbolic way that tends to be read out of the Gospel text.
	  One example comes from Tertullian, who indicates that imprisoned Christians might have this service performed as part of the care offered by their visitors.

•   Tertullian's Reference to Foot Washing  
	  Tertullian mentions that imprisoned Christians might have their feet washed as part of the care offered by their visitors.
	  This is mentioned in the context of an argument against a Christian woman marrying an unbeliever.

•   Implications of Foot Washing  
	  The practice of foot washing might have been unremarkable in itself, but it took on a new significance in the context of the Gospel.
	  It emphasized the type of service and leadership exercised by Jesus and urged on his followers.

•   Uncertainty of Original Intention  
	  Determining an original text of John is probably as difficult as determining an original intention of the author.
	  There are multiple readings and understandings of the text as soon as it comes to light.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Care for Persecuted Christians  
	  Tertullian describes women visiting prisoners to offer care and comfort
	  This care includes physical needs such as food and water
	  The act is seen as a ritual and a way to show devotion to the persecuted

•   Ministry by Women  
	  Tertullian notes that women can perform ministry, but it is typically seen as domestic rather than communal
	  However, he acknowledges that there are exceptions to this rule

•   Foot Washing as a Ritual Act  
	  Foot washing is a distinctive act of ministry in the early Christian church
	  It is seen as a way to care for the poor and the sick
	  The act is performed in a ritual manner, with attention to detail and symbolism

•   Foot Washing in the Apostolic Constitutions  
	  Deacons are instructed to wash the feet of the sick and infirm
	  This is seen as a way to care for those who are unable to care for themselves

•   Foot Washing in Monastic Rules  
	  Monastic rules specify foot washing as an act of hospitality to visitors
	  This is seen as a way to affirm and interpret the custom in the community

•   Baptismal Significance of Foot Washing  
	  The story of Jesus washing his disciples' feet is widely interpreted as having baptismal significance
	  However, this interpretation is not necessarily tied to a specific liturgy or practice

•   Foot Washing and Baptism in the East  
	  Syrian texts and images suggest that the story of the washing was interpreted baptismally
	  However, there is no evidence that this was imitated in performance

•   Foot Washing and Baptism in the West  
	  Churches in Spain, Gaul, northern Italy, and Africa knew of a form of washing feet connected with the ritual of baptism
	  This practice is seen as a communal and ritual form of foot washing

•   Early Evidence of Foot Washing and Baptism  
	  A curious reference in Cyprian's letters may be the first hint of this practice
	  Fidus, an African bishop, wanted to delay infant baptisms until the eighth day, citing the need to wait until the infant's foot was clean

•   Purity and Marginality  
	  Fidus's reasons for delaying infant baptisms reflect a notion of purity that was common in the Roman colonial milieu
	  Newborns may have been seen as marginal beings until a certain amount of time had elapsed.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Baptismal Foot Washing in Roman Africa  
	  Infants and others were washed and kissed as part of the baptismal ritual
	  This practice was well-known in Roman Africa around 200 AD

•   Council of Elvira (300 AD)  
	  A collection of canon law forbids payment for baptisms
	  Canon 48 specifically prohibits clergy from washing the feet of the baptized
	  This implies that foot washing was a common practice in Spain around 300 AD

•   Baptismal Foot Washing in Milan (late 4th century)  
	  Ambrose comments on the practice of washing feet as part of baptism
	  This was not a universal practice, and the Roman church did not have this custom

•   Ambrose's Views on Foot Washing  
	  Ambrose sees foot washing as a sacrament and a sanctification
	  He distinguishes it from washing a guest's feet as a matter of humility

•   Ambrose's Explanation for Foot Washing  
	  He uses a mythological narrative to explain the practice: the serpent tripped Adam and spread venom on his feet
	  This requires additional cleansing and protection for the feet

•   Connection to Fidus' Objections  
	  Fidus in Africa objected to foot washing around 200 AD
	  Ambrose's explanation may be connected to Fidus' objections, but this is unclear

•   Ambrose's Explanation in On the Mysteries  
	  Peter had to wash his feet because he had inherited sin from Adam
	  The feet are washed to take away inherited sin

•   Foot Washing as a Sacrament  
	  Ambrose sees foot washing as a sacrament, as commanded by Jesus
	  He argues that the Romans should be embarrassed for not following this practice

•   Diversity in Christian Practices  
	  There was diversity in Christian practices regarding foot washing
	  Different churches had different customs and explanations for the practice

•   Foot Washing as a Matter of Humility and Sanctification  
	  Ambrose sees foot washing as a matter of both humility and sanctification
	  He argues that it is a sacrament and a sanctification, not just a matter of humility.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Foot washing as a remedy for inherited sin  
	  Ambrose saw foot washing as a remedy for inherited sin, which would later become a central aspect of baptism
	  Augustine, influenced by Ambrose, would later attribute the effectiveness of foot washing against inherited sin to baptism itself

•   Augustine's views on foot washing  
	  Augustine referred to different opinions on whether foot washing should take place at all
	  He advocated for a delay between the rite and the baptismal bath proper

•   Timing of foot washing  
	  Augustine suggested performing foot washing on the third or eighth day of Easter week
	  This was to distinguish it from the sacrament of baptism and to make it a more sacred time

•   Roman church's views on foot washing  
	  Some in the Roman church dismissed foot washing as a mere custom
	  Augustine's own church at Hippo, however, used one of these times to wash the feet of its neophytes

•   Foot washing as a model of Christ's humility  
	  The church at Hippo presented the ritual as a model of Christ's humility
	  It was not seen as a supplementary baptism or part of the baptismal rite proper

•   History of foot washing  
	  There is no evidence that foot washing was a continuous practice from Jesus to Ambrose and Augustine
	  The Johannine community may have performed a similar ritual, but the evidence is too weak to support this idea

•   Foot washing in the ancient Mediterranean  
	  People in the ancient Mediterranean did wash feet and have their feet washed
	  What is distinctive in Christian cases is the identities of the washers and the washed, not the ritual itself

•   Power dynamics in foot washing  
	  In cases of postbaptismal washing, clergy physically abased themselves to those they led
	  This opened a wider and more ambiguous set of power relations, particularly in the case of martyrs

•   Liturgical significance of foot washing  
	  The earliest cases of foot washing after the Gospel narrative were not liturgical in the sense of being symbolic communal acts
	  They were not merely prosaic, but rather a genuine attempt to embody the example of humility provided by the Gospel

•   Foot washing as a practical service  
	  Foot washing was a practical service in response to the needs of those at risk
	  It was not primarily a symbolic act, but rather an attempt to embody the example of humility provided by the Gospel

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Foot Washing in Early Christianity  
	  A powerful act of service that mimicked Christ's actions
	  May have had a different history than baptismal foot washing
	  Not a continued ritual in Christian communities

•   Baptismal Practice in Early Christianity  
	  Shifted from spontaneous semipublic bathings to ceremonial splashing
	  Infant baptism became more common, but not universal
	  Adults were still being baptized in the 4th century

•   Meanings of Baptism  
	  John's call for repentance was radicalized by Augustine
	  Baptism was seen as a means of solidarity with Christ
	  Different images and meanings of baptism coexisted

•   Baptismal Ritual and Meaning  
	  Elaboration of baptismal ritual was an enacted quest for meaning
	  Each aspect of the ritual became a text for reflection and exegesis
	  Christians had to explain why they baptized, beyond mere obedience

•   Prayer in Early Christianity  
	  Christians gathered for meetings and prayed at fixed times
	  Used set formulas and bodily postures for prayer
	  Prayer was a distinctive ritual performance, not just an inner activity

•   Continuity and Change in Baptismal Practice  
	  Both continuity and change are evident in early Christian baptismal practice
	  Shifts in water and preparation occurred early, but some changes were gradual

•   Baptismal Imagery and Symbolism  
	  Baptism was seen as a means of divine adoption and vocation
	  Images of Christ's mission and salvation were prominent
	  Different images and meanings coexisted and were not mutually exclusive

•   The Role of Oil and Other Accoutrements  
	  Oil, hands, clothes, and other elements became prominent in baptismal ritual
	  Each element became a text for reflection and exegesis
	  The use of these elements was not just ceremonial, but meaningful

•   The Development of Baptismal Ritual  
	  The elaboration of baptismal ritual was a gradual process
	  Different elements were added and emphasized over time
	  The ritual became more complex and meaningful

•   The Significance of Baptism in Early Christianity  
	  Baptism was a central practice in early Christian communities
	  It was seen as a means of salvation, solidarity with Christ, and divine adoption
	  The meanings and imagery of baptism evolved over time, but remained significant.
