Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. Jewish Prayer Patterns  
	  Morning and evening prayers were well-attested in Jewish tradition
	  The Shema (Hear, O Israel) was a central formula in these prayers
	  The Shema was prescribed in Deuteronomy 6:7 and was a common practice in the first century

  2. Times of Prayer  
	  Morning and evening were not the only recognized times for prayer
	  The ninth hour (midafternoon) was also a time for prayer, corresponding to the evening sacrifice
	  This practice continued even after the destruction of the temple

  3. Cultic Offerings and Prayer  
	  The sentiment linking sacrifice and prayer was familiar in Jewish tradition (Psalm 141:2)
	  The real or supposed times of cultic offerings were seen as appropriate for prayer

  4. Patterns of Prayer  
	  Private or domestic morning and evening prayers were common
	  Public prayers related to the twice-daily offerings were also practiced in Jerusalem
	  A threefold structure of morning, daytime, and evening prayer was attested in the book of Daniel and 2 Enoch

  5. The Shema and the Ten Commandments  
	  The Shema was the center of Jewish prayer, rather than its whole
	  Some Jews recited the Ten Commandments with the Shema

  6. The Tefillah (Prayer)  
	  The Tefillah was an expanded Shema with attached petitions or blessings (berakot)
	  It was emerging during the first century, but was not a universal or fixed formula

  7. Blessing Prayers (Berakah)  
	  Blessing prayers of berakah type were prominent in rabbinic texts and older biblical texts
	  They tended to have the form "Blessed be the Lord, followed by naming some basis for the blessing"

  8. Thanksgiving Prayers (Hodayot)  
	  Hodayot were direct address to God, expressing thanksgiving, praise, or acknowledgment
	  They were found in biblical and early Jewish contexts, including the Dead Sea Scrolls

  9. Early Christian Prayer  
	  The prominence of thanksgiving in early Christian prayer confirms that similar themes and forms were important to the original Christian community

  10. Diversity of Jewish Prayer  
	  The early evidence from the rabbis indicates how this form developed in their distinct strand of Judaism
	  However, this may not reflect the typical experience of all praying Jews at the time of Jesus or Paul.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Prayer in Ancient Judaism  
	  Prayer was a common practice in ancient Judaism
	  Prayers were often performed standing with hands outstretched (1 Tim 2:8)
	  Kneeling was also known, but specific to desperate situations (Acts 9:40)

•   Jesus' Prayer  
	  Jesus' prayer was a model for his followers
	  The Lord's Prayer (Matt 6:9-15, Luke 11:2-4) was a key part of Jesus' teaching
	  The Lord's Prayer was not derived from Jewish prayers, but reflects a shared milieu

•   Similarities with Jewish Prayers  
	  The Lord's Prayer has parallels with Jewish prayers, such as the Shemoneh Esreh and Kaddish
	  Both emphasize praise of God and acknowledgment of God's holiness

•   Communal and Private Prayer  
	  Jesus' teaching reflects concern for both communal and private prayer
	  The Lord's Prayer was used in both public and private settings

•   Versions of the Lord's Prayer  
	  There are multiple versions of the Lord's Prayer in the Gospels and other early Christian texts
	  The differences and similarities between these versions are noteworthy

•   The Didache and the Lord's Prayer  
	  The Didache, an early Christian text, contains a version of the Lord's Prayer similar to Matthew's Gospel
	  This suggests that the Lord's Prayer was widely used and adapted in early Christian communities

•   Diversity of Prayer in Early Christianity  
	  The diversity of prayer in early Christianity is evident in the different versions of the Lord's Prayer
	  This diversity provides evidence of the significance and use of the Lord's Prayer in early Christian communities

•   The Lord's Prayer as a Model  
	  The Lord's Prayer was not primarily seen as a model or primer for prayer in the earliest Christian communities
	  Instead, it was seen as a set prayer to be recited

•   The Significance of the Lord's Prayer  
	  The Lord's Prayer was a key part of Jesus' teaching and a model for early Christian prayer
	  Its significance and use in early Christian communities are evident in the different versions and adaptations of the prayer

•   The Importance of Prayer in Early Christianity  
	  Prayer was a central part of early Christian practice and teaching
	  The Lord's Prayer was a key part of this practice, reflecting Jesus' teaching and the shared milieu of early Christianity.

•   Early Christian Prayer Practices

	  The content and forms of prayer evolved over time

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Tertullian's views on prayer  
	  He emphasizes the importance of the Lord's Prayer as a core text
	  He also uses the Psalms as a resource for prayer
    Established times for prayer  
	  Tertullian suggests that there is no strict scriptural warrant for specific times of prayer
	  However, he notes that the third, sixth, and ninth hours of the day are useful and have been established through secular observances
    Apostolic credentials for prayer times  
	  Tertullian tries to establish that the third, sixth, and ninth hours have good apostolic credentials
	  He cites stories in Acts to support this claim
    The 3 2 pattern of prayer  
	  Tertullian suggests a pattern of three sanctioned and shared hours of prayer (third, sixth, and ninth hours)
	  This pattern is complemented by a more personal duty of prayer on rising and retiring
    The trinitarian pattern of prayer  
	  The three sanctioned hours of prayer are seen as a trinitarian pattern, reflecting the three persons of the Godhead
    Tertullian's later views on prayer  
	  In his later work On Fasting, Tertullian again emphasizes the importance of the threefold pattern of prayer
	  He cites biblical and secular reasons to support this pattern
    The significance of the ninth hour  
	  Tertullian notes that the ninth hour has a basis even more profound than the apostolic models he had cited earlier
	  This is because it was the hour of Jesus' death
    Tertullian's views on prayer in relation to the New Prophecy  
	  Tertullian's views on prayer were not idiosyncratic, but rather reflected the movement of the New Prophecy
	  His reflections on Jesus' passion and the hours of prayer would be elaborated upon by later Christian writers
    The influence of Tertullian's views on prayer  
	  Tertullian's views on prayer would influence later Christian writers and the development of Christian liturgy
	  His emphasis on the threefold pattern of prayer and the significance of the ninth hour would be reflected in later Christian practice
    The significance of Tertullian's views on prayer in the context of early Christianity  
	  Tertullian's views on prayer reflect the diversity and complexity of early Christian practice
	  His emphasis on the importance of prayer and the significance of specific times of prayer would shape the development of Christian liturgy and practice.

•   Tertullian's View of Prayer  
	  Sees form and content of prayer as closely related

	  A new form of prayer offered by the new covenant

	  Emphasizes the will and reign of God as the foundation of Christian prayer

•   The Praying Body  
	  Carthaginian Christians prayed with the whole body
	  Used standing and kneeling, with strong connotations of praise and penitence

•   Standing and Kneeling  
	  Standing was a marker of faith and a solemnity of exaltation
	  Kneeling was not appropriate on Sundays or during the 50-day period between Easter and Pentecost

•   Kneeling and Prostration  
	  Kneeling was recommended during communal prayers at times of fasting
	  Prostration was recommended at private prayer and during times of communal exaltation

•   The Orans Posture  
	  The erect stance with extended arms and eyes raised was a typical posture for prayer
	  Was shared with other communities and traditions, but interpreted in specifically Christian terms

•   The Sign of the Cross  
	  Was an additional act of physical prayer
	  Involved making the sign of the cross at every step and stage of daily life

•   The Sign of the Cross as a Statement of Allegiance  
	  Was an unequivocal statement of Christian allegiance
	  Was engraved into the forehead by constant use, marking the Christian's body as a canvas or chattel

•   The Sign of the Cross as a Symbol of the Cross  
	  Was connected to the cross as a symbol of Christian faith
	  Was defended by Minucius Felix as a Christian symbol

•   The Sign of the Cross as a Preparation for Punishment  
	  Was seen by Tertullian as a preparation for every kind of punishment
	  Was a reminder of the Christian's readiness to suffer for their faith
