Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Early Evidence of Christmas  
	  The earliest real evidence for Christmas comes from the Alexandrian writer Clement, who wrote around 200 AD.
	  Clement reported calculations of the chronology of Jesus' birth, including the year and day of his birth.
    Clement's Calculations  
	  Clement calculated that Jesus was born in the 28th year of Augustus, on the 25th of the month Pachon.
	  He also calculated that Jesus died in the 194th year after his birth, on a specific date.
    Rival Christian Group's Calculations  
	  A rival Christian group, led by Basilides, calculated the date of Jesus' baptism and death.
	  They said Jesus was baptized in the 15th year of Tiberius Caesar, on the 15th of the month Tubi (January 6).
    Coincidence of Birth and Death  
	  Some of the calculations suggest a possible coincidence between Jesus' birth and death.
	  This coincidence may have been celebrated as a festival.
    Celebration of Jesus' Baptism  
	  The celebration of Jesus' baptism on January 6 may have been a significant event.
	  This celebration may have been associated with the Feast of the Epiphany.
    Basilides and His Followers  
	  Basilides was a speculative teacher from Syria, and his followers had different ideas and practices.
	  Their celebration of Jesus' baptism may have been more common in Eastern Christian liturgical traditions.
    Relation to Epiphany  
	  The celebration of Jesus' baptism on January 6 may be related to the Feast of the Epiphany.
	  This feast may have been associated with the story of Jesus' baptism and his revelation as the Son of God.
    Origins of Epiphany  
	  The origins of Epiphany are not well understood.
	  Some scholars have suggested that it may have originated from a sun feast that was celebrated in January.
    Egyptian Months  
	  Egyptian months were initially lunar, but by Clement's time they had become fixed within the year.
	  This may have affected the calculations of Jesus' birth and death.
    Significance of January 6  
	  January 6 may have been a significant date in early Christian worship.
	  It may have been associated with the celebration of Jesus' baptism and the Feast of the Epiphany.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Epiphany celebrations  
	  Not a reenactment of a specific event, but a celebration of Jesus' passion, death, and resurrection
	  Focused on Jesus' appearance and sonship, not his birth

•   Feast of Lights in Cappadocia  
	  A concept similar to Epiphany, but connected to various stories about Jesus' appearance and identity
	  Associated with baptism, particularly the story of Jesus' baptism in John 2:6

•   Introduction of the Nativity feast  
	  Introduced in the East in the later 4th century
	  Did not replace earlier Epiphany celebrations, but added a new layer of meaning

•   Syrian tradition and baptism  
	  Retained and developed connections between birth and baptism in later liturgical texts
	  Ephraem of Nisibis wrote hymns that intertwined birth and baptism in the 4th century

•   Mary's hymn on birth and baptism  
	  In Ephraem's Hymns on the Nativity, Mary speaks about birth and baptism as intertwined
	  Quotes: "What will I call you, you so different from us. Will I call you Son, Will I call you Brother, will I call you Spouse, Will I call you Lord, you who have birthed your mother With a new birth, in the midst of the waters?"

•   Early references to December 25  
	  The Philocalian Chronograph of 354 is the earliest undisputed reference to December 25 as Jesus' birthday
	  Uses December 25 as its calendrical starting point for records as far back as 336

•   The Chronograph's significance  
	  A list of Roman martyrs, which is surprising to see a list of birthdays headed by Jesus'
	  Raises as many questions as it answers about the origins of Christmas

•   Earlier evidence of interest in December 25  
	  The Commentary on Daniel, attributed to Hippolytus, mentions December 25 as Jesus' birthdate
	  Written more than a century before the Chronograph, but its authenticity is unclear

•   Speculation about Jesus' birth and baptism  
	  The Commentary on Daniel and other early texts speculate about Jesus' birth and baptism
	  Not evidence of celebration, but rather speculation and discussion

•   The development of Christmas  
	  The Nativity feast of December 25 took longer to become a widely observed feast
	  The modern concept of Christmas as we know it today developed over time.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Early Christian Calculations of Jesus' Birth and Death  
	  Hippolytus of Rome (c. 170-235 AD) calculated Jesus' birth and death to be on March 25
	  This date was also linked to the vernal equinox and the creation of the world
    African Texts and the Dating of Pascha  
	  A text from around 243 AD, "On the Dating of Pascha", suggests a date of March 28 for Jesus' birth and Passover
	  This text also links the creation of the world to March 25 and Passover
    Rabbinic Texts and the Significance of Passover  
	  Later rabbinic texts state that key events of redemption across history happened at Passover
	  These texts also link the creation of the world to Passover
    Tertullian's Calculation of Jesus' Death  
	  Tertullian (c. 200 AD) calculated Jesus' death to be on March 25, the eighth day before the calends of April
	  This date is also linked to the first day of unleavened bread and the slaughter of the lamb
    Shared Traditions between Christians and Jews  
	  Christians and Jews shared a notion of cosmic and historic patterns
	  This shared tradition is evident in the calculations of Jesus' birth and death
    The Quest for Wider Connections  
	  Early Christian authors sought to align the story of Jesus with the structure of the universe
	  This quest for connections is evident in the calculations of Jesus' birth and death
    The Significance of March 25  
	  March 25 was a significant date in the ancient world, linked to the vernal equinox and the creation of the world
	  This date was also linked to the birth and death of Jesus
    The Importance of Cultural Borrowing  
	  Modern arguments about the relative importance of historic calculation and cultural borrowing may need to take into account the shared traditions between Christians and Jews
    The Development of Christian Festivals  
	  Early Christian speculation about Jesus' birth and death was not driven by connections with pagan solar festivals
	  The development of Christian festivals, such as Christmas, was a later development
    The Legacy of Early Christian Calculations  
	  The calculations of Jesus' birth and death by early Christian authors have had a lasting impact on Christian tradition and practice

•   The origins of Christmas  
	  The exact date of Christmas is unclear
	  The idea of a major sun feast on December 25 before the Christian incarnation feast was established is likely a fantasy
	  The Roman cult of Sol had festivals on different days

	  The cult of Sol was a minor cult in ancient Rome
	  Its festivals were not on December 25
	  Interest in the sun as a divinity grew through the same period as Christianity

•   The cult of Mithras and the Syrian unconquered sun  
	  The cult of Mithras and the Syrian unconquered sun were influential in the Roman Empire
	  They contributed to the growing interest in the sun as a divinity
	  There is no evidence for a major festival that the Christians could have borrowed or baptized

•   Julian the Apostate's claim  
	  Julian the Apostate claimed that the December 25 festival was an ancient Roman observance
	  He made this claim in 362, long after the evidence of the Philocalian calendar
	  This claim may have been an attempt to gain leverage from an established Christmas feast

•   Christmas in Africa  
	  The church in Africa may have celebrated Christmas before the early fourth-century sources of the Philocalian Chronograph
	  The Donatists, a dissident group, celebrated December 25 but not January 6
	  This suggests that December 25 had already been observed before the violence and split

•   Sun imagery in early Christian authors  
	  Early Christian authors used sun imagery and sun-related ideas about time
	  The eventual celebration of Jesus' birth on December 25 was a conjunction of historical and cosmic interests

•   Pope Leo the Great's concerns  
	  Pope Leo the Great feared that the Christian majority was paying too much attention to the sun
	  He condemned the practice of bowing towards the rising sun before entering a church
	  He attributed this practice to ignorance and paganism

•   The fear of sun worship  
	  Tertullian feared that the Christian minority could be mistaken for sun worshipers
	  Pope Leo the Great feared that the Christian majority was still paying too much attention to the sun
	  This fear highlights the tension between Christianity and paganism

•   The development of Christmas  
	  Christmas was not a fixed date until the fourth century
	  The celebration of Jesus' birth on December 25 was a gradual development
	  It was influenced by the growing interest in the sun as a divinity

•   The complexity of Christmas origins  
	  The origins of Christmas are complex and multifaceted
	  The exact date and nature of the festival are unclear
	  The development of Christmas was influenced by a range of cultural and historical factors.

•   Establishment of Christmas  
	  Christmas was established in the 4th century
	  It took time to spread, particularly to the eastern Mediterranean
	  January 6 (Epiphany) was already celebrated in the East

•   Historicization of Christmas and Epiphany  
	  The addition of Christmas encouraged a historicization process
	  This process was different in East and West
	  Theologians debated the meaning of baptism and Jesus' divinity

•   Theological Debates  
	  In the East, a sole emphasis on Jesus' baptism was debated
	  This could be seen as implying an adoption of Jesus
	  The Nicene party's emphasis on Jesus' divinity was a key factor

•   John Chrysostom's Views  
	  He commended the observance of Christmas to his listeners in Antioch in 386
	  He saw Christmas as a new and important feast day

•   Establishment of Christmas in the East  
	  Epiphany often remained a commemoration of Jesus' baptism
	  The Western nativity feast was established in the East after the Council of Constantinople in 381

•   Council of Constantinople  
	  The Nicene party's triumph at the Council of Constantinople in 381
	  The creed was adopted, and the story of Jesus' miraculous conception and birth was emphasized

•   Arian Views  
	  Arians acknowledged Jesus as a preexistent heavenly being
	  They did not have difficulty with the feasts, but the Nicene party's celebration was an opportunity to settle a doctrinal controversy

•   Advent  
	  Advent was traditionally regarded as the start of the liturgical year in Western Christianity
	  It was the last of the major feasts to be established

•   Liturgical Developments  
	  The establishment of Christmas and Epiphany led to liturgical developments
	  Theologians and liturgical practitioners debated the meaning of these feasts
	  The historicization process was a key factor in shaping the liturgy.

•   Christmas and the Christian Year  
	  Christmas was considered the beginning of the Christian year in the 4th century
	  The Philocalian Chronograph is a source that supports this

•   Lent and its Origins  
	  Lent originated from the coupling of Easter with pre-baptismal fasting
	  It was not just an extension of pre-Easter fast days, but a new tradition

•   Advent in the West  
	  The Western Advent had a previous history before taking its familiar four-week form under Gregory the Great
	  It was influenced by pre-Epiphany fasts and baptismal preparation

•   Pre-Epiphany Fasts  
	  A Spanish council in 380 prescribed a 21-day fast from December 17 to January 6
	  This fast was part of a response to more spectacular forms of asceticism

•   Character of the Desired Practice  
	  The period of fasting was defined relative to January 6, not December 25
	  Observance of December 25 may have already begun, but the focus was on January 6

•   Advent in Spain  
	  Advent in Spain was not just a mark on the liturgical calendar, but a monument to the struggle between asceticism and communal observance
	  It was a time for ascetic performance, with some undertaking urban or rural retreats

•   Pope Gregory and the Roman Church  
	  Pope Gregory reduced the six-week Advent to the familiar four weeks
	  He accentuated eschatological themes, loosening ties to baptismal preparation

•   Western Observance  
	  Western observance eventually focused on the four last things (death, judgment, heaven, and hell)
	  This was a departure from the earlier emphasis on baptismal preparation

•   Eastern Observance  
	  In the East, the period before Christmas was and is marked by a different emphasis
	  The exact nature of Eastern observance is not specified in the text

•   Connection to Baptism  
	  Periods of 40 days preparation before Christmas appear in other Western centers in the 4th century
	  This reflects a connection with baptism, but the exact nature of this connection is not specified

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Early Christian Worship and Time  
	  The early Christian church's use of time was not as simple as a focus on the end of time (eschaton)
	  Instead, Christians developed a complex system of feasts, fasts, and saints' days

•   NT Documents and Time  
	  The New Testament documents do not provide much information about times and seasons
	  Some NT passages are critical of the observance of times and festivals, particularly those that reflect Jewish identity

•   The Development of Christian Worship  
	  The formation of characteristic practices around Sundays, Easter, Christmas, and fasting days and seasons involved distinct stories and problems
	  This development was not a "fall from primitive expectation" but rather the gradual formation of a Christian culture

•   The Importance of Time in Christianity  
	  For early Christians, time was inescapable and required structures, markers, and indicators of meaning
	  If the Lord of time was their Lord, then time itself must reflect that sovereignty

•   Debates about Christian Time  
	  Debates about the relative importance of original Christian calculations and constructions versus borrowings from Jewish and pagan tradition have not been illuminating
	  Instead, the focus should be on relating history and nature rather than subjecting one to the service of the other

•   The Nativity and Time  
	  Hints of interest in the sun and its movement from an early period suggest a desire to make sense of a divinely ordered universe
	  This interest cannot be reduced to pagan influence but rather reflects a desire to understand the world and its relationship to God

•   The Shift from Eschatology to Kairoi  
	  The increasing attention to various points in time (kairoi) is not a shift away from the eschaton but rather an affirmation of Christian existence and practice having a history
	  This shift reflects the belief that the world itself serves and reveals the God who made it

•   Augustine's Perspective on Time  
	  Augustine expresses the idea that Jesus is the meaning of time and times alike
	  As born of his Father, Jesus orders all days; as born of his mother, he sanctifies this one (Sermon 194.1)

•   The Making of Christian Worship  
	  The survey of early Christian worship ends without addressing significant aspects of later liturgy
	  Weddings, for example, did not become a typical part of Christian worship until later

•   The Significance of Time in Christianity  
	  The use of time reveals that Christians were a new cultural formation as much as a religious one
	  The Christian understanding of time reflects a desire to make sense of a divinely ordered universe and to relate history and nature.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Defining Worship  
	  Worship is not just about services or rituals, but about service and allegiance
	  Ancient Christians did not focus on gestures or signals of belief, but on living out their faith

•   Ancient Christian Worship  
	  Not just liturgical, but a whole of communal identity and service
	  Embodied theory and practice of worship through complex means

•   Distinctive Christian Rituals  
	  Baptism and Eucharist are associated with commands of Jesus
	  Created a new sense of worship, rather than inhabiting an existing one

•   Tension with Other Cultures  
	  Christians' lack of sacrificial practice and rejection of others led to tensions
	  Apologists and sanctions placed on Christians in the 4th century

•   Other Forms of Action  
	  Acts of prayer and praise, reading and teaching, had parallels in Jewish and Roman communities
	  Shaped by the allegiance of the emerging church to the crucified and risen one

•   Christian Liturgy  
	  Not a single or generic whole, but a sum of specific and concrete performances
	  Constitutive of faith, allegiance, and identity

•   Characteristics of Christian Worship  
	  Not just about rituals or ceremonies, but about obedience and service
	  Embodied in the actions and discourses of the community

•   Historical Context  
	  Many modern Christian traditions are later medieval or modern additions
	  Early Christian worship was not focused on organs, spires, or special clothes

•   Cemeteries and Burials  
	  Buried Christians attracted attention in the early centuries
	  Cemeteries had prominence, but not in the same way as modern Christian traditions

•   Understanding Worship  
	  Worship is not just about services, but about service and allegiance
	  Ancient Christians' understanding of worship was distinct from modern Christian traditions
